LIBRARY

Fool's Gold By THERESE BENSON 

When Hope Willing found her family's fortune gone and even her old home whisked into the ruthless hands of autioneers, a very drab future in a nondescript New York office loomed before her. Then, almost before she realized what was taking place, she had met Martin

Newbold, become his private secretary, and was on her way to France with him and his tigerish wife, Mirabel. There, in a whirl of business and social activity, Hope and Martin drew closer together while Mirabel indulged her extravagant tastes until her thirst for "fool's gold" almost ruined the lives of all three. 

Thérèse Benson has written a lively, modern story of love and ambition, and brightened it with generous flashes of kindly humor. The character of Hope Willing is charming and refreshing and the tone of the whole story, free from the morbidness and frustration so prevalent in our modern novels, has that clean sparkling common sense which has characterized all the work of this popular author.

CHAPTER I 

HOPE WILLING looked down with a frown at the dark footprints defacing the white marble of her doorsteps. These steps had known the tread of many of her ancestors and their distinguished guests. She recalled her mother's tales of the stout, red-faced women who used to come bearing on their heads shining tin pans containing sea sand or marble dust, for sale to the housewives at a nickel a measure, to aid in scrubbing the stone to spotless purity. Next, on special occasions, followed the unrolling and laying of a strip of carpet of a royal purple, over which generation after generation had mounted, silken, scented and laced, the finest flowers of their day, to find generous entertainment. With such memories, the mud tracked there earlier that morning appeared as a symbol of the stain that might sully her father's fair fame unless her intervention proved successful.

In the interest of economy, Harkins the butler had already been replaced by a parlor maid who now opened the door for her young mistress. "Take my things, Rose," she said, divesting herself of the black hat and coat that weighed down her spirits as constant reminders of her loss, her father having always insisted that gay clothes alone were suitable for the young; "only, before you go up with them, bring me two dusters, one silk, one coarser. 

"Yes, miss." Rose's tone was deferential and she did her errand quickly; yet, when the woman had gone again, before she set about her task, Hope waited, listening to the maid's footsteps as she mounted to the floor above and to the faint slam of a distant door which signaled the fact that Rose had ascended to the servants' quarters by the back stair.

For Hope Willing was about to do a most unconventional

FOOL'S GOLD 

thing in the most conventional city in the world, a thing that any of her living relatives would have regarded with horrified amazement and concern. Philadelphia, a Tory city during the Revolution, gently sympathetic with the South during the Rebellion, had not yet come to realize the changes wrought by the World War-and now she was going in person to remove the dried mud from her front steps! She would have said there was no one else to do it, since the furnace man, who had charge of pavement and steps, had finished his labors and left hours before; yet to herself she must have acknowledged that there was something of superstition in the action, an omen was invoked that thus she would remove all stain upon her name.

Her task accomplished, without one glance along the street to see how many had observed her at the work, she reëntered the house (having providently propped the door open with the vestibule mat) and returned to the front parlor where she had planned to continue cataloguing the miniatures in one of the cabinets. Instead, finding herself in no mood for careful description and appraisal, she wandered over the dark old Spruce Street house and, moved by a novel affection, appreciated for the first time its air of quiet elegance and its familiar heirlooms, now that she knew all must go in the general smash. 

"I ought to be glad our family were such connoisseurs,' she thought bitterly, examining one treasure after another and laying each down with a sigh of renunciation; "it's all money in my pocket now." 

Tender memories obliterated even the dislike she had always felt for the two high-ceiled, narrow parlors; the front one, its windows draped with heavy hangings of brocade over filmy lace, opening on the street; the smaller room at the rear, with but one window, giving on the typical Philadelphia side yard, from which the stairway, dining room and pantry also were lit. The kitchen, reverently regarded by the early housewives and their architects and builders, was not stowed away in the basement. It was the last room in this long extension of back buildings, looking out on the large, sunny, brick-paved

FOOL'S GOLD 

3 yard adorned with two grass plats, a magnolia tree and borders with rose bushes. Vines of honeysuckle were trained over the top of the white fence, which had a green gate to admit provision boys from the narrow back street. As a child, one peeped out now and then, consumed with curiosity; for, through it there were orders that one was never, never to go! 

The bright day nursery on the top floor was the last place she visited. Here she gathered up the colored bulb glasses which made the deep window sills gay. Each winter her mother had brought the bulbs, and together they had poured in water and watched for the white rootlets which heralded the coming of the leaves. Later the flowers pushed up and, while their scent was too strong if one bent over them closely, the faint perfume that filled the whole room was one of her nursery souvenirs. If she ever had children she would fill their playroom windows with flowers. Bulb glasses, so the expert had said, were a bygone fashion, they would not sell for enough to pay for packing them. She could keep them with a clear conscience. They were part of her youth and of Philadelphia.

Hope knew now that all of this was precious, even its inconveniences and prohibitions which had so irked her when she was little. She knew now that she wanted to keep it, with its implication of privilege, as a background for an ordered life. The house had been her mother's, a wedding present from her father-in-law, and at first her lawyer had had some thought of attempting to save at least an interest in it for the girl; but there were things Hope held more dear than property and she very shortly made it plain that she would not countenance any such action. "If my father had lived and had sold out his investments at a profit, he would have taken the money, wouldn't he?”

She gazed at Mr. Bohlen with that peculiarly limpid gaze from her clear eyes that always set him thinking of a mountain stream he had fished when he was a boy. Not waiting for his answer, which was obvious, she had gone on: "Also, he would have paid his losses like a gentleman, so that is what

FOOL'S GOLD 

you must do for him-and for me. Our honor is at stake."

The lawyer regarded her indulgently, sure of his ability to persuade her to a more reasonable stand. He thought of her as quixotic, and a mere child, charming in her earnestness and lack of understanding of the sacrifices the course she suggested would necessitate. "My dear young lady," he said, "that is very admirable, and a very natural reaction on your part. You'll forgive me for pointing out again that it is not practical. As I see my duty, it calls on me to save for you anything I can out of the wreck." "After his debts are settled, I shall be grateful for anything that is left," Hope returned. "First, no one must be able to say that my father owed a penny that was not promptly paid. 

Is there enough for that?" 

"I hope so," Mr. Bohlen replied grudgingly, "but, permit me to add, I don't think you understand what the decision you are making entails. You will be stripped to the very bone-' 

Here a happy thought struck him. "Moreover, you're not of age. Your guardians must be consulted before any such drastic action can be taken." Having jumped to this conclusion, he beamed on her, mildly triumphant.

Involuntarily Hope smiled at the relief this inspiration plainly brought the worthy gentleman and shook her head chidingly, as might one where the balance of years lay the other way. "It's no good," she explained gently. "Mother left everything to Father, and I have his will in my box at the Trust Company. It didn't seem worth while to probate it, though perhaps I ought. He left me everything, absolutely, to do with as I pleased after I reached eighteen, and my birthday was in July. You see-he trusted me."

Nothing moved her from her decision. Not even the flock of relatives, near and distant, who descended upon her to point out all the patent disadvantages that must accrue from the course she was pursuing so steadfastly. "At least I pray there is no truth in the report that you are even turning over the ten thousand dollars your Grandfather 

Biddle left you in Pennsylvania Railroad shares?" Great-aunt

FOOL'S GOLD 

5 

Thorne Shippen said, her massive head shaking as if palsied in her excitement. Great-aunt Shippen was nearly eighty. A tremendous force in her day, she yet retained her influence in the family. "We are paying Father's indebtedness dollar for dollar," 

Hope told her. "Unless some other claim turns up, I'll have a little left." "How much?" Cousin Harriet Norris demanded without false delicacy, small black eyes twinkling expectantly against the background of her red face. Cousin Harriet was subject to a rash, which she attributed to inherited gout and others attributed to gluttony. She was a widow, very rich and very penurious. Her interest in money, her own or another's, was unfailing. "Oh, four-perhaps five thousand dollars—” "That's not so bad," Louise Denckla, Aunt Thorne's oldest child, a small-boned, bird-like woman interrupted, nodding briskly as if she pecked at each sentence she emitted. "I introduced Camilla on less than that. You'll not be done out of your début after all, Hope."

Possibly no one save a Philadelphian aged eighteen can understand what it means for a girl there to be born to the Purple, so to speak, which calls for governesses or private schools and in good time graduation from the select "invitation" dancing classes of adolescence to the Dancing Class and the Assembly, and then, when she is of an age to enter upon this Promised Land with its proffer of attention, belledom, admiration and an eminently suitable Philadelphia marriage where pedigrees and pocketbooks are in agreeable accord, to find oneself cut off, by the sudden failure of that necessary evil, money, from all one has looked forward to, from kindergarten days, as a natural and inalienable right. After

As thoughts of all this coursed through Hope's brain, Cousin 

Louise continued: "Let me see, how can we manage it? this house is closed, you'll have to stay with one or other of us, turn and turn about, until you are out of mourning. We can certainly arrange that. I'll be glad to have you at Devon

FOOL'S GOLD 

for April and August and I'll bring you out next winter. You should marry in your first year. You'll have to make a point of it-" "That ought to take care of itself." Harriet Norris looked the girl over appraisingly and enumerated her good points as though she were not present. "Hope has the sort of looks that stand out in a crowd. She's tall without being gawky, indeed I've heard her called graceful; her skin's excellent, I never saw her with a pimple; her teeth are fine, my dentist told me they were close to perfection; she's got a head like a deer—” "If I have to remind you of some animal, I'm glad it wasn't a cat," Hope interposed, resenting the personalities. "It's as well you should understand your advantages, Hope; they outweigh mere money." Great-aunt Thorne, like most 

Philadelphians an unconscious snob, spoke with deliberation. "You have a very fine presence. You will be a very handsome woman when you are older. And you are of superlatively good birth. Anyone you marry must recognize the fact that you are not a mere fortune hunter."

The family conclave was relieved. A suitable solution of 

Hope's affair appeared to have been arrived at. They had no need to remind each other that Louise Denckla had always been noted among them for her sound common sense. A low murmur of congratulation passed from one to another, hearing which their young relative lifted her delicately drawn brows and regarded them with a puzzling expression. "And what happens if I don't marry in my first season?" she asked. 

"Then we'll have to consider what we all can do for you,'

Great-aunt Thorne said, gathering her minions with a commanding glance about the circle. 

Hope met her eye, a wry smile twisting her lips. "You're very kind," she murmured, wondering who there was present who would sympathize with her in what she was about to tell them. Cousin Maria Pepper? No, she would never prove an active ally.

FOOL'S GOLD 

7 

Cousin Maria, Mrs. Shippen's younger daughter, was sternly disapproved of by the elders of the clan, being suspected of democratic leanings. She had once crossed on the steamer with some common people from North Broad Street, and, to her amazement, had found that they were quite nice! When her mother learned that they had called on her in Philadelphia and that Maria was contemplating returning the call, she had felt it necessary to intervene. She would countenance no lowering of the barriers that divided the city north of Market Street from that sacred precinct lying south of it. "Would you like Caroline to marry one of the sons?" she had asked coldly.

Caroline was then five, but Maria was sound at heart, all that a Philadelphia mother should be. "God forbid!" she had answered piously, and the call was never made.

None the less the confidence of her relatives had been shaken. 

Feeling their lack of trust, she yet was unable to reinstate herself. By nature a voluble woman, she was silent at family councils or absented herself from them entirely; for thereafter she knew herself to be regarded doubtfully as one having manifested Bolshevik tendencies. Her daughter, little Caroline

Pepper, who had no right to be a member of such a serious assemblage but had come because she adored Hope, and who had behaved very well, so far, for one of the younger generation, now interrupted her cousin. "Gee, Hopie, I'm glad that's settled. Mamma'll ask you to stay with us most of the time, and I speak first to be your maid of honor." "Don't be a-monkey!" Hope modified her language in view of her audience. "Naturally I have other plans. I'm not going to spend my last cent speculating in husbands." 

Her audience stared at her as if stunned. "Oh, I know you've all been very kind," she went on; "but in your hearts you're blaming Father for acting on information exactly as any one of you would have done in his place. 

And I'm not saying your advice now isn't sound. I'm only

FOOL'S GOLD 

saying it's gambling, and that's exactly what you condemn him for " "Suffering Sam!" Caroline exploded. "What do you mean to do? I'll bet it's something crazy." "If it's another tea room," Cousin Sarah Shippen, wife of

Aunt Thorne's oldest son, said with an air of anticipating the worst, "I must tell you in strictest confidence that in spite of her title and the long list of patronesses I got for her, the Countess Platsudska's Polish tea room is not a financial success -and she has such a charming place on Locust Street—such atmosphere such—' 

"It's not a tea room," Hope assured her, and an audible sigh of relief rose from her audience. "Then it's antiques," Louise Denckla's experienced eyes roved from a Savery curio cabinet to a Gilbert Stuart portrait, flitted to a Duncan Phyfe desk and came to rest on one of a pair of early Adam console tables over which long mirrors hung, reflecting and doubling the length of the rooms. "You've got a good start here for that, though I think some arrangement should be made to save the family likenesses. 

It's when you come to buy more that lack of experience will hamper you." "What's here no longer belongs to me," Hope briefly explained. Cousin Louise had touched on a sore subject; there had been a pang in arranging to part with treasure that had come into the possession of her people through the generations before her. "You don't need to guess. I'm quite willing to tell you my plans. I intend to take a business course and find a position in an office."

Her auditors sat as if stunned. None of them had expected anything so revolutionary. Aunt Susan Cadwallader's mouth dropped open until she resembled a well-bred codfish. It was the oldest present who rallied first, all the inherited prejudices and precautions of years behind her words. "You in an office?" Mrs. Shippen exclaimed. "A Biddle, a Willing in a man's office? Impossible, my dear. Who would chaperon you?"

FOOL'S GOLD 

9 Long and heated on the part of her relatives, reasoned but determined on the part of Hope, was the discussion that followed. She had no intention of accepting charity even from members of her own family, and pointed out patiently that she possessed none of the superficial accomplishments. "I'd never make a fortune painting place cards, or cadging for orders for miniatures among my friends and relations. 

I've just one tune on the piano which cost Father about two thousand dollars. In fact I've no genteel talents to fall back on in case that hypothetical marriage in my first season fails to come off. But Mr. Bohlen acknowledges that I've a head for business. To my mind it's a case of being thankful there's something practical I am suited for." 

Her relatives left that day defeated, only to return again and again to the attack. When they talked of the prestige of the family, Hope countered by asking if it would be improved by having an object of charity living on them? When they tried to persuade her to salvage all that was possible from the wreck, she explained with good-natured patience that her lawyer was prepared to do just that-when all outstanding accounts were paid. In principle, it was impossible to disagree with her. In practice they refused to acknowledge that her father's brokers were deserving of such generosity. Had they not led him on to speculate, not for his gain but for their own? Despite all argument, her code remained unaltered.

A debt was a debt and must be paid in full. Finding herself unable to convince them, and being entirely certain she was electing to follow the proper course for her, Hope eventually avoided formal good-bys and betook herself to New York and her new life. 

Great Aunt Thorne's old house on Fourth Street, near

Walnut, the last of the wide, roomy, red-brick and whitemarble residences there still occupied by a descendant of the man for whom it was built, resounded with protests at the next family dinner. Every Saturday the fern dish for the center of the table was refilled by the florist, and this event of the week followed promptly at two-thirty on Sunday. Jef-

IO 

FOOL'S GOLD 

ferson, the colored butler, set places for all who reached there by two-fifteen, and woe betide any late comer! Her cook had no equal, and Aunt Thorne's dinners were a gourmet's delight. She had private sources of supply for her oysters, crabs, lobsters, terrapin and game; her late husband's cellar was watched over with loving care by Jefferson; and she herself was a connoisseur of both food and wine. 

To that she attributed her good health. In fact her only illness in years had occurred the previous winter; and the cure, mildly suggested by her doctor, had been a milk diet for a few days. "Two quarts of milk a day, Mrs. Shippen, for three days. 

Then let me know how you are." "I don't care for milk-but anything you say, doctor," she had replied with unwonted meekness, since she had really been feeling very badly.

When he was gone it was quite another matter. She took the two quarts of milk as ordered, but she ate her regular meals as well. 

To her daughters' protests she presented an iron front. "You can't suppose the man meant to starve me at my time of life?" was all the satisfaction they received; and she promptly got well, assured that the doctor, in his wisdom, had divined that she was faint from undernourishment.

She was still a force, though feebler physically than of yore. 

Those near to her clung to her, fearing that there was none among them to take her place when she was gone. In their restricted circle they did their best to ignore the changes in their city. Their ideal was the ease, the protected grace of living in an age that had been left behind. They refused to recognize a world that did not admit their preeminence and, while they knew it existed, still hoped that their contempt for it might destroy it.

At this particular dinner the talk circled away from the girl's affairs only to return to the subject again and again, Edward Shippen being especially censorious. "The modern girl is the curse of the age," he declared

FOOL'S GOLD 

II finally. "Hope has been allowed her own way too long," his brother Wurts chimed in. "Someone should have taken a firm stand against her quixotism.' "Should have nipped it in the bud," Edward agreed, and was rewarded by a chorus of assent. 

Old Mrs. Shippen allowed the protests about Hope's course to go unchallenged for some time, possibly with the idea that, expressed, they would exhaust themselves. But, uncontradicted, the general censure seemed to gather force and she waited for an opening although apparently devoting herself affectionately to her dinner. "To think that the child has gone to live in a place where a Biddle is only a joke!" Susan Cadwallader mourned publicly. 

Mrs. Shippen's chance had come. "Perhaps it's just as well." She dipped the ladle into the silver tureen of green turtle soup with what in a less elegant person might have been described as greediness, selecting bits of fat with care. "If no one knows who she is, she can come back here when it dawns on her how reckless she is being.". 

She supped her soup with gusto. "Well, she had better not stay away too long or everyone will be asking what she did with the baby, Aunt Thorne." 

Harriet Norris chuckled vulgarly.

Mrs. Shippen stopped in her appreciation of her soup to regard her niece disapprovingly through her Oxfords. "Coarseness is growing on you, Harriet," she said with stern dissatisfaction. "While I live, I will be answerable for my brother's granddaughter's reputation, and when I die, Louise and Maria and the rest of you will be called on to protect her. 

Heaven knows, I never expected to see Philadelphia society what it is today, overrun by parvenus, people whose money is their only title to distinction; but I hope there is enough family feeling left among us for that." With dignity she returned to her soup, and Harriet Norris, whose husband had departed from the rigid rules of her family in making his fortune, now even redder in the face than usual, stuttered her excuses.

FOOL'S GOLD 

"Of course, of course. I was only joking. No one would ever dare to hint that Hope—” "They would be foolish if they did." Mrs. Shippen, ready for the next course, glared at the offender while throwing out a challenge to the table at large. "I can tell you all that the only one of you capable of carrying on the old traditions 

I have upheld-" she paused, and her glance, laden with contemptuous pity, swept past her sons, both pallid intellectuals; their wives, fashionable nonentities; her daughters; sons-inlaw; several grandchildren and nieces, and rested at last on the deviled crabs Jefferson was presenting to her notice, "is just Hope Willing!" she ended her delayed sentence. "Good for you, Grandmamma Shippen!" It was Caroline 

Pepper's high young voice that dared to applaud. "You've found it out too. Hopie's got guts, and that's the truth.”

Mrs. Shippen had decided on her crab, large, luscious and browned to perfection, and Jefferson had deftly deposited it on her plate with a massive pair of silver tongs. 

Everyone waited breathless for the word from the mighty that would reduce Caroline to the atom she was. 

Slowly the great lady took in the expectancy of her guests. 

She nodded her head ponderously and smiled at little Caroline. "Possibly it is not the word I should myself have selected”she paused as if to consider a debatable point; "but you're quite right, Caroline, my dear. As you say, 'Hopie's got guts.'

CHAPTER II 

PRIOR to the date of her final departure from the city of her birth, when the sale by auction of the Willing furniture, silver, china and pictures took place in New York at the well-known Art Galleries there, the young owner of the collection, who had written the catalogue raisonné herself, was advised to be present to answer any questions that might arise as to the origin of articles presented for sale.

She was alone, having refused escort and given Aunt Thorne her word that she would take the first train back to Philadelphia at the end of each session.

As her taxi pulled up at the curb, a large sign in black and gold caught her eye. She paid the cab-driver before allowing herself to read it.

AUTHENTIC ANTIQUE FURNITURE AND OBJETS D'ART THE POSSESSIONS OF ONE FAMILY COLLECTED THROUGH SEVERAL GENERATIONS NO REPRODUCTIONS 

Sale on Friday and Saturday afternoons at 2.30 and 

Saturday evening at 8.45

The Board of Directors of the Galleries had suggested advertising "The Willing and Biddle Collections, from Philadelphia," a suggestion Hope had refused to consider. It was bad enough to be obliged to sell what had been theirs for so long, without vulgarly dragging honored names into the glare of electrics at a public auction room. The estimate given her of the sale value of her possessions had been consoling. Large as his indebtedness was, her father's bankers and brokers would 13

FOOL'S GOLD 

be paid in full. Making capital out of the names of her family might perhaps put more money at her disposal, but, while not hiding from herself that it would be welcome, something within her rebelled at using such a means to that end. Her self-respect was precious to her. Provided all debts were paid, she would manage "to worry along" somehow. "I won't have much else left but I'm glad I could afford to keep my pride," she told herself, rejoicing at the simplicity of the announcement. She was glad also that this sale was to take place in New York rather than in Philadelphia, where the fact that it was her belongings that were going under the hammer would have been sure to leak out.

Socially, Philadelphia had always refused to recognize the existence of New York. Baltimore, yes! One visited in

Baltimore, although-and this was a perennial quarrel-they did not know how to cook terrapin as it was done at home. 

New York might be a rich and growing city but it remained beyond the pale. Philadelphia could not consent to be interested in anything that went on there. 

The experts of the Art Association had inspected her possessions at her house, and there had been no necessity for Hope to go over until the first day of the sale. Afraid of being late, she had arranged to be at the Galleries three quarters of an hour before the hour advertised. She wandered around rather aimlessly, amazed at the extent of the space for display and glad to find that her cherished belongings, divorced from their familiar surroundings, no longer stirred her to emotion.

When by accident she stumbled upon the sale room, a vast assembly hall with a raised platform, or stage, at one end, she was further surprised to find that many of the camp chairs and plush divans were already occupied. 

These first comers were mainly habitual auction addicts, bent on securing their favorite posts. Some of them bought rarely but with discrimination; some never bid upon anything, deriving from the various contests a certain pleasurable excitement which was measurably increased when the bidding ran into large figures; and yet others took great joy in a bar-

FOOL'S GOLD 

15 gain and stood ready to snap up any article they thought cheap, no matter what its character or period. 

To certain individuals, of whom there are a surprising number, the red carpets and hangings, the rich women and those they presume to be rich, the luxurious furs and scents, the presence even the rumor of the presence of this or that notability, the very over-heated air they breathe in such company, afford an intoxication comparable perhaps to that of those who go to a race meeting not to see the races but to watch the crowd. Added to all these were the professionals, who would bid cautiously for themselves, with dash when they had a commission.

In few places is there a better chance offered to study human nature, and by the second day of the sale Hope fancied herself quite auction-wise.

She knew in which quarter to look for the most persistent bidding. She had learned to recognize the representatives of the various museums. She could sense the fluttering excitement of the woman carried by her own eagerness beyond the limit she had set for herself, as well as the calm persistency of one who was from the beginning determined to possess a coveted lot without regard to price.

On the one article she meant to save for herself Hope felt that she was quite qualified to do her own bidding, in fact that she would rather enjoy the experience; but as number after number was put up and knocked down, usually after spirited competition, her nerves grew tense and she wondered if it would not have been wiser to commission the Art Association agent to bid it in for her.

She had established herself in an end seat on a side aisle, and now, to steady herself, instead of concentrating on the auction, she began to listen to the conversations around her. 

Beside her on her left was an old lady in priceless sables and a dusty black velvet toque, who bid once or twice on many of the lots and who was tactfully restrained from buying by whispered assurances from a neat companion that she already possessed superior examples of that particular genre.

FOOL'S GOLD 

In the row in the rear, a man and wife bought with discrimination and disagreed about their purchases with acerbity; while seated in front of her were two very smartly dressed young women who were reserving a chair between them by piling it with their wraps and furs. The room was crowded, and already they had been called on several times to explain that they were saving the place for a friend, and evidently, in the circumstances, they had begun to feel uncomfortable about it, for one or other of them was continually turning to gaze anxiously toward the entrance in the rear. "It would be just like Mirabel not to come at all, after making us promise to keep a seat for her!" the little blonde exclaimed sotto voce. "Don't worry," the brunette rejoined. "She'll be here.

She wants that picture-heaven alone can tell why-and I never knew Mirabel to give up anything she really wanted." "I can't see what she sees in it," the pretty little blonde said fretfully. "It's not an antique. I'd understand it if it wasn't so old-fashioned or if the man was some relation of hers or her husband's-" 

A sharp rap from the auctioneer's hammer interrupted. "This, ladies and gentleman, it may surprise you to learn, is not a salon for the encouragement of brilliant conversation. 

It is an auction sale, and with your permission we will now resume. Let me call your attention to Number 377 in your catalogue-" Under cover of the ripple of laughter this rebuke brought forth, Brunette whispered swiftly to Blonde: "Depend upon it, if Mirabel gets it, the man will be a relation—a near one! 

She feels the need of something real to offset Mark's blue blood."

It had been decided that the Willing pictures were important enough to justify an evening sale. Men who were generous bidders, and who had neither the time nor the inclination to go to daylight auctions, would attend after dinner. 

Private offers had been made for two Gilbert Stuarts, a Peale and a Copley. These tenders had come from museums, and

FOOL'S GOLD 

17 the amounts suggested had staggered those members of the family who had thought to acquire the family portraits from 

Hope at a nominal price.

All such proposals had been refused because the girl had been advised that in open competition even larger sums would be realized. Some of the most valuable furniture and all of the oil paintings and Rembrandt etchings had been reserved for this last event of the week, making so full a catalogue that it had become necessary to place a few original drawings in the afternoon sale in order to reduce the evening list. Of these the subject of one only was a man, a peculiarly sympathetic portrait in colored chalks on tinted paper of Hope's father by J. McClure Hamilton. This had been a gift to her mother from the artist, and had continued to hang on the wall of her vacant room after her death. It had never belonged to 

Hope. When she had suggested buying it privately, Mr. 

Bohlen had told her to take it and say nothing about it. No one would begrudge her such a memento where she was sacrificing so much more than the law called for. 

Hope had refused doggedly. "It's by McClure Hamilton," had been her answer; "it's worth something, and I want that something to swell the general fund. I think I'm superstitious about keeping anything back. I have a feeling that I must placate the Fates." She looked up at Mr. Bohlen appealingly and, as always, he capitulated before what he read in her eyes. "Very well, very well, my dear. Have it your own way.

But don't buy it privately. Let it go into the sale. It's so obviously a portrait that no one else will want it, and you'll be able to pick it up for a song." 

Yet here was someone who wanted it. 

Hope waited anxiously for a sight of her opponent, and at last she came. 

She had dubbed the two other young women "Brunette" and "Blonde," which left her no word to describe this silvery, radiant creature, so pure and fair in her coloring as hardly to suggest mere flesh and blood. Fresh from out of doors, she

FOOL'S GOLD 

swept in among those jaded by the heat and contests of the auction room like a breath of reviving air.

There was a scramble to clear her chair of its protecting wraps, a murmur of: "My dear, we thought you were never coming!" and the beauty was settled in her place. "I'm not at all late," Mirabel said calmly. "I inquired in the office what time lot 400 would be likely to be reached. So, knowing you were keeping a seat for me, there was no need for me to hurry. I'm a believer in fresh air, and these rooms are always horribly stuffy during a sale. Something really ought to be done about the ventilation."

A sharp rap of the auctioneer's hammer again drew the attention of the audience to the fact that business was still being transacted, and Mirabel appropriated the little blonde's catalogue to inform herself how the sale was going, while behind her bowed head her friends exchanged exasperated glances. "Frightful prices things are bringing," she said nonchalantly to Brunette when Blonde had insistently reclaimed her catalogue to write in the figures for the last lots. "However, I've simply got to have that picture." 

Hearing this Hope squared her jaw and looked this newcomer over appraisingly. "Beautiful but not dumb," was her conclusion. Whatever the girl wanted the portrait for was likely to be part of a carefully considered plan.

In the interval between the next sales, Brunette asked the question that was uppermost in Hope's mind. "I thought you had cut out pictures as stupid over-decoration, Mirabel," she said. "What in the world makes you want this?" "Sentimental reasons," Mirabel answered unhesitatingly, "and I depend upon you not to let Mark know I bought it here. You see, it's so exactly like my dear father that I shan't hesitate to say it's his picture. Why not, when it couldn't be more like him if he'd posed for it? Sent me by Aunt Annie, of course. I've written to warn her she's sending it. Mark's so queer he probably wouldn't wish to have it around unless

FOOL'S GOLD 

19 he believed it was really one of the family-so the 'Eleanor' on it is providential! My mother's name, you know.' 

Hope flushed angrily. Her father-her father's portrait at least, to be appropriated so brazenly by this insolent young beauty, who was reputed never to give up anything she really wanted! If it took every penny she had-but the drawing was being set up on the pedestal, held upright between two uniformed attendants.

It was, she found, too delicately executed to carry well in that great audience hall. Hope was rather glad of that, since it would reduce the number of bidders. "Number 401 in your catalogues, ladies and gentlemen. 

What am I offered for Number 401? This is a piece for the connoisseur, the work of a distinguished artist, an unusual thing to come from McClure Hamilton's skilled hand, a sketch which it is plain to see has been a labor of love-" (How did the man know that? The inscription, of course, "To Eleanor, in fond remembrance of-") "I venture to prophesy that, not in your lifetime, perhaps, nor in mine, but eventually, this little masterpiece will be ranked as high as the Romney it will be my privilege to present to your discriminating judgment tonight. Will someone start this, please?" "Five dollars!" someone shouted from the opposite side of the room, so unnecessarily loud that everyone laughed. 

However the auctioneer took the offer up. “Thank you, sir, for your generosity," he said ironically. "No doubt you realize that your bid bears no relation to the value of this work of art-and-ah-work of affection; but it proves at least that you are not tongue-tied and I am glad to accept it as a first bid.” "Seven-fifty," said Mirabel clearly.

An instant later Hope raised her catalogue and the contest was on.

Her muscles constricted and she fancied she heard her heart beating with heavy thuds that echoed and re-echoed inside a hollow cavity that was her chest, yet she managed to keep

FOOL'S GOLD 

her head and to bid without undue eagerness.

The man with the large voice dropped out after a couple more explosive bids, always rewarded by a murmur of applauding laughter, and although once or twice raised catalogues indicated other bidders, the main competition was between Hope and the young woman whose name was Mirabel. 

The result was a foregone conclusion, since the intrinsic value of the sketch was not a factor with Hope. It was finally knocked down to her and she sank back in her chair, her heart at rest. Her father would remain hers and hers only.

To her amazement, when the boy came down the aisle in search of the buyer's signature Mirabel intercepted him, holding out her hand for the pencil and card he carried. 

In an instant, stirred to action by indignation, Hope was on her feet. "That picture was sold to me!" she cried sharply.

Mirabel did not turn her head but continued to write on the card balanced on her handbag. "Mr. Bergson!" Hope appealed directly to the auctioneer. "You took my bid on 401. The lady in front of me appears to be claiming it."

From his elevated position Bergson looked down and nodded. "Your bid, miss. As you state, I took it myself." 

Without undue haste Mirabel stood up in her place, her expression that of a wronged angel. Her beauty had carried her far and she meant it to carry her farther. "I'm very, very sorry there should be any mistake," she said with appealing softness, "but really, it was my bid, and I can't give the picture up.' 

"Sorry, too," Bergson, far from melting at sight of so much sorely troubled pulchritude, was annoyed at waste of time that would cut into his dinner hour. "There's no possibility of mistake. Every bid you made was on the halves, madam.

This young lady had the break on the even numbers, and I knocked the lot down to her." "But I hadn't nearly reached my limit!" Mirabel cried

FOOL'S GOLD 

21 angrily. "I demand that you put the picture up again." "All sales are final," Bergson replied, tired of the controversy. "We will now proceed with the sale of Lot 402, ladies and gentlemen. The last bid was yours, Mr. H. $175 for this Lowestoft tureen-" The business proceeded. "This is an outrage. I shall lodge a complaint with the 

Board of Directors of the Art Association," Mirabel muttered 

angrily. "I won't submit to such treatment. I'm going.'

And regardless of her friends or the convenience of anyone else, she pushed along the line of seats to the center aisle, up which she marched, head in air, never wasting so much as a glance in the direction of the successful competitor.

Hope bent low over her catalogue, and in the next interval the little blonde spoke with the evident intention of being overheard: "That was just once Mirabel got what was coming to her! 

I'll bet my boots she's gone out to register a kick at the office and, when she finds that gets her nowhere, she'll leave a whacking big offer of an advance in price on that picture. And 

I hope, I very much hope, she doesn't get it!" 

Hope lifted her head and smiled at the girl without disguise. "She won't!" she said.

CHAPTER III 

To others, the transition appeared cruelly abrupt, To Hope, who had faced it and fought for it through all the eleven long months required to settle her father's tangled affairs, it came as a blessed relief to find herself established in a room of her own in a club for women in New York City, freed from the nagging solicitude of a family who in the main cared more for the prestige of their own precious clan than for her as an individual.

In every way it was easier for her to begin away from home a life that must necessarily be on a much less luxurious scale than that to which she was accustomed. She shrank from exciting the compassion of her former associates because “poor Hope Willing" could no longer afford to join their expensive amusements. Once out of Philadelphia, there was a pleasurable excitement in entering on an existence so foreign to all she had known before. Unconsciously she held herself aloof from her associates in New York, not from an inherited snobbishness, which would have been natural enough, but because they failed to stimulate her imagination. Fortunately she was young; school was so recent an experience that she had not lost the habit of application, and she quickly found a deep interest in the business course she had selected. "It's the first thing I've studied since I could read that has seemed to be of any use-to me, I mean. Of course, except languages,” she ended explanatorily. She was speaking to one of her teachers, a well-tailored, dried-up, hard-featured woman of middle age, an expert at shorthand, who had left the class room to catch the same elevator and had fallen into step with her on the street. "You are doing very well, Miss Willing. You are taking advantage of what is offered to you. I may say that I have 22

FOOL'S GOLD 

23 not felt in you what I call 'sales resistance.' So many of our pupils seem to fancy that they are granting me a personal favor when they learn what I am here to teach them. I am obliged to 'sell' my subject to them, if you see what I mean. You might think it was I who was the loser when they giggle away the time they pay me for." The tight-lipped woman stared at her companion to make sure she understood. 

Hope nodded. "There're a lot of silly kids come here," she remarked judicially. "It surprises me. I expected a business college to be much more serious." 

"The majority of 'em only think of a job as a steppingstone to a star position where they'll marry their boss and never have to do another stroke of work," Mrs. Weismuller grumbled. "Well, I married my boss, and see me! What's the nursery rhyme? 

Needles and pins, needles and pins, When a man marries his trouble begins. 

What about a woman's troubles, I'd like someone to tell me? 

They not only begin, they never end."

Hope had nothing to say to this and they walked a short distance in silence, which Mrs. Weismuller broke: "You mentioned languages, Miss Willing. Can you speak German?" "No," Hope replied, "I'd just started to learn it when the war came. Then, if you remember, it ceased to be very popular. Like most girls, I had a French nurse, so I can speak 

French. After my mother died, my father spent some winters in Italy, and I was with him on all my holidays, so I understand a little Italian too."

Mrs. Weismuller noted the unconscious betrayal in that mention of a French nurse as one of the ordinary necessities. 

It simply confirmed her previous impression that Hope was not the usual type of girl to enter the commercial ranks. "Do you mind my asking if you mean to stick to a com-

FOOL'S GOLD 

mercial career?" There was no idle curiosity about this inquiry and Hope answered frankly. "It interests me. I know of nothing to prevent my sticking." "Then you ought to study German," Mrs. Weismuller declared. "My husband's American, born and bred, but he has folks in Germany, and you'd be surprised at the things they write him. In the business world, Germany's coming right back; and it might land you a really big position some day if you could speak the language." "I'll take lessons." Hope accepted the suggestion without hesitation, and, pushed away by the crowd, her companion lost step with her and regained it again before she continued: "You'll think I just brought it up on that account, still, you'd do well if you'd let Herman teach you. He's educated, and he speaks good German." "Herman?" the girl's tone was interrogative. "My husband. He's paralyzed. Run over by a 'hit and run' driver, coming 'round the corner lickety split against the lights. Drunk, most likely, but he got away. Herman was head buyer for the rug department at one of the big department stores and I was his stenog. When he was hurt we were both thrown out of work and there was nobody to sue for damages. Willie was a freshman then, and of course I wouldn't let him stop college to help support his father. I never was one to throw money away, so we had something saved, and he's our only child. He'll be a professor some day and we're not going to be a drag on him, not while I can work, at any rate. She paused, a little breathless. She was not a woman given to talking about her own affairs, and it excited her, although even when she mentioned her son, whom she idolized, her face showed no trace of emotion. As Hope still said nothing, she went on: "It's not only the money, Miss

Willing, though I don't deny every little helps, it's the effect it would have on Herman. It'd build up his morale, I mean, to know he was earning something again and wasn't just a burden. There were times before I got this job when I was afraid to go out looking for work for fear of what he might

FOOL'S GOLD 

25 do to himself while I was away from him. Now we're right enough-only, if you'd care to learn German, I can't think of any better chance for you-' 

"I'd have time in the evenings," Hope suggested gently. "I saw from the first that you weren't like most of our girls.

You don't spend all you have on beauty parlors and the 

Talkies." Mrs. Weismuller had long before appraised Hope's black clothes and knew them to be good; to her mind another point in the girl's favor, for she regarded cheap ready made things as poor economy. "Well, I want to turn off here.

Our address is 293 West Fifty-sixth Street. Better write it down. Any time that suits you'll suit Father."

Thus Hope's little leisure was soon filled to overflowing. 

On holidays, and frequently over week-ends, she returned to Philadelphia at the insistence of Great-aunt Thorne Shippen, who did not propose either that her grandniece should be forgotten or should give anyone an excuse for such slander as

Harriet Norris had suggested; and even when she had left the college and taken a secretarial position she kept up this custom until Mrs. Shippen's death; which occurred about two years later after a particularly delicious light supper of Philadelphia broiled oysters followed by ducklings stuffed with wild rice, raisins and candied orange peel and served with a madeira sauce.

After this sad event, Louise Denckla, with great presence of mind, snapped up Jefferson and the cook. Her brothers divided the cellar between them. Various heirlooms were allotted to each child and the big house was shut until such time as a sufficiently substantial offer for it should be received from one of the commercial enterprises that had invaded the neighborhood.

The old days of open hospitality were over. Aunt Thorne's fortune was split up among her heirs. The Dencklas had not the money necessary to support such lavish and indiscriminate entertaining, even had they been so inclined, and no one else stepped into the breach. "You're the only one who could do it, just as Grandmamma

FOOL'S GOLD 

Shippen said," Caroline Pepper reminded Hope tearfully, shortly after the funeral. "Why don't you marry Charlie 

Page? He's crazy about you, and with his money, you could reëstablish the prestige of the family." "Nonsense!" Hope said briskly. "Don't try to tempt the poor working girl. I've got a job, woman!" "Well, I'm tired of seeing Philadelphia society at the mercy of the descendants of mutton butchers and shopkeepers. 

Unless something is done about it, the next generation will be making playmates of bootleggers' children. If you really won't marry Charlie, I'd probably better do it myself," Caroline sniffled, with a wary eye on Hope to see how she took this hint.

Her cousin's indifference was patent to any observer, so it came about that the positions as first advertised were reversed. 

Clad in pale yellow chiffon with a big brown hat, in which she looked very lovely and felt very conspicuous, having for so long been accustomed to black attire, Hope acted as maid of honor for Caroline Pepper. Thereafter when she went to

Philadelphia it was to stay with the young Pages. 

Theirs was an intimate, horsy, happy-go-lucky crowd that vibrated between hunts, races, dog shows and horse shows, and made well-meaning efforts to meet Hope on her own ground (classified by them vaguely as "highbrow"). She enjoyed them immensely as a diversion but was never sorry to return to her independent existence in New York. 

Meanwhile her earnings grew, enabling her to leave the club and take a tiny apartment. This consisted of a living room, a wee bedroom and bath. It was not an old house but what it lacked in atmosphere was compensated for by the fact that she had not fifty years' accumulation of dirt to contend with. Indeed the building was painfully new and sanitary. 

The rent took more of her income than seemed at all reasonable, yet, although she could have saved money had she elected to join forces with another girl, she shrank from this suggestion whenever it was made and continued to live alone. A life that might have grown increasingly narrow had it not been

FOOL'S GOLD 

27 for the Weismullers.

Through the crippled Herman, whose gentle acceptance of his hard fate was a never failing source of wonder to her rebellious youth, she came to have an appreciation of many small things that might otherwise have passed unnoticed. As if the curtailing of his activities had sharpened his faculties and increased his enjoyment of all that was left to him to enjoy, Herman Weismuller rejoiced in the skies as seen from his window, in the changes of the seasons, in every little bit of beauty that came his way. The first migratory bird, the first spring flower, the blooming of a plant on his window sill brought him a flood of happiness. Music was a passion with him, and when Willie installed a radio for him, it is doubtful if he would have exchanged it for the use of his legs had such an exchange been possible. And withal he was practical. It was he who controlled the budget for their meager income now that he was no longer the chief wage earner, and who sternly refused to countenance many small extravagances his wife and son would have indulged in for his sake, telling them that if he could not make money he did not mean to spend it, and that was flat!

Both Mrs. Weismuller and Herman looked on a certain intimacy between Hope and their only son with approval. 

Willie was going to be a great man some day. Mrs. Weismuller, while recognizing Hope's quality, saw no reason why there should not be a match between them in the future.

Herman, of German parentage and more sensitive to distinctions of caste, was well aware that that could never come about; but to his mind association with Hope must be all to Willie's advantage and would keep him from mingling with girls of coarser fiber. 

And Willie himself was of much the same opinion. He admired Hope to be sure, but entirely impersonally. He was a single-minded youth and his aim was the Pulitzer Prize for the best play of the year. To that end everybody he met was so much material and no more. He cross-questioned Hope exhaustively concerning all the idiosyncrasies of the Philadelphia

FOOL'S GOLD 

social strata. Never again would he have a like opportunity. 

In Great-aunt Thorne Shippen's character he saw wonderful possibilities, only relinquishing the idea reluctantly after Hope had given him The Matriarch to read. "Not, mind you, that it would be the same thing as I would treat it, but the judges would be sure to think I'd got the inspiration there," he explained.

Hope was entirely satisfied so long as he gave up the subject, not being desirous of seeing caricatured one whom she remembered with affection and respect. "You'll never write a great play on hearsay evidence," she insinuated softly. "Study the people about you. No doubt there are a dozen dramas going on right under your nose, and a hundred characters as picturesque as Aunt Thorne, only they are too close to you. You don't get them in the proper perspective." "Do you know, Hope, you really do say some very intelligent things once in a while." From the eminence of his recent Master's degree and his more recent appointment to an instructorship in English, Willie looked down and assured her.

Hope smiled. She liked Willie. His egotism was unconscious and he was a devoted son, one who would never give his parents a moment's anxiety. The smile was occasioned by the thought that he would never give her any anxiety either, although he was accounted handsome and, at the University, certain of his female pupils were already speculating as to whether it would be fun to be coached privately by the new instructor.

For Hope, he was no more than a convenient escort. She was not accustomed to going about unaccompanied at night. 

They went to the theater together whenever "Joe's" had tickets for anything Willie accounted worthy, each paying for a seat without any embarrassing argument and disagreeing absolutely on what they admired on the stage. Hope would rather have had Herman beside her, but his utter helplessness made that impossible. At least it was a satisfaction to feel

FOOL'S GOLD 

29 that both she and Willie were “safe” from each other. "Evidently I'm of what Caroline would call a 'frigid temperament.' Indeed I doubt if two more sexless people exist," 

Hope boasted to herself.

And such of the little gods as are possessed of a sense of humor must have smiled politely behind their hands.

CHAPTER IV 

ONE fine Saturday found the girl on her way to Philadelphia.

Usually, on the trip from New York, she economized by not taking a Pullman seat. Early arrival at the Pennsylvania 

Station ensured a comfortable place next the window on the shady side of a day coach. Coming back, there were a number of reasons why her procedure should be different, the most prominent being the sporting blood of the young Pages, who found a pleasing excitement in missing a train at Broad Street Station in order to see if by fast driving they could catch up with it at North Philadelphia, when it was sure to be crowded.

On this occasion Hope, delayed by a traffic block on Thirtyfourth Street, had been barely in time at the New York end and, hampered by her suitcase, bumped through the day coaches without finding a seat until she reached the Pullmans, where the only vacancy was in a drawing room. This chair she took thankfully, sinking into its plushy embrace as they entered the tunnel under the river, to discover that the middleaged gentleman next to her was Mr. James Bohlen, her father's and her own lawyer.

Expecting immediate recognition, she looked at him with a smile, but in the artificial light, it was a moment before he reacted to it. "Why, bless my soul, if it isn't Hope! How are you, child, and how is the world wagging with you now-a-days?"

He held out his hand and Hope placed hers within it as she made a conventional answer. She was glad to see Mr. 

Bohlen. She had always liked him. 

A younger man seated opposite rose with a muttered "Going to the smoker, Uncle Jim," and left the compartment. 

Hope looked after him comprehendingly. 30

FOOL'S GOLD 

31 "I'm sorry. 

I suppose that was because I came. Otherwise he would have smoked here; but isn't it rather old-fashioned to have such a scruple? Most women smoke.” "Do you?" Mr. Bohlen inquired curiously. During the settlement of her father's affairs when he had seen Hope frequently, he had found her unusual and appealing. To him, it appeared that the youth of the day had become standardized, conforming to the same styles, the same tastes, the same vices, as if they were turned out after one pattern by some vast machine. It would be a pity if the charming girl he remembered had changed into anything so uninteresting. 

Hope laughed, she was really a little touched by his evident concern. "Sometimes. Just to show I don't disapprove. I confess I don't really like it, and I always hesitate to cultivate expensive habits. Only I'm still sorry I drove your nephew away." "I dislike stale smoke. It was on my account Mart made himself scarce," Mr. Bohlen assured her. "I'm glad to see you, my dear, and to have a chance to ask you how you are making out in your tussle with the world? You haven't married?" cc 'Nobody axed me, sir.' No, I'm still a spinster, a fullfledged business woman trudging to my job every morning. 

I'm secretary to the executive head of Schoonmaker's, wholesale dealers in upholstery textiles." "And do you find your work interesting?"

Hope stroked the fox fur she had laid across her knee meditatively. “No,” she said at last, turning on him that peculiarly limpid gaze that had lingered in his memory. "I'm disappointed with business, Mr. Bohlen. What comes my way is no more than a dull routine. When you and I were working over 

Father's estate, I had a goal to aim at. I meant to pay his debts and keep his name as clean as he himself would have kept it. I don't need to tell you that anything we could save for me was badly needed. There was excitement in all that. 

A sense of elation at every little success. In my job I'm an

FOOL'S GOLD 

automaton, just a shade more important than an adding machine or a typewriter because I happen to have a good memory. 

Only-I get no thrill out of it when we enlarge our showrooms to accommodate screens for eighteen thousand samples, for instance. I haven't planned it or worked for it, except indirectly. As a matter of fact it doesn't interest me and I doubt its utility. I find the newest improved lighting blinding and the multiplicity of samples bewildering, and ten chances to one if I want material for anything I rush into a department store and buy it at a counter. I sometimes wish I'd had money enough to study a profession." "Surely there was enough for that?" "Enough to see me through a university, yes. But how many years after a woman gets her degree before she's selfsupporting? And suppose I fell ill? Sickness is the bugaboo that frightens me. I don't suppose you know how frightfully expensive it is nowadays. No, I dared not risk it. I fancy I lack courage. Only isn't it kind of fun being a lawyer? Each new case must be like a battle to be planned for and won.' 

Mr. Bohlen smiled at her enthusiasm, yet found himself liking the girl more and more. There is no flattery more effective than appreciation of one's life work, and this was unconscious flattery. "The law isn't all beer and skittles," he said dryly. "A great deal of it is dull grind, and nine tenths of the rest consists in bringing two unreasonable parties to a reasonable agreement." "Well, even that sounds more exciting than the textile business," Hope declared. "It is so hopelessly commonplace and common sense.' 

"Evidently what you feel the need of is romance-natural enough at your age- 

"Oh, but it isn't that at all!" Hope cried. "And it's not even adventure, although I fancy it must be very satisfying to be an explorer. Imagine the thrill of going somewhere no one had ever been before! No, it's just that I want to feel that I'm of real use. I mean that I'm doing something that

FOOL'S GOLD 

33 no one else could do quite so well. Mrs. Weismuller (she's one of the teachers at the business school I went to) advised me to study German, with the idea that languages might be a help; and the first job I landed was with a German drug firm-where everybody spoke Yiddish!" "Why did you leave them?" 

Hope twinkled at the memory. "At least they didn't fire me," she said. "I left because they were Germans and for no other reason. There was a chemists' convention or something in San Francisco, and I was told to arrange transcontinental transportation for a certain number of visitors who were arriving from Germany. I took all sorts of trouble and I was sure that, coming from Europe, they would appreciate the superior comforts provided for them. 

On their return to New York it soon became painfully plain that I'd been mistaken. So, before any complaints were registered, I demanded to know where the trouble lay. 'Everything was very nice, very nice indeed,' was the lukewarm answer, given with such lack of enthusiasm that I was thoroughly alarmed. 'I may have to do something of this sort again,' I urged, 'it would be a kindness to my inexperience to tell me where I went wrong.' Then it came out. 'So far as we were concerned it was all right. We were quite comfortable. But our wives didn't like the upper berths!"" 

Mr. Bohlen threw back his head and roared till the tears ran down his cheeks. "And they fired you for that?" he asked, wiping his streaming eyes. "I told you they didn't fire me,” Hope replied with dignity. "They didn't even mean to. My boss couldn't see the joke, so 

I resigned. I simply couldn't stand his being so dumb." 

Again Mr. Bohlen shook with laughter. "My dear, you're as good as a tonic," he declared. "The mistake I made was in not insisting on taking you into my own office.' 

"I'd have loved it, but I couldn't have gone to you," Hope assured him regretfully. "My family only refrain from dis-

FOOL'S GOLD 

owning me because I have the good taste to hide my servitude in New York, rather than to flaunt it under their eyes. However, I'm on the lookout for something that offers greater opportunities. After all, I can speak French, German and English and can make some sort of a stab at Italian. There ought to be a position where I can make use of what I do know." 

They continued to talk with interest until Hope left the train at North Philadelphia, where she was met by the young 

Pages and sundry assorted friends, chanting, "Welcome, little stranger," at the top of their healthy young lungs. 

Mr. Bohlen waved his hand to her as she was swept off, gay and laughing, toward their waiting motors, and only turned from looking after her as his nephew, Martin Newbold, reentered the compartment, shaking his head chidingly. "Who was your swift young friend?" he asked with mock disapproval. "Swift nothing!" Mr. Bohlen retorted. "That's the finest girl I know, the bravest and the most honorable. More honorable than most men. Why didn't you come back and meet her?" "I'm no spoilsport. I know when I'm in the way," his nephew declared, still joking. "Besides, I've seen that bright young thing before. She's striking looking, but she's one of the Charlie Page crowd. We can't afford yet a while to run with that bunch. They're too expensive for us, and too rapid." "Stuff and nonsense," said his uncle testily. "They'd be much better for Mirabel than the Little Theatre lot who are making her believe that she's another Duse. Their fun's open air stuff, with wholesome horse-play to work off their high spirits. Not a mite of harm in any of them." "Perhaps you're right," Mart returned indifferently, and changed the subject. The grain of truth in his banter had lain in his statement that he did not feel he could afford to go with the Pages. And the reason for that grew out of a defect in 

Mirabel's make-up. She must outshine anyone with whom she associated. To introduce her to the Pages' set meant demands he felt unable to meet, a matter he did not care to explain in

FOOL'S GOLD 

35 detail to his uncle, who was quite impervious to his wife's charms, being destined to go down in history as one of her few failures. 

For Mirabel was willing, even eager, to please Martin's

Uncle Jim, who had it in his power to do so much for his nephew; and it might perhaps be taken as evidence supporting the theory of chemical attractions and repulsions that his dislike for her increased each time they met, no matter what she did or did not do.

On Sunday evening, his business with Mr. Bohlen and others satisfactorily completed, Mart reasoned that if he could catch the eight o'clock train he would be in time for a party which his wife had been distinctly pouty at his missing. Broad Street 

Station was out of the question, but at North Philadelphia he had a chance. He made for it in a taxi and on arrival, hearing the train draw up at the platform, he dashed up the long flight of steps past a group who seemingly were assisting someone who was lame to mount. "There's a lady being carried up the steps-" he said tentatively to the conductor who, watch in hand, was waiting. 

At this corroboration of a previous statement, a chauffeur, standing helplessly to one side, cut in: "And that's the truth! I told him already, sir, on'y he won't believe me-" "I will not," said the conductor with tightened lips, his eyes glued to his watch. "You can tell Mr. Page I wouldn't hold this train for a split second for the prettiest little liar he knows. He's played that trick before. I wouldn't wait for him, not if he was comin' right up them stairs in an ambulanch-" A bustle on the top step and general excitement. Cries of "We got you here, Hopie, old sport!" "Caroline, you owe me ten. You bet we'd never make it." "Good-by, Hopie darling. I think you're a priceless ass. Why can't we 'phone your boss that you've busted your ankle?"

The conductor snapped shut his old-fashioned hunting-case watch. The girl was hustled on to the already moving train,

CHAPTER V 

INWARDLY Martin Newbold was annoyed. He cursed himself for his officious kindliness. He had no desire to be burdened with the responsibility for a strange girl-one of the Pages' rackety friends at that. Outwardly he was entirely complaisant and solicitous as he helped Hope to her seat. perfect was his manner that a lady comfortably ensconced in the next chair hastily offered to exchange for the one the porter assigned to him, and insisted on so doing, despite Hope's stammered explanation that it was quite unnecessary. 

Once established, both of them conscious of the benign gaze of the mistaken if kindly spinster, who plainly scented romance,

He found the smoker rather full and was glad he had no acquaintances there to hail him with the usual camaraderie of casual traveling companions. There were business matters which he wished to consider dispassionately-a decision to arrive at which would be reached more easily perhaps in the crowded solitude of the smoking car than in the feverish gayety of his home.

He never allowed himself to complain of Mirabel, even to himself, and quite honestly admired her dash and go, and the fact that she was as much the life of any party at four A.M. as at eleven P.M., when he was feeling worn and weary and ready for nothing save bed.

He never permitted himself to contrast his hours of grueling work (spent at a Trust Company where he was the youngest of its Vice Presidents) with her leisure, although she might stay all day in bed if it so pleased her. Instead he reminded himself of her varied interests, her Junior League activities, her Little Theatre sponsorship. "Gad, she's a wonder!" he told himself. "I'll be hanged if I see how she keeps it up. 

It'd kill a mere man."

He took a number of documents from his pocket and went over them frowningly, only to reach the conclusion that, after all, he could come to no decision without consulting his wife. 

In reality his own mind was made up, or he would never have

FOOL'S GOLD 

41 gone to Uncle Jim with the proposition; but would Mirabel see it in the same way? If he took advantage of this exceptional opportunity he would have to resign from the Surety Bank and Trust, and Mirabel was proud of his position there. 

He had had to stop her bragging about it-indirectly, of course, she was never blatant. Moreover he could not conceal the fact that there would be an element of risk in the step: he had faith in himself, yet he might fail utterly to make good in a new field. And his headquarters would necessarily be in Europe.

Mirabel was very secure in the leadership of her chosen set where she was. It was quite conceivable that she might object to such a sudden translation to a city where the social structure was unfamiliar. At all events it was her right to be consulted. 

He sighed rather wearily as he returned the papers to his pocket. He was tired physically and in his mind. This scheme was his own, he had had to convince of its practicality two of the shrewdest men he knew, his uncle and the banker brought into consultation by him. And he had succeeded beyond his most sanguine expectation. They had both endorsed his enterprise and promised to find him any money needed. It was a victory. "I ought to be all pepped up, while I feel like a bag that has been blown up and slapped," he thought. "Damn this party! How I would love to go to bed when I get home.

Mirabel?" Not in the morning, Mirabel was always as irritable as a teething baby in the mornings. There would be no time tomorrow. Plainly Uncle Jim was right. New standards of living had had to be met by new methods in business and in the home.

When reached, the vaunted elevator proved to be of the kind that is operated by the tenants. Thinking irritably that he was never going to be rid of her, he yet could not leave her to run it alone. He felt more cheerful. He'd be on his way in a minute or two. Not that she wasn't a nice enough girl. She had talked very sensibly and unaffectedly coming up in the taxi.
