CHAPTER VI 

HOME at last! The familiar canopy was a welcome sight.

Martin felt as if he had been away for a very long time instead of a couple of days. He was so eager to see Mirabel and 

Tony that he had the fare, including Hope's dollar, ready in his hand and was out of the cab the instant the attendant opened the door. 

Going up in the elevator the elevator boy said, glancing sidewise at his passenger, as he brought the car to a stop at the correct floor: "You're kind o' late, Mr. Newbold. Your party's begun." 

That damn' party! Martin pulled out his watch. Of course he was late. Well, perhaps Mirabel would let him off dressing. It would be so easy to explain that he was just that minute off the train.

He opened the door with his key. Mirabel must have been on the watch for him. She came into the foyer with a rush, and once again a realization of her beauty swept over him, as she stood against the background of a great Léon Jallot lacquer, a modernistic design of brown and gold with long rays of metallic light patterning it, and strange fish swimming out of its depths.

She was tall and still slender almost to fragility, although a woman would have foreseen the day when, becoming lazier with age, she would put on flesh. Her hair, which was the first thing everyone remarked on, was ash blonde without a tinge of gold, so colorless that in some lights it appeared to be silvered. She had a great quantity of it, which this night was wreathed around her head and braided with pearls. Her nose was short, her mouth full and petulant; her eyes, red-brown, the exact shade of fine old sherry, always drew comment from strangers, and if her hair had not been of a color impossible 47

FOOL'S GOLD 

for art to achieve she would doubtless have been accused of dyeing it, so unusual was the contrast. 

Nature, which otherwise had been profligate of favors to her, had not endowed her with black lashes and brows. That detail she took care of herself and with such skill that even Martin did not know that they were tinted artificially. 

She was wearing a very striking gown, one he had never seen before, and she was plainly excited. She did not even pause long enough to greet him. "I thought you weren't going to get here!" she exclaimed breathlessly. "Hurry and dress. Your things are all ready in Tony's room.' "That's a shame. I'll be sure to wake the kid- 

"He's not there. I sent him over to the Maraseks'- Her tone was impatient, and she gave him a little push toward the staircase instead of the kiss he had anticipated. Standing at the foot of the stair, he still hesitated for an instant. "Mirabel, I think you're imposing on those people” 

His wife snapped him up at once. "Nonsense, Mart. Don't waste time now finding fault with me. The Maraseks are paid in full. I asked them to this party and, unless they're stupider than I think, they know they'd have had no smallest chance of an invitation except to cancel an obligation. I consider I've treated them handsomely. 

They'll brag of it for months. Oh, Mart, you'll never guess who's here-the duc de Versac!" She swept away from him, an arresting and alluring figure, looking back over her polished shoulder as if to say: "Follow me." "Hurry along, and for goodness' sake don't putter," were however the words she threw to him.

Plainly there was no help for it. He must dress and play the host, no matter how utterly fagged he was. The duc de 

Versac? Now where had he heard that name before? He could not remember. He ascended the short flight of steps still pondering the point.

On the balcony he had to press sidewise past the men and instruments of a small orchestra. Glancing in at his own door,

FOOL'S GOLD 

49 the flat face of a strange maid amid a rich welter of furs and brocades drew his eyes. He did not need to be told that this was now the ladies' dressing room. A puff of perfumed air met him and he hurried by with an irrational sense of annoyance. He knew that Mirabel prided herself upon a plan which put the men in her room, the women in his, but it was curiously distasteful to him despite her explanation that it preserved their privacy as no other arrangement could. "If the women were in my bedchamber, they'd poke into everything just out of curiosity," she had pointed out. "Your collars and neckties won't interest them at all."

In Tony's room he missed the child and, although his evening clothes were laid ready with meticulous care, his irritation grew. This was the hell of a home-coming. He'd said when they took this duplex studio apartment that, big as it was, there was no place in it for a kid to sleep in peace. The bedrooms opened off the balcony and everything that went on in the studio was audible.

Mirabel explained that they gave so few parties Tony would seldom be disturbed. This explanation meeting with unexpected opposition, she had grown angry and exclaimed that, since she was willing to be deprived of a guest room because of her young brother, she thought Mart ought to be satisfied.

Really he acted as if he took more interest in the child than she did. Moreover she had looked everywhere in every possible locality in New York. She had quite worn herself out hunting for an apartment. There was no other studio living room to match this. If he didn't believe it, she hoped he would take the matter out of her hands and find a better place. And at the same price! 

That last was a good argument. The rent was surprisingly low, a consideration that could not be ignored. Mart had made a final effort, suggesting that Tony be allotted one of the servants' rooms in the rear, where he would be both comfortable and quiet. 

Knowing her battle won, Mirabel had raised haughty brows. "My little brother that far away from me? No, thank you.

FOOL'S GOLD 

Not while he is so young. I'm amazed that you suggest it. 

And where would Julia sleep?" "She could have Tony's room," he said weakly, well aware that his wife would never consent to install a servant in a master's room.

And of course she had had her way. And of course it was too noisy for Tony. The little chap wasn't particularly husky, and Mirabel's parties, now that she had a suitable place to entertain, had grown more frequent. Hang it all, this was the fourth-no, the fifth-time in a month that Tony had been bundled out and parked with the young Maraseks; while there was no counting the times that "quiet" little bridge parties had deprived him of hours of rest. And he came back from a night of ghost stories and pillow fights with the Marasek roughnecks looking like a little ghost himself. 

Above everything Martin hated to fuss with Mirabel, yet this couldn't go on. The boy was too young and too delicate to send to boarding-school. As he revolved plans for Tony in his mind, he mechanically removed the papers from the inner pocket of his discarded coat preparatory to placing them in safety and, touching them, he smiled.

No need to work himself up over nothing. If Mirabel agreed that this opportunity was as good as he rated it, was indeed too good to miss, they would be on the ocean in little more than a month.

In a happier frame of mind he shook off his overpowering sense of fatigue and went down the stair to the party. 

This was no different from Mirabel's other entertainments save that it was more elaborate, in consideration probably of the distinguished guest.

Mirabel was dancing with him as her husband entered the studio, and at once Martin placed the man. Too much like a dancing master for his taste, was his inward comment as he greeted guests here and there, although after all it was a feather in Mirabel's cap to have captured the foreigner for this small affair. Older and more experienced hostesses than she had intrigued for his presence at their entertainments.

FOOL'S GOLD 

51 Martin himself felt no elation. Women were different. He could see from Mirabel's sparkling glances that her cup of joy was full, and he was pleased that she was pleased. It undoubtedly was a social triumph for her. When the two stopped dancing he made his way to where they were standing, never suspecting that his position as vice president of the Surety Bank and Trust Company had been the lure to attract and capture this particular prize rather than his wife's wit and beauty. 

To Martin's surprise, he found himself liking the duc very much. The Frenchman was apparently between thirty-five and forty years of age. Not handsome; his narrow face, made to appear longer by hair retreating from his temples, was lighted by peculiarly straight glancing eyes. This foreigner was sure of himself, of his position and his purpose, and was quite evidently a person of the widest experience and culture. Most surprising of all, Mirabel's capture was a business man, engaged actively in the steel industry and in America on a business trip. His English was as perfect as his manners, and his savoir faire equaled either.

The two men got on famously together, and in the interest of his own new enterprise Martin found himself acquiring much useful information, while Mirabel hovered near, at first pleased, later annoyed that her husband should monopolize her most distinguished guest; a fact which De Versac quickly sensed and hastened to set right. He was not one to underrate the dangers that might arise from a woman's ill will. "Ah, madame," he said, "Mr. Newbold has the constructive mind that at present seems to be so sadly lacking in Europe. 

Why do you not try to convince him of the opportunity there for such men as he? Here he has many rivals. There, none.

And in Paris you could establish a salon that would help to lift us all from the rut into which we have fallen in our relaxation after the efforts of the war." His bold regard softened as it met hers, to assure her that it was for her sake he was courting Martin's favor; and at once she was content. "I wish you could persuade him," she returned, casting a meek glance toward her husband in what she conceived to be

FOOL'S GOLD 

the proper spirit of the Continental wife. "I spent two of the happiest years of my life in Paris when I was a schoolgirl and I should welcome a chance to go there, but Martin is so wedded to his business that I fear there's no hope of that. The only way I'll ever return is as the usual tourist, and I have no love for the rôle." She made a pretty little face indicative of her distaste, which the duc appreciated to the full, while Martin saw an unexpected opportunity and in this Frenchman a possible ally. "I'm not so sure you can't go there to live if you have a fancy to," he said, cloaking his eagerness with an air of indifference. "I had rather a flattering offer made to me today. I didn't accept because I didn't think you'd fancy moving to Paris-" "Martin!" Mirabel exclaimed, her voice rising hard and excited, “don't tell me you refused. I mean"--she softened her tone and addressed herself to the duc, "I couldn't bear to think my likes or dislikes would be determining factors to decide my husband against any project that was definitely to his advantage. You didn't refuse, did you, Mart?" “Oh, I suppose it's still open," Martin told her carelessly. "But it would be such a complete change, involving resigning my position at the bank and sailing almost at once, that I really hesitated to so much as mention it to you. Forget it, my dear. Forget it. It was a mistake to let any word of it slip out.' 

The Frenchman looked from the one to the other, a light of comprehension flickering across his face. So this was how the Great American Marriage worked out? He did not admire it. The man, young, handsome and evidently able, or he would not have reached his present position, but oh, so worn with worry, was obliged to subordinate his wishes and his interests to his wife's caprice. She was a striking looking woman to be sure, and of a chic unsurpassed, but was she practical? 

One could easily find that out. "This is an opportunity, you say? Yet already you are in a commanding position, is it not so?"

FOOL'S GOLD 

53 

Martin smiled at the thought. "I'm in a good position for so young a man. I sit at council tables with the chiefs and medicine men and am listened to if I speak. I'm a rather superior hireling, I suppose. In the new job I'm offered I'd have a much larger salary-but that's nothing; I'd need it to support the necessary establishment. The thing that intrigues me, and I beg you not to laugh, is the power that would be mine. I would be the directing brain. No one would dispute or even question my decisions, and the success, which in my judgment is certain, would be mine alone." The fatigue had left his face and bearing. Flushed with the thought of the triumphs he foresaw, Martin was a new man and his voice rang out convincingly. 

The Frenchman, a sentimentalist where his own relations with women were not concerned, regarded him meditatively. 

In the future it would appear strange to him that at first it was altruistically, for Martin's sake alone, that he counselled the move to Paris. "Can you hesitate, chère madame," he asked, and his eyes captured and held Mirabel's regard. "You see before you a prisoner eager to be freed from his shackles. And for yourself what new worlds to conquer! Already I can picture you with Paris at your feet. Your clothes-I do not mean that you are not wonderful as you are, but your style is your own, everything must be designed for you and for you only. A true artist would make you unique. I shall rejoice if you will permit me to escort you when you go to choose your couturière. 

Your type-ah, that will be novel to my compatriots. You will be a sensation in the ateliers. Who dresses you will reap a fortune. Hold your husband to his new undertaking, madame. Give it your sanction without delay, and I shall be your witness. I shall feel like a great impresario with such a débutante to sponsor. It will be a consolation and will help me to forget that I am only a humble ironmaster.'

How could you think for a moment that I would hamper you in any way?" Her tone was gently reproachful and at once 

Martin blamed himself that he had so misjudged her. 

This reaction was characteristic of his relations with his wife. 

Whenever he reached the border line beyond which lay disillusionment, he drew back, unwilling to acknowledge that what he had glimpsed was other than a figment of his own imagination.

CHAPTER VII 

THE gayest and latest of parties must come to an end. This one finished about four A.M. with the serving of popovers no bigger than a lady apple, and tiny sausages or Malagueña eggs. It had been a success, and Mirabel was still flushed with triumph when Martin, pajama-clad, entered her room to say good night. "Wasn't it all too wonderful?" she demanded. "Sit down and let's talk it over." "It was great," Martin assured her, so tired he could think of nothing more eloquent to say. "I'm off to bed. I'm all in-" "Mart!" Mirabel stormed at him indignantly. "You may have no interest in my entertainments, but you can't mean to leave me without some information about this new position you say you're offered.” "That's just what I must do," he answered, positive with her for once. "It will take hours to let you into the ins and outs of it. I'm dog-tired and I've an accumulation of work to face tomorrow with almost no sleep to brace me for it. I simply can't explain all that now." "And you expect me to allow you to go into it blind, so to speak? You think I'll consent to your throwing up what you have here without more of an idea of what I may anticipate? 

Not likely!" Mirabel regarded him suspiciously, an immediate doubt of his judgment and a belief that he planned to hide something from her forming in her mind. "An American woman in Paris needs literally millions at her back to get her anywhere. I'm not going over there to spend my days saying, 'I can't afford this!' 'I can't afford that!' and you may as well know it before we start." "My dear" Martin interrupted, "you have only to say the 55

FOOL'S GOLD 

word and I'll turn the whole affair down the first thing in the morning; but whether you do or don't, I've got to have some rest." 

He kissed her lightly on the forehead and went into his own room, shutting the door behind him. His head had hardly touched the pillow before he was as deep in sleep as if he had been drugged.

On the other side of the door Mirabel picked up her gown, placed it on its padded and perfumed hanger and hung it in her closet, then while she finished her preparations for bed she had ample time to consider the situation that faced her.

The fact was that residence abroad presented many attractive and glittering facets, among which her acquaintance with the duc de Versac and his promise to arrange that she met some of the preeminently "right people" at once, hinted at possibilities that otherwise might be beyond her reach. As she wiped off the toilette cream she had used, she began to fear that her husband's lack of enthusiasm over her recent entertainment had annoyed her, aware as she was of the unnecessay expense she had just indulged in, until she had been unwisely abrupt in her dismissal of his new project. What if he took it as final? What was it he had said? would turn the whole thing down in the morning? She could not be sure she remembered accurately, and the point worried her until for a moment she contemplated the extreme measure of rising in time to breakfast with Mart. Only the discomfort of such a change in her luxurious routine discouraged the thought. 

That he "He acts as if a trip to Philadelphia were an endurance test," she said to herself pettishly. "There's no earthly reason he can't talk to me right now-and he's going to do it." 

She opened the door into Mart's room and switched on the lights.

Many women, women less "good" perhaps than Mirabel, would have been disarmed by the utter abandon of the figure of the man lying on the bed, who had been so spent that he had not even drawn up the covers before he fell asleep. 

Not so Mirabel. She crossed the room hastily and shut the

FOOL'S GOLD 

57 window with a crash. Still he slept on. She had hoped the noise would wake him. The room was too cold for comfort and she clattered into her own quarters, her mules clicking viciously where the parquet was bare, seized a warm bathrobe and wrapped it around her before she returned, shutting the connecting door behind her with a slam. 

This time Martin sat up, startled. Mirabel was poised on the foot of his bed, her feet tucked up under her. “Hello,” he said, only half awake; "what's wrong? 

"Nothing." Mirabel was bent on presenting an appearance of extreme amiability. "I just waited until you had had a nice nap, then I came in to hear all about this marvellous chance that's been offered you. I'm awfully glad for your sake.

In a bank promotion is so slow. You have had to work so hard, you poor dear, with so little prospect of further advancement, or even appreciation of your utter devotion to your duties. 

Of all masters such an institution is the most impersonal as well as the most demanding. I often say you are married to it while I rank only as your first concubine."

Usually he would have been touched by Mirabel's solicitude for him. Martin however had glanced at his wrist watch, he had even held it to his ear, thinking he must have forgotten to wind it. His "nice nap" had lasted for little more than ten minutes, and this hardened his heart. Hang it all, she must have seen that he was dead to the world. He had hardly slept while he was in Philadelphia, spending his nights working over figures and tabulations intended to convince his uncle, and he felt indignant that he had not been allowed now to rest undisturbed.

Abruptly he made up his mind to tell Mirabel nothing of his new enterprise. Previously he had always confided all the details of his affairs to her. When they were first married she had listened in rapt and wide-eyed admiration. Of late her interest had waned until, when he stopped to think of it, he realized that business bored her. Her concern was with the results, the dollars and cents, and those she should be told of now but nothing more!

FOOL'S GOLD 

"All you will care to know is that this is a very far-reaching investment proposition which Uncle Jim and Mr. Shober are prepared to back to the limit," he said. "My salary, to begin with, will be doubled-and money still goes further in Paris than here. Then I am also to receive a percentage of all profits, a percentage which increases on a sliding scale as the business progresses. That's all that really need be said. Do you wish me to turn it down tomorrow?" "Mart!" cried Mirabel, her eyes shining, her face prettily animated, "you couldn't believe I'd be such a pig as to demand that sacrifice? My whole thought is for what will be best for you and naturally I want you to accept." Then thriftily supplying herself with an opportunity to complain later: "It will be hard to give up this dear old apartment" (Mart reminded himself that they had lived there less than two years)-"and all our friends in New York; but I could never, never forgive myself if I laid a straw in the way of your advancement. I have always stood ready to further your interests at any cost to myself. Nothing would tempt me to be a drag on you.”

She paused, and as Martin said nothing, went on again: "Now, darling, you really need some sleep. Do try to relax." Gracefully she slid from the bed and moved toward the door in a leisurely way. "There, Mart dear, is where you make your mistake. You are too tense. Mrs. Thorpe, the wonderful psychoanalyst who charges such fabulous sums, says that is the real secret of life. 'Learn to relax,' she told me; 'fill your mind with pleasant thoughts Twice our present income, you said?" "Yes," Mart assured her curtly, "and I'll have to work twice as hard to earn it, if that's your idea of a pleasant thought." 

Mirabel switched off the light before she said anything more. She considered it selfish of Martin to mention such details. "Good night, old precious," she opened the door and stood on the threshold, a seductive figure silhouetted against the soft illumination of her own room. "After all, why should you get

FOOL'S GOLD 

59 up in the morning? You just stay in bed and have a good rest. I'll telephone your secretary when I've had my breakfast and say you are so worn out from overwork that I could not permit you to be called. You're going to resign, so it doesn't matter what they think of you at the old bank." "Good God!" exclaimed Martin. "Have women no sense of honor or obligation? It's the very fact that I'm going to leave them that makes it imperative for me to give the bank now the best service that I'm capable of giving. I'll be on the job at the usual time in the morning, if you'll only let me get forty winks before then." "That's exactly what I'm urging you to take," Mirabel declared, highly offended. "Only I know you better than you know yourself and I'm sure you would never be so irritable with me if you were not overfatigued. It isn't going to be any help to the bank, or to your new job either, if you break down from overwork. Mrs. Thorpe, in her very first interview, always stresses the importance-" 

Martin groaned. Would she never go and let him have some sleep? He sat up on the edge of his bed, slipped his feet into his slippers, crossed the room to the window Mirabel had closed, throwing it wide open; instantly a stream of icy air struck her where she stood in the doorway. "Mart!" she exclaimed indignantly, "do you want to give me pneumonia?" The door between them closed emphatically. Mart lowered the window half way and got into bed chuckling.

Once there however, the sleep he so sorely needed eluded him. He found himself vaguely unhappy over Mirabel's attitude. He could not escape the thought that her greatest interest had lain in the increased amount of money he would earn. 

She had not so much as asked if the task would be congenial. 

Moreover her suggestion about the bank rubbed him the wrong way, although it was hard to blame her for wanting to give him a chance to rest. Certainly he needed it, yet now sleep would not come. His mind, stirred to activity once more, went over the events of the last few days until every step he had

FOOL'S GOLD 

taken toward success lay clear before him. He could tell the exact moment when Uncle Jim had begun to waver in his opposition to the scheme; he could recall how he had looked when he had gone to the telephone and called Mr. Shober into the conference. And Mr. Shober had taken a deal of convincing too; but at last he knew he had them both hooked and, once they had decided to back him, how generous they had been both with their confidence and their money. With pleasure he recalled the picture of that mellow, book-lined room, a suitable setting for the two older men. 

He had looked forward to telling Mirabel all this, to making her see what a victory over inertia, prejudice and scepticism he had won; he had hoped she would rejoice, not for the sake of the additional income but because of the opportunity it offered him. And then he had flown off the handle and told her nothing. Perhaps she was right in accusing him of being crusty. And plainly he was overtired or he wouldn't have expected a girl to share a man's ideas of honor.

Of a sudden, this brought before him the face of his traveling companion, and with this vision he recalled the exact words his uncle had spoken about her: "More honorable than most men. That had been his eulogium and, remembering her austere young face and her effort to hide the pain she was suffering, Martin now believed the words. 

"By Jove, she was plucky. I didn't realize how bad it must have been until after she'd fainted. And she had the nerve to keep up till she'd given me the money for the cab and shut the door. Poor kid, I'd like to ask how she is in the morning and, since I don't know so much as her name, I don't see how I'm going to do it."

So it was with thoughts of Hope in his mind that he at last drifted into a troubled sleep.

CHAPTER VIII 

THE days that followed succeeded each other with bewildering rapidity.

When at last Mart was freed from his duties at the bank, where his resignation had been accepted with genuine reluctance and regret, he was able to devote more time to his preparations for his great venture; yet his progress was disappointingly slow.

Mirabel was usually at some pains to hide the fact that she was extremely capable, since it was her pet theory that those who showed themselves competent always found work loaded upon their shoulders; and she was averse to exerting herself except for her own benefit. But the manifest advantages of the projected move became more apparent to her as her friendship with the duc de Versac progressed and spurred her to complete all the essential rearrangements with a businesslike dispatch that earned Mart's admiration. She had sublet their apartment, disposed of the furniture, and was ready for the move, having even secured accommodations on the boat that was to take the Frenchman back to his own country, before her husband had effected the combinations and made the connections necessary to the success of his project. 

In taking this last step Mirabel had exceeded her authority, and she felt just a trifle doubtful as to how Mart would receive the news of what she had done as she moved forward to greet him one evening after he had divested himself of his hat and coat in the foyer. "I'm expecting praise, dearest," she announced, smiling at him ingratiatingly. "I know you'll be proud of me. Tony and I are all ready to be off and I've made our reservations for the Barataria sailing the twenty-fifth-" "Now why did you do that without consulting me? I can't 61

FOOL'S GOLD 

possibly be ready so soon," Mart cut in curtly. 

Mirabel's eyebrows went up, yet she met this opposition with extreme good nature. "Of course you can, you dear old slow poke," she said indulgently; "there's nothing you can't do if you make up your mind to it. And you must remember that your salary doesn't begin until we reach Paris, while we'll be spending money like water after the Friedmans take possession here." "I understood that they were in no hurry to move in. You certainly said that was a factor in consummating the deal with them." Mart spoke wearily. It seemed to him that for all her executive ability, Mirabel's arrangements had a bewilderingly kaleidoscopic quality, unwelcome changes occurring without warning. Friedman has decided the newest little Yid must be born here instead of at the hospital. I might trouble you with a host of petty details that I try to spare you; a fact you don't appear to appreciate."

Either qualification simple enough separately. Taken together, almost impossible to find." "Don't try," Mirabel counseled him lazily. "Everyone learns half a dozen languages in Europe. We'll pick someone up there who'll be just what you need and you'll save the expense of one passage across. It's only in America that people are content to speak no tongue but their own." Mirabel was wise enough to contest the matter no further for the moment. Instead she began to arm herself with arguments to explain why Tony and she should sail on the Barataria in case Martin really could not be ready in time. If she let

FOOL'S GOLD 

65 He saw his nephew's problem only too clearly and agreed that the situation would be dangerous, the whole success of the undertaking menaced, unless the right man could be found.

Martin, with what he characterized as a flash of business genius, had discovered an opening, a potentiality in international finance which no other had recognized for the opportunity it was. The field was theirs. Once they were established, it would be too late for anybody to dispute it with them. 

Meanwhile a traitor in his employ might easily be fatal to the enterprise, might indeed sell the idea out to anyone who would buy; and Mr. Bohlen was sure there would be many ready to seize upon such a chance. 

If Mart could only carry on alone—but that was impossible, the work would prove overwhelming. The position he had to offer was a wonderful opportunity for the right person, while the damage the wrong one might do was plainly incalculable.

CHAPTER IX 

HOPE stopped outside her apartment and opened her purse to find her key. It had slipped inside the lining, and she made a mental note to buy a new bag when she saw one that was not too dear. Her foot was out of the cast now. It had been a nasty sprain and toward evening was apt to bother her a little still; consequently she was glad to reach home. 

As she fitted the key in the keyhole, a door down the hall was flung open with a crash and a girl came rushing out, a striking looking girl with flaming red hair brushed severely back from a low forehead and flaring out behind beautifully formed ears. "Miss Willing!" she cried, "wait just a minute. I've a telegram for you." She pranced down the hall waving a yellow envelope. "The boy came with it just as I got back from Mr. 

Pirello's studio. I pose, you know. And you'll pardon me, but 

I've noticed how much you stick in your rooms. Very bad for the health, that is. So I said to myself: 'P'rhaps it's a bid from her boy friend to go out to dinner or a Talkie.' And it seemed a shame you should miss it; so I signed for the telegram, tellin' the boy I was your best chum, see, and expected you home any minute-" She paused, a little out of breath and embarrassed, and Hope smiled at her appreciatively. "You're very kind," she said. "It's probably nothing more exciting than business. I'm afraid I haven't any 'boy friend.' 9 99 "No boy friend?" the other girl stared at her in undisguised amazement. "No boy friend at all? And with your looks! Good gosh, woman, what do you do with your spare time?" 

At this Hope laughed outright, a laugh with a silvery chuckle underlying it that seemed to tell of secret sources of amusement deep within her. "I read and I study-and I go to the theater a lot." 68

FOOL'S GOLD 

69 "But don't you ever step out? Now see here"-this in recognition of the negative shake of Hope's head. "I'm Arethusa

Miranda, Thusa for short. Sounds like a Wop name but we're a genu-wine old family; my grandfather was naturalized

American, and I'm as respectable as sin, even if I do pose for a living. You come out with me now and then. I'll show you a good time and you won't hear a word a real lady could rightly complain of.” "Thank you." Hope recognized the kind intention and unaffected friendliness. "I wish I could, but I've simply got to keep up my studies." She unlocked her door. "And thank you so much for delivering the telegram— 

"Aren't you going to open it?" Arethusa inquired, roundeyed. "Goodness me, when I get a telegram I'm not expecting, I'm all in a twitter trying to guess who's died on me."

Her curiosity was so evident and so innocent that Hope succumbed. "Do come in then while I read it," she said. "I have a bad ankle and can't stand long. Besides, I like to take my hat and coat off and wash my face before I bother with letters and such things."

Nothing loth, the other girl followed her in, shutting the door behind her and looking around with undisguised interest. "I'll say it's neat," was her comment.

Hope, with a word of apology, went on into her bedroom. 

When she returned Arethusa was still going from one object to another with childlike inquisitiveness. "I can see you're terribly highbrow," she announced, "such a lot of books! I wouldn't be surprised if you told me you'd read 'em all. Now do open your telegram." And she added with great generosity, "you don't have to tell me what's in it if you don't want to.' 

Hope laughed aloud and tore the end off the envelope. The message was brief.

She handed it to Arethusa without comment.

I'm used to an early dinner and if I have to be fashionable and wait till eight o'clock I'll be famished beyond the bounds of politeness. You'll take a cup with me, won't you?"

FOOL'S GOLD 

71 the position in Troy which she had resigned to try her fortunes in New York. If she noted that her hostess was not equally expansive, she doubtless set it down to the all-explanatory fact that she was from Philadelphia.

"You've got a job now, didn't you tell me?" She hung the cups on their respective hooks while she talked. "Yes," Hope replied, "a good enough position, and well paid, but uninteresting. I'd rather take less money and see a future where I'd amount to something." "Well, listen." Arethusa, who plainly had something to say, for the first time displayed a certain diffidence. "You may think your job's uninteresting, but so is posing. You sit stuck up on a stand hour after hour and can't open your mouth even to yawn. 

If anyone says a word to you it's "Thusa, a little more to the left, please,' or 'Gosh, I wish your neck was longer.' Something sentimental and exciting like that. If it wasn't for the rests, you'd go sound asleep. I only took it up because I couldn't get any other work to do and artists are goofy over red hair. I'd be tickled pink to find a steady job among real men, or women either for that matter. Artists ain't people, and that's a fact. They haven't any romance, wishing a neck like a giraffe on a poor girl!"

Despite her rambling, Hope saw where this was leading and said nothing. She was not ready to sponsor this girl, whom nevertheless she was inclined to like, on the strength of an hour's acquaintance. enough to leave the situation I mentioned to you in Troy because I took a notion that I could better myself and see more of life at the same time here in New York. The collar factory folks thought a lot of me and I've an A-1 reference from 'em, which they'll back up if necessary."

Arethusa inquired, now feeling herself established as an old friend on a footing of perfect good will. "We're about the same size. I'll be glad to lend you something if you haven't a full evening dress. You ought to look swell if you're going to the Ritz. That's a very tony place, you know." "Oh, I've an evening dress or two. I don't go out here, but when I visit in Philadelphia I need some clothes," Hope explained, "and I almost always wear black.” "It's kind of old for a young girl," Arethusa said disparagingly. "Don't you want just to look at my new magenta and heliotrope? It's a real French model. I bought it secondhand through one of the tony magazines. It belonged to a society queen, the kind that can't wear the same gown more'n once or twice without being disqualified. I had it cleaned and it's as good as new. You'd be a picture in it." "I think I'd better stick to black," Hope hastened to tell her, adding explanatorily: "You see, I've never really gone out of mourning except when I was a bridesmaid for a cousin, and then you've no idea how queer I felt.” "Well, let's see what you're planning to wear. Maybe it won't be so bad." 

It was quite obvious that Hope was not going to be rid of Miss Miranda until she left for the Ritz, so she steeled herself to accept the girl's ministrations. "After all, it's no worse than breaking in a new lady's maid," she assured herself, "and I'm positive Eugénie hadn't as good a heart or as generous impulses as this red-head, so why should I mind having her around if it gives her pleasure?" 

Meanwhile Arethusa chattered on:

FOOL'S GOLD 

73 "You were dead sensible not to bob, only I should think you'd shorten your hair and have it thinned; more like mine, see? It makes it so much easier to buy hats. Don't you use any make-up? Not any at all? Honest, I'd feel naked without it, as if I'd left some of my clothes behind in mistake, the way you do in a bad dream. Those long earrings look as good as if they were real. They're grand on you. They're better even than some I got at Nat's for four ninety-five, and that was a half-price sale."

Hope refrained from explaining that her ornaments were inherited, and at last stood ready. When she brought forth a black and silver wrap with a massive collar of silver fox she caught Arethusa looking at her with a glint of doubt in her expressive eyes. To her mind such a wrap went with orchids, large square-cut diamonds and emeralds, an apartment rent free and guilty luxury. "That ain't phony, at any rate?" the girl spoke questioningly. "No, it isn't," Hope agreed. "It was my Christmas present from a rich cousin. Caroline Page would be quite ready to have me do nothing for the rest of my life and would dress me as if I were a millionairess. I have a terrible time convincing her that if I accepted half the finery she tries to shower on me my position as a poor working girl would be open to suspicion. I confess I'm human now and then. I couldn't resist this wrap." She folded it luxuriously around her and regarded it in an utterly inadequate mirror. "I should think not!" Arethusa exclaimed, passing her hand reverently over the silky skins. "You're just the very image of a model I saw at a swell fashion show at Macy's. 

Gee, I never thought I'd know anybody with a wrap like that! I hate to think it has to go in a taxi. I tell you one thing, I'm going down first to call you a cab and make sure you get a clean one.”

FOOL'S GOLD 

wrap, was allowed to enter, the wrap's owner leant back in a corner and tried to guess what sort of employment was to be proposed to her. If Mr. Bohlen had wanted her in his own office it would have been mentioned earlier. Indeed he had spoken of it on the train, but jestingly and not as one giving the suggestion serious thought. Yet it was because of the dissatisfaction she had expressed at that casual meeting that he had sent for her now. And she would not have to wait much longer for the answer to this puzzle. With a screech of his brakes, the chauffeur drew up at the side entrance of the Ritz, the lights glinted on the silver of her wrap as the door of the cab was thrown open, mechanically she handed the man his fare, with a generous tip added, and descended. The night was clear and cold, the pavements showed dry white in the electric lights. She lingered for an instant, drawing in long breaths of the crisp air, as if preparing herself for an ordeal. 

Then she shrugged her shoulders, impatient of her nerves. 

What was there to be afraid of? Mr. Bohlen was an old friend.

She ran up the few steps and through the revolving door as if to escape a presentiment that had descended like a weight upon her spirits a warning that this was the end of old habits and ways of living and the beginning of something new and as yet unfathomed.

Hardly had she crossed the threshold when above the heads of the other loiterers she saw Mr. Bohlen's handsome profile and silvery hair, and beside him another man equally tall, to her eyes equally handsome and straight-glancing, but younger.

Both were so masculine in type as to suggest the Nineteenth 

Century rather than the Twentieth. "I felt it in my bones that he would be here," Hope said to herself, and was angry that she could not control the color that flooded to her face and made her more than usually striking looking.

Mr. Bohlen had received his impression of her when grief had left her pallid and had fixed her face in a protective mask. "Bless my soul, the child's a beauty," he thought. "I won-

FOOL'S GOLD 

75 der if I was a fool to suggest this?"

It was too late to back out. 

He hastened forward to greet his young guest with all the ceremony of old-world manners, and to introduce his nephew.

CHAPTER X 

CONSIDERED gastronomically or conversationally Mr. Bohlen's dinner party was an unqualified success. 

As a business conference it left much to be desired, since business was never mentioned.

Hope was quite happy with a new and heady happiness that she did not attempt to analyze. Had she tried to do so, she would probably have said it was the result of a delicious dinner and the consciousness that she was looking her best, as many admiring glances from other diners attested.

Had she needed further confirmation of this last fact, she would have found it in the advent of Larry Toland. An intimate friend of the Pages, he had never, seemingly, cast a thought in her direction until his eyes met hers as he stood that night in the doorway of the Ritz dining room. Then, leaving the men who accompanied him without so much as a word of apology, as if drawn by a magnet he made a bee line for Hope and seized her hand demonstratively. "Who'd have thought of seeing you here!" he cried. "'Do, Mr. Bohlen. 'Do, Newbold. You're looking ripping, Hope. 

Wasn't sure it was you at first. What about you and me having a canter together tomorrow? Lunch with me at 

Pierre's and we'll fix it up then." 

Hope shook her head. "Impossible," she told him. "You forget I'm a working woman. My time's not my own." "You're here," Toland hinted. "Cinderella," she smiled. "Tomorrow I go back to my rags.

He was hard hit and under such circumstances never reluctant to let everybody know it. Besides, what business had Newbold butting in! He had a wife of his own, a damn' good looker, at that. His eyes followed Mr. Toland's very well tailored back with great disfavor.

Hope fell in with this proposal at once. "I was just going to suggest that we go there. My rooms are tiny but I have them to myself—at least I had. I may have to move"; and in the taxi, she amused them by an account of the arrival of Mr. Bohlen's telegram and Arethusa's reactions thereto.

"I never felt so helpless as I did outside your door the night 

I brought you home and heard you fall when you had shut it between us, I didn't so much as know your name. I didn't dare to ask for assistance here in the building under the circumstances. And when I called up your friends I was taken for a taxi driver! You'll never believe how insulted I felt after I'd trotted out all my swellest inflections in order not to disgrace you."

Fumbling with her key, Hope laughed back at him over her shoulder, then threw open the door and found the light switch with accustomed fingers. "I've not thanked you for all you did for me that night, when I could see that you were tired to the verge of exhaustion. I was grateful, and so ashamed to have fainted. I never knew I was such a poor sport. I ought to have managed to hold out for a minute longer. That was certainly the last straw of imposition on your kindness." She slipped out of her wrap and disappeared into her own chamber, while the men removed their overcoats.

The living room was not so small as she had led them to fancy. The walls were sand-color, the furniture dark walnut upholstered in brown. There were a few cushions of burnt orange and brown, widely patterned. Except for books, the only relic of her old life was the small, charmingly sympathetic sketch, done on tinted paper in colored chalks, of a delicate looking, fine featured man. "Her father," Mr. Bohlen supplied the superfluous information. "Must have been done some years before he died though. 

He looked older than that.”

Martin decided the room was livable-at first sight rather a man's room than a girl's, the only feminine touch being a few flowers carelessly stuck, not formally arranged, in a Syrian water pitcher of copper and brass on which a worn patine of silver plating bestowed an elusive charm of color.

The chairs were all comfortable and capacious. When 

Hope returned, she seated herself opposite Martin, her clear eyes seeking his, expectant and childlike.

79 "And now," said he, rapping on the table with a paper knife, "the meeting will please come to order," and looked to his uncle to begin. "Go ahead, Mart," Mr. Bohlen suggested. "You know more about your own business than I do. Tell Hope exactly what your plans are. You can trust her to keep them to herself if she doesn't see her way clear to helping you." He was still feeling uneasy that he had brought these two together and found himself hoping she would decide against the position to be offered to her. Mirabel, save that she was too thin, in appearance followed almost exactly the pattern he would have set had he been asked to describe his ideal woman; but he disliked her so heartily that he could never quite satisfy himself that Martin had not, in secret, something of the same repulsion. 

In which case constant association with a girl of an opposite type and as brilliant as he now recognized Hope to be, might prove dangerous for his favorite nephew. Manlike, he never gave a thought to Hope's possible reactions.

So once again Martin told the story of his project, this time to a vastly different audience. Across the table from him sat the girl, her eyes never leaving his face, her nods of instant comprehension, her kindling glance, the inspiration for an eloquence he perhaps had never shown before. FOOL'S GOLD 

"I've put the cart before the horse, as usual," she said. "That is what I could do if you found me suited to your needs.

I'm terribly afraid I might fail you in a pinch."

Mr. Bohlen was relieved. Absolutely she was too attractive. 

He would rather lose his money than see Mart drawn to make one of the members of the familiar triangle. He decided to put in a word to back the girl's expressed view. "If Hope feels herself unequal to the job, she's doubtless right!" he exclaimed in a voice overly loud and jovial. "Where you are unsure of yourself, you are certain to make a host of mistakes. Nothing could be easier. Anyone I employ will have moments of doubt, because the real responsibility must always rest with me.

Before they parted Hope had definitely accepted the position Martin Newbold offered, had promised to give her present employers due notice on the morrow and to be ready to sail on the twenty-fifth unless notified of a change of sailing date.

Her passport only needed renewal, so she was spared that worry, and Martin promised to send her labels for her luggage.

The fact that she was an accustomed traveler made everything easier. 

When the men left her, Mirabel's name remained unuttered, which is not so strange as at first blush might appear.

Mr. Bohlen's dislike of his niece by marriage explained the fact that he never spoke of her if he could reasonably avoid it. 

To Martin the thought had never occurred. Mirabel had nothing to do with his business life and he supposed his exact status known to Hope. He had been married for years, was living with his wife, had always been a devoted husband. 

Moreover this girl had agreed without demur to crossing the ocean with him, a thing which she assuredly would not have done had she imagined him to be a bachelor sailing alone.

FOOL'S GOLD 

After they had gone Hope stood in a happy daze. 

This was exactly the sort of position she had dreamed of. 

She shut her eyes and saw again Martin's face, concentrated, interested, yet with a quizzical little smile at his own overenthusiasms.

She wondered if he meant to sail on the boat with her. She assumed not, since Mr. Bohlen, Philadelphian to his backbone, would in that case have suggested asking some lady among their fellow passengers to play propriety.

For herself she had no doubts. It was all right for her to go anywhere in the wide world if Martin Newbold needed her. "He said I was treasure-trove," she murmured to herself.

CHAPTER XI 

A KNOCK at the door, a double rap, loud and determined, brought a smile to her lips as she opened it. Martin Newbold was so deep in her thoughts that it was his face Hope expected to see, fancying something had been forgotten.

Instead, swathed in an amazing pea-green kimono beneath which orange pyjamas peeped forth coyly, Miss Miranda stood before her. "May I come in?" the girl asked. "I bet you had a grand time. I heard you when you came back. I turned out my light and sat with the door open so I could see when they left. 

I just couldn't wait till morning to hear if you got it." "Got it?" Hope asked vaguely. "Got what?" "The job, of course!" Arethusa's tone was one of amazement. Wasn't the job supposed to be the reason for this swell dinner party? "Oh, that!" Hope filled the pause mechanically, her head still in the clouds. "Yes, I took the position." "When are you going to tell your boss you're leaving?"

The other girl's interest, entirely practical, concentrated at once on the next step, one of vital importance to her; and of a sudden Hope saw that she might be made serviceable. She herself was eager to be free of Schoonmaker's, but had felt that she could not, in fairness, give them less than two weeks in which to fill her place. If then, they would engage Miss 

Miranda at once, a few days' instruction should fit her for her duties and enable Hope to leave without injustice or inconvenience to her employers. "I'll take you down with me in the morning," she suggested; "then you'll be sure to be the first to apply for the place."

Arethusa, uninvited, seated herself at the table, her chin 83

FOOL'S GOLD 

on her hands. Hope was glad she had selected the chair she had used rather than Martin's. "Let's think," said Arethusa. After a moment or two of concentration she looked over at Hope. "When do you want to quit?" she asked. "Not considerin' me, y'understand, just yourself." 99 "As soon as possible. As soon as they can replace me.

Hope told her briefly, her mind busy now with the thousand and one details essential to her leaving the country for a stay of indeterminate length. "I shall have a great deal to do before I sail." "Sail?" Arethusa picked out the word. "Where are you going? Say, this ain't a wedding trip, is it? I'm not blind, you know. I saw your old gentleman, but I saw the young one too." "It's not a wedding trip." Hope felt her color rising and turned away to conceal it, plumping up a pillow here and there and generally setting the room to rights while she talked. "I'm to go as confidential secretary to a new company with headquarters in Paris. I've lived abroad a good deal and I've kept up my languages.' 

"Some people have all the luck," said Arethusa admiringly and without a trace of envy. "Now I might sit up nights to study Chinese, but would I ever get a job in Hongkong? Not on your life. Yiddish is the only foreign language I'll ever hear, and in New York you can hardly call it foreign, can you?" 

Hope wondered if Arethusa never slept. Of a sudden she herself began to suffer from a reaction after the excitement of the evening; a lassitude crept over her, she wanted to be alone, to lie between the cool sheets of her narrow bed, to sleep if she could; if not to dream, waking, of her future and how valuable she meant to make herself to Martin Newbold. She was very, very happy. A happiness that struck her as excessive, but which she attributed to relief at the prospect of being lifted out of the dull level that life had become to her and which she had endured without complaint through the years

FOOL'S GOLD 

85 since her father's death. "I'll take you down with me in the morning," she repeated. "No!" Arethusa brought the word out explosively. "I know a trick worth two of that; but I'll tell you why not.

It ain't in human nature for your boss not to be mad when you give him notice, see? He'll want to fire you at once, yet he won't want to engage anybody you recommend, even if it would be convenient. Now I'll happen along casual, like a gift straight from heaven, about half an hour after you've said your piece. You don't know me and I don't know you, see? I live with my aunt in Harlem-and that's no lie; I'm just staying here in my friend Miss Montescue's flat to oblige while she's away. 

It'll be up to me to convince your boss that I can fill your shoes. If I can, he'll be tickled pink to let you know that he can get along without you." "Have it your own way," Hope said. "You're probably right. Ask for Mr. Rosen. And now I'm going to bed. 

I'm so tired I ache." "You poor kid!" Miss Miranda was all sympathy at once. "Can I help you undress? Is your bed made? Well, it would be, you're that neat. Sure there's nothing I can do? 

Good night then." The door shut behind her with a bang. 

Hope was really alone at last.

In a quarter of an hour she was ready for bed. For a moment, her light out, she stood looking from her window on a familiar view of rear elevations and neglected back yards. 

She would leave this place without regret. It had never been home to her, had never had the feeling of permanency. Rather it had been like the villas her father and she had occupied for a season now and then in Italy or the south of France. A pleasant enough perch on which to rest while deciding on the next flight. Home still meant for her Philadelphia, the dark old house on Spruce Street, now gone from her forever; but most of all the feeling of family, of a backing from those of her own blood. That she had not lost, even though she had lived in New York. Having done nothing to forfeit it, she had always known it was there for her to fall back on should

FOOL'S GOLD 

the need arise; only, except for Caroline's affection, it had not been warmly present in her life of late. 

What would the family think of this new venture? It was safe to predict that they would not approve, although Mr. 

Bohlen's part in arranging it would attest its respectability. 

A long hoot, followed by two short blasts from some big boat on the East River brought her to the affair of the moment She opened the window, mechanically looked to make sure the dust screen was secure, and pattered off to her bed. once more.

Once under the covers, she stretched herself until every muscle was taut, then relaxed voluptuously, much as a cat arches its back and extends its claws before turning around and curving itself into purring comfort. 

It was good to lie there and recall the events of the evening one by one. Larry Toland's sudden admiration had been embarrassing if not wholly displeasing. Perhaps he'd had too many cocktails. Martin Newbold had been funny about that. 

Clearly he had wanted to be rid of Larry, and that might go to strengthen the cocktail theory. He was the kind of man who would not permit a girl to be made conspicuous. She'd ask Caroline some time if Larry drank too much. Carol would probably return: "What is too much? Never heard of it before." All that crowd loved to fancy they were hardboiled. Oh, well! It didn't matter, anyway. But thinking of Caroline had taken her to Philadelphia again. There was no head of the family now as there had been in Great-aunt Thorne's day. She would have to explain her plans over and over again. To Cousin Maria Pepper, Caroline's mother, and to Cousin Louise Denckla. To Cousin Harriet Norris. To the various Shippens and a Biddle or two. To Aunt Susan Cadwallader-on no account must she be forgotten. She and 

Cousin Harriet Norris were both very touchy and jealous about anything affecting any of the younger generation, perhaps because neither had any children of her own. Of the lot there was none to whom it really mattered except Caroline 

Page, who was honestly fond of her. Yet the others would

FOOL'S GOLD 

87 have their feelings terribly outraged if they were not duly forewarned of her plans. That was the one drawback to having a family. "In reality it's sort of silly to bother to tell them," she thought. "They don't care, and neither do I. Not really. 

Nothing they could say would change my mind. Martin

Newbold needs me, which is more than anyone else in the world does. And working with him is going to be terribly interesting. Like a game of skill. We'll have to pit our brains against the best in Europe, and secretly at that. At first, at any rate, it will have to be secretly. When we've skimmed the cream and hired most of the cows for years to come" she chuckled at the innocent metaphor-"it won't matter if they find out what we've been doing. But we're awfully young to cope with them-at least I am. Martin has such wonderful insight that his age doesn't enter into it." 

She settled herself on her pillow and tried to sleep, but her mind was still too active; one vista after another opened before her and she traversed each with interest. 

She decided to engage a room at that nice pension where she had always stopped when en route to rejoin her father after school had closed. It was such a quiet, safe place. 

Madame Masson who kept it, had a black mustache; but she made one feel that even a full beard would be a womanly adornment if she wore it. Hope giggled. She had not been so light of heart for years, and the picture this thought presented to her mind was amusing.

Residence there would be entirely satisfactory to her family. 

Last year she had given the address to Cousin Harriet Norris. 

Always ready to economize, that lady had stayed there for weeks and pronounced it both highly respectable and comfortable. "Actually, it was so clean it might have been in Philadelphia!" She mimicked Cousin Harriet's amazed intonation, she being one of those loth to concede the virtue of cleanliness to anything alien.

Yes, that was an inspiration; the fact that she intended to live at madame Masson's would go far to reconcile them all

FOOL'S GOLD 

to her going to Paris. It might be tactful to ask Cousin Harriet's advice as to quarters there. She must remember to practice being politic, since it would be of importance in her new job. That was a good idea. She sat bolt upright to give it due consideration. She would begin with Philadelphia. She would try to propitiate every member of the family she was forced to interview, so that each and everyone would be prepared to back her in case of opposition. That was not very logical because if all were on her side, who would there be to oppose? Maybe she'd better practice being logical too. What she meant was that she ought to try to get everybody she could on her side, and the way to do that was to consult each one separately as if she were doubtful about accepting Martin's proffered position. She must point out that the advantages were so great that she could never expect to have another such opportunity to justify her choice of occupation and make herself a credit to the clan; but possibly she would be forced to refuse because, while the individual she was speaking to would be broad-minded enough to understand, others (and to each one she could name a special intimate enemy) might not.

Her schemes were amusing Hope too much. At this rate she would never go to sleep. She turned, smoothed her pillow and tried to settle herself quietly. Still picture after picture seemed to unroll before her eyes. She had a vision of the offices they would occupy in Paris. Her office and Martin's, communicating perhaps with a little reception room. These must all look very substantial and prosperous. Modern with out being New Art. 

She must not make too sure she would be equal to the responsibilities Mr. Newbold intended to shift to her shoulders. 

Suppose suppose she should fail? "I won't fail," Hope declared aloud. "No one need fail if they are ready to sacrifice everything else. You get what you want if you want it enough. The trouble with me has been that I've never wanted anything very much before. 

Besides, he won't let me fail. He said we'd learn-together." 

And on that magic word, of a sudden she fell asleep, not to wake until the sun shone in her east window.

CHAPTER XII 

BEFORE starting down town in the morning, Hope stopped at

Arethusa's apartment. It had occurred to her that appearances went a long way with a man like Rosen (late Rosenthal) and for the girl's sake, she was anxious that her new acquaintance should make a good impression.

She found her suitably turned out in a neat blue suit, well cut, well fitted and showing her fine figure to advantage, but busily engaged in fastening a huge yellow artificial chrysanthemum to her shoulder. "You look very nice without, so why the sunflower?" Hope asked. "I thought it would brighten me up," Thusa told her, standing off and admiring the effect in the looking-glass. "I don't want to give an imitation of a funeral procession." "With your hair, you don't need anything to brighten you up. I'd cut out the decoration. You're not trying for a place in the front row of the chorus.' "Well, if you say so-" Arethusa reluctantly abandoned the cherished ornament. "I had it, and it seemed a shame not to show it off." “Really you look handsomer without it," Hope assured her comfortingly. "I'm going now, you'd better come right along after me. 

Mr. Rosen's always early and it won't take me two minutes to break the news of his approaching bereavement."

That she was amply able to do just that was proved by the fact that when Hope returned about half an hour later with the various invoices, it was to find the girl installed at her own special desk. She smiled to herself as Mr. Rosen hailed her, a trace of embarrassment inducing a truculence he did not feel: "Ah, Miss Willing! I don't remember if you said when you planned to leave us." He did not look at her as he spoke but fumbled nervously with a mass of papers lying under his hand. 

Again Hope smiled. Plainly Miss Miranda's prophecy was to be fulfilled. "I didn't say. I judge it will be agreeable to you if I leave at once.' "You see it wasn't difficult to fill your place, and there ain't desks for two of you-" Rosen was a little man and inclined at times to spitefulness. "I might not have another opportunity to secure a young lady as competent as my good friend Mr. Silberstein writes me Miss Miranda is." "I quite understand," Hope said. "I'll get my hat and coat."

She went to her locker and returned ready for the street.

FOOL'S GOLD 

91 She paused an instant by Mr. Rosen's desk and he pushed toward her a voucher he had made out for two weeks' advance salary.

Hope looked down at it for an instant without touching it, then gently shook her head. "Thank you very much," she said. "I was paid in full last night." Then with a very pretty dignity she added: "And 

I want to thank you also for your unfailing courtesy while I was in your employ. Good-by."

She bowed without offering her hand and to his everlasting amazement Mr. Rosen found himself on his feet, opening the door for her, salaaming before her as if she were the firm's most cherished customer. 

"I'm sure you won't need us, Miss Willing," he walked at her side to the elevator and pushed the button for her, “but a reference now, or any time you would like to call on us- The elevator began to descend. "Anything we could do would be a pleas-sure, y' understand." "Thank you again," said Hope, and looked her last on

Mr. Rosen at the level of his feet, encased, as she was pleased to note, in patent leather shoes having pearl gray cloth uppers fastened with large and resplendent pearl buttons: “Almost as good as real!" she murmured to herself. "What did you say, miss?" the elevator boy asked the question anxiously. He had a lively admiration for Hope, who had never spoken to him before further than to say good morning or good night.

Meanwhile Mr. Rosen walked away from the elevator back to his private office mopping his brow with a large silk handkerchief elegantly bordered and monogrammed in lavender to match his tie and socks. He would gladly have acknowledged at any time that he was a snappy dresser. He could never explain even to himself what had impelled him to give Hope so triumphal an exit. "It wasn't because she didn't take the check," he assured himself. "I couldn't have been that glad about savin' eighty dollars of the firm's money. It was all of a sudden as if

FOOL'S GOLD 

7 she was Mrs. Vand'bilt or somebody." 

Hope roamed the streets rejoicing in her liberty. Everything entertained her as if she had never seen the pageant before. The eager faces, the shop windows, the traffic lights, the young policeman at the corner of Forty-second Street and Fifth Avenue, who, for their own good, tried to control the impatient crowds at the crossing. "Wait for the lights, wait for the lights!" he crooned. "You've got to learn sometime. You'd better learn now."

Then as two boys ducked under his outstretched arm and ran across the avenue, dodging oncoming cars skillfully, he caught Hope's amused expression and spoke as if to her: "The pity of it, the pity of it. They never get killed!" he said. 

She went on her way chuckling inwardly.

She had so much more free time ahead of her than she had expected to have before sailing that she began, like a child with an unforeseen holiday, to plan how best to spend it. First she would look over her wardrobe. She knew pretty well what she had and in what condition, and made up her mind to buy nothing except shoes before she sailed. She did so like 

American shoes, although Caroline had said they had improved abroad since she was last there. 

She must see the Weismullers. Herman would miss her, and she tried to think of ways to reconcile him to her departure. For one thing, she meant to place all her small investments in his hands. He should have a power of attorney to enable him to manage everything for her. He had a number of pupils now, and he had his music. She would promise to send him records from Europe. She knew how thrilled he would be at that prospect.

She had taken a three year lease of her apartment, which still had two years to run. It should be easy to sublet. Small flats in that vicinity were in great demand. There was the furniture, too. Something would have to be done with it, although there was nothing there she had any desire to keep. 

With these things arranged and her packing done, she would be free to make Caroline a visit before sailing.

FOOL'S GOLD 

93 Everything was golden in the golden sunlight, and through all came and went little visions of Martin. He, too, must be making preparations for the adventure ahead of them; he, too, must be looking forward to the work they were to do-together!

The matter of the flat arranged itself agreeably and surprisingly. Miss Miranda rushed in that night on her return from business declaring her new job "a cinch," and suggesting that she would be glad to take the rooms off Hope's hands just as they were. "I'll have to pay for the furniture in installments," she explained. "That dirty sheeny tried to make me believe he was giving me what he gave you; but, at lunchtime, I pieced together that check you wouldn't take, and he'd knocked off ten dollars a week on me. So I've got to economize at first, see?

At that, you'd get more in the end off me than if you sold to second-hand dealers over on Third Avenue. And I'd like to buy that picture most particularly." She pointed to the chalk sketch of Mr. Willing. "That shows your good taste." Hope looked at the picture long and lovingly, her mind going back to another young woman of a very different type who had wanted to acquire it. "It's not for sale. It's a portrait of my father, drawn by an artist who was fond of him; so it means a great deal to me and goes wherever I go.' 

"It looks swell on that wall, and the paper's going to be sort of faded around it," Thusa said disappointedly. "I'll buy you a photograph of Whistler's Mother to hang over the spot," Hope promised. "That's the best I can do for you.

It's sort of sappy for a girl to have mother pictures around in plain sight. Gives people the idea that she's kind of easy, see?” "There's the Carlyle then. It would be all right there. If

you don't like it you can find something else later."

So the Carlyle was agreed on and finally hung, and that acid gentleman would have been vastly diverted could he have heard the terms on which his presentment was finally accepted. I don't say he ain't respectable. When I have gentlemen visitors I think I'll tell 'em it's a photo of my grandfather who was a Baptist minister up in Troy." "I didn't know your grandfather was a Baptist minister!" 

Hope exclaimed. It was difficult to connect Miss Miranda even remotely with the ministry. "Neither did I, dearie; but my boy friends won't know that.

Grandpop Miranda was a bricklayer (builder sounds better), and Grandpop Schwartz peddled milk. Maybe that's where

And despairing of bringing her to see reason, Hope desisted from the effort. After all, the girl's friends were not likely to be better informed than she, and Carlyle would have enjoyed the joke in his own dry way. So Miss Willing held her peace and completed her arrangements to turn the little apartment over to Arethusa.

Her books she sent to Caroline's, to be stored in her vast attic, and she planned to go directly to the boat when she left Philadelphia.

She debated with herself whether she should notify Martin Newbold of her change of address, finally deciding that it was necessary, since there had been a possibility when last he communicated with her that her sailing date might be changed.

She wrote him a short note, saying that she was ready and would be on the Barataria on the twenty-fifth unless notified to the contrary and, after some hesitation, she signed herself,

FOOL'S GOLD 

95 

Faithfully Yours, Hope Willing.

There was nothing left to do but to say good-by to Miss Miranda. Hope was surprised to find that she parted from her with genuine regret.

CHAPTER XIII 

GOING to Philadelphia to visit the Pages, Hope had unhesitatingly allowed herself a Pullman seat. In view of her improved prospects, she no longer regarded it as an extravagance. 

She was settled in her place and glancing through a New 

Yorker when she was hailed, and looked up to see Martin 

Newbold smiling down on her. "Miss Willing! Do you spend all your spare time on trains between New York and Philadelphia?" “About as much as you do, seemingly," she returned. “I wrote you just before I started, Mr. Newbold. I thought you ought to know that I was ready and would be at Chestnut 

Hill with my cousin, Caroline Page, from now till sailing day." "Good!" exclaimed Martin. "Women certainly are better executives than men when they make up their minds to get something done." He was about to add that Mirabel also was ready, when one of the men of his party called to him: "Our seats are in the car ahead," he explained, and Martin waved a hand. "I'd better run along. I'm taking some of the potentates and powers over to talk with Uncle Jim and Mr. Shober; and policy suggests that I show them every attention." 

Hope nodded understandingly. "I'm practicing being politic too," she told him. "I can see we're going to be a wonderfully politic pair," 

Martin grinned, showing very white teeth; and they exchanged understanding glances. "It's going to be such fun!" His tone was boyishly reckless, and Hope noted with a rush of joy that he looked as if a burden of years had been lifted from his shoulders.

He went forward through the train and she did not see 96

FOOL'S GOLD 

97 him again until, by prearrangment with Caroline, she descended at North Philadelphia. There he awaited her on the platform, bareheaded and eager. "I thought it likely that you'd get off here. I only wanted to tell you it's certainly the Barataria on the twenty-fifth now. 

I can't let a girl beat me to it." While he was thinking only of Mirabel, Hope naturally enough applied his remark to herself.

There was no time for more. He stepped on the train again, Caroline's chauffeur picked up her bags and Hope followed the stream of outgoing passengers down the steep steps, resisting the desire to look back to see if Martin was still standing in the vestibule watching her.

Strangely enough the only antagonism to Hope's plans came from the one from whom she had expected staunch support, while she was surprised at the lack of objection from the elders of the family. Seemingly their opposition had worn itself out when first she took up a business career against their advice. 

They now accepted the fact that she had definitely left the fold of their protection, and made no attempts to influence her. Indeed, very sadly, they had been forced to admit to themselves that their city, as they had known and loved it, was no more. Gone were the days when on Sunday after church Walnut Street filled on the south side with what to a stranger appeared like one vast family; when friends and foes met and mingled, and a man's hat was more often in his hand than on his head; when age and experience received the respect due, and young womanhood's only occupations were those of wife and mother. A degeneration had begun even before the 

The automobile had decentralized their lives. Trade had crept in where formerly were only the homes of the élite. 

Town residences were abandoned. People lived now in the suburbs, in houses designed with taste and surrounded by ample grounds laid out by zealous landscape architects, a clan that had grown in numbers once Frederick Law Olmsted the elder, had pointed the way. Such places were artistic, were beautiful, but they lacked the background of family association, war.

FOOL'S GOLD 

and, worse still, the lack was not felt. 

The war when it came had given the finishing touch to tribal life. The young of the war generation had had experiences that led them to feel gently superior to their stay-athome elders; and the fact that their former autocratic power had been questioned had been passed on, how no one could say, to the succeeding youngsters, ever crowding onward, eager for their own taste of liberty.

Aunt Thorne alone had retained her influence and had managed to maintain the solidarity of the family against all the forces ranged against it. But, as she herself had said, she left no successor unless Hope should prove equal to the task, vastly more difficult than it had been in her great-aunt's day. 

And Hope was going to Paris to live. No place at all for a faithful Philadelphian.

Caroline it was who voiced her opposition to the whole plan. "I don't think you ought to go away, Hopie." The two girls, in the ease of undress preceding final good nights, were in Hope's room; a dainty creation in dotted mull and flowery prints amid which Caroline's Pagal pyjamas, vivid enough to have entranced Arethusa Miranda, looked entirely out of place. "Why not?" Hope inquired, entirely at sea. "It's desertion," Caroline told her. "The family ought to have some solid center. Grandmamma Shippen always said you were to take her place.' "Oh, stuff!" Hope threw herself back in her chair restively and stared at her cousin. "I'm not a center for the family in 

New York, working myself stupid for Schoonmaker's. I might just as well be in Paris getting some fun out of being alive." "What you ought to do is come and stay with me for a year. Then you'd make a suitable marriage-" 

Hope sat erect, defiant. "In plain English, pretty lady, what the devil are you driving at? 

You don't want to introduce your little cousin to society and take her to the Assembly at this late date, do you?"

FOOL'S GOLD 

99 "Well, it's time you were getting married," said Caroline defensively. "You know it is. You can't expect me to like to see you turning into an old maid, can you? When I remember that it's partly my fault for encouraging you and getting Grandmamma to back you up." She managed to look pathetic under this self-blame. "Give me a cigarette," Hope said. As she lit it, a puff of smoke serving as a partial screen, "How do you know I may not be going to marry my boss?" she asked lightly. "That's the legitimate ambition of every honest working girl, I'm told." "Oh, Hopie, are you?" Caroline demanded joyously, then her expressive face fell. "But, no, you can't be!" "Why not? What do you know about it?" Again Hope sprang stiffly erect in her amazement. "I haven't told you anything.' 

"No," Caroline acknowledged, "except that Mr. Bohlen got you the position. But Larry Toland saw you with him and

Martin Newbold at the Ritz, so naturally, when Queenie

Mirabel? Who was this chatty Mirabel? His sister? Hope had just presence of mind enough not to ask. "I didn't feel at liberty to talk about it, Carol. Since you've guessed, please don't pass the information on, even in the family.'

If a sister, Mirabel evidently was a jealous one. "I don't fancy it will be a difficult task," Hope remarked a trifle loftily. Already something told her that this Mirabel was no sister of Martin Newbold, but she refused to listen to the warning. "Unless all signs fail, I'll have work enough to keep me out of mischief."

Caroline snapped her fingers loudly and Hope jumped, then, recovering herself, laughed sardonically, not, as Carol thought, at Larry, but at herself. She would have been so ready a short time before to deny that there was such a thing as love at first sight. Yet she was game.

101 "At first sight?" she repeated with derision. "Larry

She made a great hit, all the boys were crazy about her and Martin, being a crack athlete, was spurred on by the competition. It swept him right off his feet, he just had to have her. They were married immediately after the last baseball game in Commencement Week. Probably she'd have made him marry her before, only he was captain of the team and wouldn't let the other men down like that, although he was insane about her.

"Didn't you know Tony isn't their child? He's her halfbrother. She resents it because he got more than she did of their father's estate.
