Hope rose and lounged over to the window, thrusting aside the curtains to look out not over back yards now but the wide expanse of the Whitemarsh Valley. "The sun will soon be rising," she said. "In this quiet, bucolic existence you lead, do we now go out and milk the cows or do we get a little sleep?" "I vote for sleep." Carol slid over to the door and opened it noiselessly. "Nighty-night, Hopie. The worst I wish you is that the Lord will soften your hard heart. Dream of Larry, like a good child." She shut the door without so much as a click of the latch and was gone.

CHAPTER XIV 

AFTER Caroline had gone Hope left the window and roamed about the room restlessly. Her thoughts were bitter, but the bitterness was for herself, never for Martin. She realized that he had had no intention of misleading her. Knowing that she was Caroline Page's cousin, it was entirely natural for him to assume that, in true Philadelphia style, she was conversant with his family history from the time of his greatgreat-grandparents down to the present day.

There was nothing for it but to acknowledge herself just the sort of fool she most despised. Susceptible, ready to turn a man's most casual word into a personal compliment and to treasure it. Most of all, worst of all, susceptible! Falling headlong fathoms deep in love because Martin Newbold was tall, too thin for his height, pale where he ought to have been brown, pathetically overworked yet possessed of a quick and responsive smile that had seemed to pierce her heart. She attempted no denial to herself. She loved him, a married man devoted to a beautiful wife. There was where she stood. 

She faced the fact without attempt at palliation.

He had done nothing to arouse such depths of feeling. 

Walking by his side she had noted his suitable height, yet they had not even danced together, since Mr. Bohlen had declared dancing between courses an insult to good food. 

Remembering every minute of their few meetings, she stepped in front of the dressing table and snapped on the side lights to regard herself with scorn.

It amazed her to find there was no subtle alteration in her appearance. After a searching examination she was assured that she was not outwardly commonized. To all intents and purposes she was the same girl who a short time before had congratulated herself on her sexlessness and frigidity of tem- 103

FOOL'S GOLD 

perament.

It occurred to her as a comforting thought that, if she did not show it, she ought to be able to live through it. What she could not have borne was that anyone should suspect she had been so quick to bestow her love unasked. Here she defended herself in a measure. Something had deluded her into imagining that Martin too was finding pleasure in their meetings which, of course, did not excuse her folly. "I never before accused you of being that kind of idiot, Hope Willing." She apostrophized her image in the mirror before she switched off the lights again and began pacing the room, moving back and forth, back and forth, as lithely nervous as a panther in a cage.

Well, she told herself, she might as well face it at once; plainly that was the kind of fool she was—and what was she going to do about it? How could she go abroad with the

Newbolds, how be associated with Martin so intimately that she would spend eight hours daily within sound of his voice, when short and casual meetings had wrought such havoc in her ordered world? On the other hand how could she disappoint him and what explanation could she offer if she backed out now?

Caroline, having various engagements, had provided her with a car the previous afternoon and Hope had managed to call on a prodigious number of her relatives to tell them of her plans. This she now regretted, since she had waxed expansive before their indifference, even explaining that she had resigned her position at Schoonmaker's with the sole object of going to Paris.

Plainly, to save her face, she must go. When she was there she might arrange to resign or be dismissed; of course giving 

Martin plenty of time to secure another more competent secretary.

That argument failed of conviction. She knew he would not be able to fill her place, could never find anyone to help him as faithfully and as selflessly as she would do. And this brought her squarely face to face with his angle of the

FOOL'S GOLD 

105 bargain. He had considered the selection of his confidential secretary to be one of the most important problems confronting him. He had told her so-had told her that he did not propose to sail until he had secured someone in whom he could repose full confidence. He had thought her such a one. And he had not been willing, so Caroline had said, to let down anything so relatively unimportant as a baseball team. Was she to let him down in what was clearly the great opportunity of his life? Was such a betrayal compatible with love? Was it not entirely selfish? Rather, was she not capable, because she cared so truly, of sacrificing herself to further Martin's interests? Was she not, indeed, in honor bound to carry out the bargain she had made?

Unhesitatingly she conceded that she was. 

Very well, then she must fulfill her contract to the best of her ability whatever it cost her. She would go to Europe on the Barataria. She would work with him until he was established. There must finally come a day when she could resign without injuring his prospects. Until that day came, could she depend on herself to hide her feelings, to let no one suspect what an idiot she was? If she could, there was no reason to hold back, since no one would suffer for it but herself. 

In the glimmering dawn she lifted her head proudly and again switched on the bright side lights beside the mirror. "I promise you, Hope Willing, that I will not let you down either," she murmured under her breath. "You have a right to your self-respect. I give you my word, and I've never consciously broken it, that I'll keep your secret."

Almost she persuaded herself that the girl in the lookingglass looked back at her approvingly and nodded her head in recognition of the sealing of this pact.

Her determination arrived at, a certain peace descended upon her. She darkened the room from the sun, now appearing above the horizon and powdering the mist in the valley with gold, and threw herself on her bed where shortly she drifted into a sleep beset with dreams, none of which she could remember on waking.

FOOL'S GOLD 

The days before her departure were eventless, save for Larry Toland's fervor. To the great amusement of the foxhunting set around Philadelphia, he followed her about with all the single-minded devotion of a well-bred pup, and spent his moments apart from her in devising new methods of presenting his proposals, asking advice of all and sundry as to how they would proceed, or had proceeded, when situated as he was. 

Inventing new ways for Larry to soften Hope's hard heart became a popular sport in the neighborhood of Chestnut Hill; and even the elect of the Main Line grew interested and suggested that the one who devised the winning trick should receive a prize, such as a prominent place in the wedding procession.

Hope would have been more or less than human had she given no thought to the fact that here an escape from her difficulties was offered. Her engagement to Larry would release her, in the eyes of her relatives, from any obligation to go abroad as Martin's secretary. Obviously Larry Toland's fiancée would be silly to prate of a necessity for earning her living.

Her code denied her a way out that might have been possible had she not known what love was. She had given her heart so completely that she had nothing left for any other man, and her generosity forbade an unequal bargain. Then too, even had she now found that she loved Larry Toland, it would have brought no release from the obligation she had assumed to Martin Newbold. "He said I was treasure-trove," she repeated obstinately to herself. "He really and truly needs me. Moreover I don't love Larry and I do love Martin. I hope I'll get over it in time. People do, I understand. Probably when I'm used to him I'll find he's not all I thought him. But so long as I love one man I trust I'm square enough not to marry another-even if he does own the most perfectly darling litter of pups I ever laid my eyes on!" The anticlimax was conscious, said pups having been the last bribe offered by Larry that morning.

FOOL'S GOLD 

107 Thus it came about that sailing day brought Hope a curiously diverse group of friends to see her off. Most prominent, perhaps, was Miss Miranda, attired in a new and striking purple costume with a real gardenia, and carrying a large box of candy as an offering from herself and, more amazing, an equally large bunch of roses from Mr. Rosen. "I asked him to let me off to say good-by," she explained breathlessly. "He was terribly nice about it. I don't feel right about that eighty dollars,' he says, forgetting he'd tried to make me believe it was only sixty. 'Suppose you take her a bunch of tony flowers. Something with class to 'em. So she'll know Schoonmaker's ain't really mean.' 

Here they are, from the firm, if you please." With pride she exhibited

Schoonmaker's business card attached to the rose stems with a piece of magnificent gold ribbon. Plainly no expense had been spared.

Willie Weismuller came next. He brought a book from his father and one from himself; from his mother, regrets that she was unable to come in person and a silver measuring spoon folding into a compact case. Mrs. Weismuller could always be counted on to be utilitarian. The only wonder was that she had not included a bottle of castor oil. 

To Hope's surprise, it developed that Arethusa and Willie had met previously and had struck up an acquaintance. Herman, taking his duties as custodian of Hope's fortunes very seriously, had sent Willie to make sure that she had secured a reliable tenant for her flat. 

Larry came early, enveloped in a gloom that even Arethusa's flamboyance could not lighten. Indeed when asked afterward what impression the girl with red hair had made on him, he had returned innocently, "Was there a girl with red hair? I didn't notice." "As if anyone could help noticing!" Caroline exclaimed indignantly.

Caroline herself had accompanied Hope in a very depressed state of mind, and, in one form or other, reiterated the same sentiment again and again.

FOOL'S GOLD 

"Remember, Hopie, if you ever get in a jam you've always got me to fall back on," she would say, near to tears, and

Hope, conscious of a choke in her own throat, would try to lift their farewells to a lighter mood. "I'll promise not to contract an alliance with anyone of lower rank than a prince without your consent, darling. 

Doesn't that take a weight off your mind?" "For God's sake, don't marry a prince, Hope," Larry had interjected hoarsely. "They're the worst of the lot. Always after American girls for their money bags."

This fortunately brought natural laughter, although it was offered in entire seriousness, Hope volunteering the statement that her money bags were full of nothing but holes. "Prince or no prince, he'd need good eyes to find anything so tiny as my fortune," she averred. "Besides, Willie's father is in charge of everything I have, and he'll see that nobody gets away with a penny of it, won't he, Willie?" She found it rather difficult to maintain a liaison between the two groups of her guests. "He will be extremely careful," said Willie, measuring his words. "My father appreciates your trust in him and feels his responsibility deeply." My mother advertised in my father's name for English pupils and there was instant response. Now he has many students, a circumstance which has improved his health and his spirits. Your cousin is really highly intelligent,

Mrs. Page." 

"Oh, Willie, such fulsome flattery from you. It's as well

FOOL'S GOLD 

109 thing he says. He admires you very much and he's kind of intelligent himself, if you ask me. Will you believe it, he claims there ain't any such word as ‘ain't' in the English language and offers to prove it on a pile of dictionaries? As if dictionaries mattered. You needn't tell me any of 'em's perfect. With all the words we have, it'd be a funny thing if there wasn't more than one of 'em left out."

Caroline found herself fascinated by this judicial pronouncement. "You're wonderful, Miss Miranda," she declared with enthusiasm. "You've rid me of a fetish which has dogged my steps from my childhood. "The dictionary is always right,' was what I was told at school, and I actually believed it. I've been a slave to that book. There was considerable more of the same brand of triviality, designed to plant it firmly in Mrs. Newbold's mind that Hope was the favorite cousin of Mrs. Page and that it would be politic to treat her with every consideration. 113 A bilious type. I suppose she will be seasick every minute of the way across." Mirabel made a point of describing all brunettes as "bilious types." As events proved, there was no necessity to worry over the secretary. Hope had made many voyages and had never been sick in her life. Martin, strange to say, was no sailor. He was always ill and, on this occasion, took to his berth and stayed there; glad of the rest and the opportunity to store up energy for the difficult tasks ahead of him.

Mirabel, who knew his weakness, was prepared for this and had laid her plans accordingly. Three chairs, the duc's, Hope's and her own, were located on one side of the deck, Tony's and Martin's on the other. It was too bad not to be together, she explained, but the boat was crowded and Martin was so happy with the child that she knew he wouldn't mind. Now that he was sick she did hope that Miss Willing would not object to being left with monsieur de Versac. She herself would have to stay with Tony, since she had brought no nurse for him and he was really too young to be trusted alone. 

Quite naturally, and without suspecting Mirabel's maneuver, Hope asked if Tony would not be content if she went to sit with him? She would prefer that arrangement to being with a party. She had counted on spending much time in study. 

So the matter arranged itself as Mirabel had cleverly planned, and no one suspected her directing brain, except the duc, who smiled a rather sardonic smile; although he never blamed a woman for getting what she wanted by any finesse, however devious. She had the Frenchman to herself and both made good use of their time.

Hope was quite happy with Tony. She had seen the boy first trotting contentedly around with Martin, holding tightly to his hand and looking up at him with adoring eyes. Not a pretty boy, young Tony. Small for his years, which numbered just short of seven, with straight potato brown hair and a pallid skin, faintly powdered with freckles across the snub nose. 

Nothing picturesque about him to tempt Mirabel to add him to her becoming adjuncts. Nothing beautiful except the shape of his head and his eyebrows and eyelashes. Those were of a sooty black and redeemed his rather small and light gray eyes from mediocrity. Mirabel was wont to draw attention to them, claiming them as his only point of resemblance to her. 

An old-fashioned child, with a pathos hard to pin down or to convey in words, he looked less than his age and talked with. the manner of a little old man in a voice so low and deep that it never failed to arouse a passing interest when first heard. 

He slipped into his chair beside Hope on the first morning at sea as silently as a small shadow. "Good morning." Hope smiled at him and he returned the smile, displaying a gap where a front tooth was missing. "I see you are a good sailor. That's nice. Children so frequently are ill at first.'

Horses are hitched to this and we drive over the country roads or along the Wissahickon Creek. 117 doctor he would say so. I don't think you need worry.

Later when she produced some of the fruit with which she had been plentifully supplied and offered it to the boy he looked at it longingly but shook his head. "I know I may have one pear, but I'm afraid. You see, my other front tooth is loose-" he demonstrated the fact, "and Mirabel was very annoyed when I lost the first one biting an apple. She told me I mustn't be careless again, because if the first teeth fall out too soon, the second ones grow in crooked-and it's very expensive to have braces made to straighten children's teeth, you know."

Tony was plainly oppressed by thought of the burden he was to his sister; and remembering vaguely that Caroline had told her Mirabel had secured a substantial allowance from the Courts for his support, Hope waxed wroth as she cut the pear in slices small enough for him to pop into his mouth where teeth which he assured her were quite up to their responsibilities could do the necessary work. She was indignant that so young and sensitive a child should be made aware unnecessarily of problems of ways and means. It did not incline her to like Mirabel, since it was obvious that she denied herself nothing, her clothing and all her properties being the most expensive possible.

Yet Mirabel was very pleasant and very charming when they met, never neglecting to pay a visit to their side of the deck at least once a day and always very solicitous that Tony should not be troublesome. Having definitely invested Hope with the responsibilities of a nursery governess, she was in the best of humors and never thereafter suggested that Miss Willing should make one of the fashionable group headed by the duc de Versac and herself. 

This suited Hope perfectly. She and Tony became fast

FOOL'S GOLD 

friends and she made a number of acquaintances more entertaining than she judged Mirabel and her admirers to be. 

Tony slipped up to her one day, leading by the hand a long, lank young man with a face of the most exaggerated melancholy such as belongs to the really great among clowns. "This is my friend, Miss Willing," he said, “and this is my new friend, Mr. Warren. He's a naviator. That doesn't mean he's in the navy," he added explanatorily. "It means that he flies. Up in the sky. Just like a bird. I always wanted to talk to a naviator but I never caught one on the ground before." "I always wanted to talk to one too." This remark was drawn from Hope by her sense of kindliness, it being plain to her that Mr. Warren was not quite sure he had not exceeded the latitude allowed on shipboard in inducing the boy to present him to her.

Thus began her acquaintance with Bob Warren, an acquaintance that was destined to ripen into a friendship upon which both knew they could count to the full.

CHAPTER XVI 

THERE was a pleasant party on the port deck of the Barataria. 

Mrs. Morton, the young, agreeable, if rather aggressive, wife of Professor Morton, a distinguished member of the staff of the Rockefeller Foundation, who was going abroad to oversee the field work of his juniors in several countries; a well-known author and his wife; a celebrated statistician with an alert sense of humor; a callow but entertaining playwright and a traveled spinster who appeared to be at home in every quarter of the globe, were all grouped near Hope. Mrs. Morton, whom she had met through Willie Weismuller, quickly drew her into their circle, to which two girls, art students from the Chicago Art Institute, occasionally joined themselves. These two, Selma Robb and Dorothy Grace, were of a type new to 

Hope. Like Topsy, they might have "just growed," for they acknowledged to no roots anywhere. Usually both loudly disclaimed personal talent or ambition and vowed the trip abroad to study was to give the other the opportunity her genius deserved. Yet, when the mood struck them, they might reverse this statement and declare roundly that they had been misunderstood and that each knew herself for a genius unequaled since El Greco while her friend was a poor unfortunate who could neither draw nor paint. They were amazing and amusing, and their views on art in general appeared to include a gentle leniency toward most of those mistakenly ranked as masters and an intention to show the world something new from Chicago-when they were ready!

The voyage was a delight to Hope. She loved the sea, she was enjoying more congenial companionship than she had had for several years and, above all, she was enjoying Tony, who had attached himself to her with a fervor that she did not suspect, although she knew he was fond of her. 119

and dusty room in a cheap, out of the way corner and everyone would applaud: 'Is he not prudent, the young monsieur

No matter that we know it is untrue. No matter that the French are richer than they ever were, an American who is not rich is incredible. Or if not that, he is a swindler come to see what he can rob away of the pittance poor gullible Jacques Bonhomme has left. You follow me? An

The furnishing should be similar to that of a bank. Real, heavy and good.

125 with Mirabel about her. To his amazement, his wife had objected seriously to his employing a woman secretary. A man was more suitable, more dignified, would be more useful. The sternness of his manner had intimidated Mirabel. 

Never, in all the years they had been married, had he taken such a stand in opposition to any whim of hers. As a last resort, she tried tears. When these proved a failure she capitulated without reservation, she acknowledged that she had been wrong. It would kill her to have Martin go abroad without her.

There was no doubt that she wanted to go to Paris to live. "Certainly," it was Hope's cool voice that interrupted his revery and brought him back to the present, "you want the big men, but they have valets, clerks-oh, I know I am right-you can't tell from what unexpected quarter pressure may be exerted that will tip the scale your way." "You're right," said this same Martin, who had vowed to let no woman have a finger in the direction of his business. "I'll promise to do nothing final without talking it over with you."

CHAPTER XVII 

WHEN Mirabel Mercur married Martin Newbold right out of college, she had achieved what at the moment was the height of her ambition. Was he not captain of the baseball team, a crack hurdler and a member of one of the most exclusive secret societies? Also, although she had not yet calculated its exact worth to her, he came of a distinguished family. There was enough competition for his favor to make her fancy she was in love with him, or, at any rate, that, in the circumstances, most girls would have been. She gloried in the belief that many of them, openly or in secret, envied her her conquest. 

In her favor it must be remembered that for eight years thereafter she had retained his devotion, even though at times it bored her. Essentially, despite a luxuriant exterior, she was a cold woman. 

Cold and virtuous. Whether virtuous because she was cold it would be hard to say. 

Now, at twenty-seven, she had developed until the girl she had been at eighteen was almost forgotten. Almost, not quite. 

She had assured herself that her marriage had been a wise step, notwithstanding his family's lack of enthusiasm over her, until Martin's recent rebellion had alarmed her. She warned herself that she was not going to enjoy it if he grew dictatorial. 

The fact that he was so easy-going had always constituted his chief charm in her eyes.

Always unwilling to brook the slightest opposition, his unusual resistance to her edict had unconsciously put her in a mood to encourage the devotion of monsieur de Versac more seriously than she otherwise would have done.

Granted that Martin had a certain position and could make money, here was a man who was an adept in that useful pursuit and who had a very substantial title as well. And not entirely out of her reach, either, since fortunately he was a 126

FOOL'S GOLD 

127 Huguenot as much as he was anything. Religion nowadays exerted little influence on men of the world, yet it was as well he was not a Roman Catholic. She determined to foster his friendship as, for the present, she named it to herself. She might find him useful to frighten Martin into submission. It was a defect in her mentality that she did not recognize the fact that her husband was not a man who could be frightened. Her paramount belief in all circumstances was that anything she wanted was attainable. It only remained to find the easiest means. 

The first and totally unforeseen result of her tactics was to inaugurate in the duc's mind a consideration of the advantages and disadvantages of an American alliance. He had no thought of love therewith. It was the possible dot of the bride that was the lure. De Versac's business was badly in need of more capital. He had gone to America to secure this and could have had all he asked on the terms of his American bankers, which included the formation of a stock company, the installation of a manager of their selection and the apportionment of fifty-one percent of the stock issued to them. 

He had no doubt of the benefit to him of this comprehensive plan. They would make him a rich man if he could reconcile himself to seeing the control of the great enterprise to which he had devoted some of the best years of his life pass from his hands. He was going home to seek for a preferable alternative. What he had would always be valuable. He risked nothing by delay. 

Strangely enough, Frenchman though he was, marriage as a way out of his difficulties had not occurred to him before. He would have told anyone that he had no liking for chains, even for chains of orange blossoms. "This young Miss Willing," he was addressing Mirabel, who was beside him in a deck-chair comfortably wrapped in a rug, "how does it come that she takes a business position? Offhand, I should say that she was not that type.'

Mirabel shrugged her shoulders. The subject did not interest her. 

"I believe her father lost his money before he died. He

FOOL'S GOLD 

probably never had much. Those old Philadelphia families enjoy being poor and proud, you know." "Miss Willing does not look poor-" "Caroline Page probably dresses her in the hope that she'll make a good match. She seems to have managed to keep a tight hold on Caroline since she married Charlie Page, who is really a rich man.' 

"And doubtless madame Page will furnish a suitable dot-"

Mirabel twisted her head, held stiffly within a turned up collar of fur, to stare at him. Then she laughed, a rippling tinkle copied from a musical comedy star who was greatly admired. "If she married-a duke, let us say? I did not realize before that you were attracted by the young lady or I might have turned matchmaker." 

"I am not sufficiently attracted to contemplate a folly, if that is what you mean.' De Versac did not attempt to deny that she had read his thoughts. "I am not a hot-headed young fool, nor is my temperament one that demands a hearth and home. Other considerations, such for instance as a need for money to develop my business, might induce me to marrysuitably. That is, a very substantial dot would be the first requirement, but necessarily combined with a presence and a personality that I could esteem. It goes without saying that she must be a femme du monde." "And Miss Willing has the essential qualifications?" Mirabel strove to keep any hint of jealousy out of her tone; to make it gay, vivacious, amused, and at the same time to hold out to him the lure of her own personality. "If she had much money, yes. Yaretsky was exactly the sort of meteoric genius she prided herself on attracting. She longed now to ask if De Versac knew him and to suggest that he present the great violinist to her. Metaphorically the Frenchman tore his hair. A woman was always so personal. She knew no world that did not circle around her, but his fault was easy to repair. De Versac raised thumb and forefinger to his lips and blew upon them as if releasing a bit of thistledown. "Exit Miss Willing," he said, without undue emphasis, and meant it. He admired her entirely impersonally. Had she been rich, he would have thought her a very desirable parti. 

As it was: Exit Miss Willing, who need never know she had occupied the stage for an idle hour and had so narrowly escaped being cast for the part of duchess to his grace. "If you are seriously considering matrimony, you would do well to let me help you," Mirabel suggested, her mind working rapidly on a plan to enable her to retain an influence over this man. Mirabel was bored. The man was talking exactly like

Martin; but, whereas she would assuredly have told Martin that he was suffering from delusions of grandeur, she now assumed an expression of understanding, and murmured: "How like you that is! You don't desire money just for the pleasure of expending it as a smaller man would. How much I admire you for that."

131 After all, no man can object to a pretty woman's admiration when it is so wisely placed, and, looking into her red-brown eyes, De Versac told himself, not for the first time, that Mirabel was more than merely pretty. He expected her to make a genuine sensation in Paris. He had meant all he had said to her on that score. "Have you ever worn plum color?" he asked her, changing the subject without preamble. "I do not mean a purple plum -not an aubergine even. I mean a red plum, dark, yet with a blue bloom on it. And with fur dyed to match, it would be most original. I shall suggest it to Paul. He continued to enlarge upon his theme while Mirabel regarded him shrewdly between her half-closed lids. Which, she wondered, was the real man? The iron-master, with his heart in his mills; or the Parisian fop, longing to sponsor the introduction of a striking-looking woman?

Doubtless he would be credited with more than a passing interest in her career, but discreetly (she knew her French) and groundlessly (she knew Mirabel Newbold née Mercur).

That being her one aim in life, she could be counted on to take care of Mirabel, and she calculated that she had done a good stroke of work in suggesting that if De Versac contemplated marriage with an American, she should be allowed a hand in making the match. Should the time come when lack of money drove him to that point, he would remember her offer. He was French. He would take no definite step until he was sure he was selling himself and his title in the best market. And thus she could count on being forewarned were there any reason to dread such an event. No marriage should be arranged unless she found it to her advantage in some way. 

On that point she was determined. Whatever he thought of her beauty, De Versac was underestimating her force, or so she told herself.

CHAPTER XVIII 

ONCE Paris was reached-a much changed Paris since she had visited it before-Mirabel began a search for a house that would offer the setting she desired for her own personality. Not that she disdained comfort. With money at her command, she intended to supply any deficiencies when she had located the interior she had in mind. 

Because of Martin's position at the Surety Bank and Trust

Company, De Versac had sought his acquaintance, with the underlying thought that he might possibly be able to make use of him to attain his own ends. He had only met him on the eve of his resignation, and once that connection was definitely severed, he lost interest in the American. Moreover, in the sudden intimacy of an ocean trip, his impersonal attitude toward Mirabel had in a measure undergone a change, a change which he had not defined even to himself, although he was more than ever eager to see her well placed.

He called upon her one day at the hotel to tell her that the large and elaborate modern residence erected by a rich American who had married a title was unexpectedly to be thrown on the market. "It is the most perfect thing of its kind in Paris," he declared with real enthusiasm. "I am so glad for your sake, chère madame.” "I do not want it.” Mirabel's statement was abrupt and De 

Versac opened his narrow eyes in amazement. "But I tell you it is perfect and you have not yet seen it! he cried. "I don't mean to see it." Mirabel, sure of herself, made no effort to conceal her obstinacy. "I don't doubt it is all you say it is, and I don't propose to allow myself to be tempted." 

As his astonishment increased, she offered her explanation. "9 132

FOOL'S GOLD 

133 "The countess is notorious for her wildness and her extravagance. Moreover, in America she had no position at all. 

Here, she tried to buy her way into society. If I take her house it will look as if I had stepped into her shoes with the intention of following in her footsteps, and"—with a reckless mixing of metaphors-"I do not intend to hang that weight around my neck. I'll hunt until I find a house with a garden, a house that smells of something besides money." 

She had occasion thereafter to remember that metaphorical remark more than once, the smells in the old houses she investigated proving to transcend description. At last she was rewarded by discovering on the Ile St. Louis exactly what she had in mind. This property was for sale, and she urged 

Martin to buy it. At the moment he was averse to tying up any of his capital and begged her to look for a place they could rent.

More and more as time passed she was becoming indifferent to his wishes and opinions. Although he had never promised a miracle, she began even to cast doubts on the wisdom of his severance of relations with the bank. 

Who could say that there would ever be any substantial return from this fantastic enterprise he had launched out on? He never told her anything about it, from which she deduced that there was no success to report. Also, because he did not at once give in to her new whim, she decided that he was increasingly difficult to get along with.

Impatient at his opposition to her wishes, she took her own money, bought the house and rented it to Martin at an exorbitant figure, thus making sure of an increased return on her investment.

Under these conditions he offered no objection to the place she had set her heart on. It had a little garden for Tony to play in, which more than made up to his mind for the inconveniences of the location as regards the office he had secured some time before Mirabel had located her "perfect treasure."

This had presented no problem to her, as she had determined at the outset that she could not allow any outside considerations

to hamper her in her search.

She had complete control of Tony's and her own incomes as well as the very liberal allowance Martin now made her. 

She never told him the house was her property and, as he never asked inconvenient questions, he remained in ignorance of the fact that she simply transferred the amount of the rental from her allowance to her personal account. 

A governess, well recommended, had been engaged for

Tony immediately on their arrival, and thereafter, as was her way, she had washed her hands of the boy. The happiness of a sensitive child was something with which she did not concern herself. He was taken care of, why should she worry? 

Martin had never been able to understand her attitude toward her young brother. The custody of the boy had devolved upon them when he was only three years old, and Mirabel had conscientiously attended to his bodily welfare since then, while making him an excuse for evading the cares of motherhood.

The explanation of her lack of affection for Tony was twofold. The circumstance that Tony's mother had outlived their mutual father, thus giving him a preponderant share of the fortune she had long counted on as sure to be her own, had added to the bitterness she had felt when her father had rejoiced over the birth of a son. She had hoped there would be no children of his second marriage.

To her, from the first, Tony was uninteresting if not repulsive. Feeling her dislike, the boy, young as he was, shrank into himself and became silent with her. While a more picturesque child might have won her admiration, if not her affection, her brother's crowning defect lay in the fact that he did not feel her fascination. Mirabel could not endure that anyone should deny her charm.

It was a conversation with the late lamented Bridget Nolan that had revealed his opinion of her. "An' didn't Mrs. Newbold look beautiful in them new furs?

As soon as he was old enough, she planned to rid herself of him by sending him to boarding school. Probably in Switzerland. There was a good and long-established school at Vevay, but they did not care to take boys so young as Tony; which did

In the end it took much more time than she had expected to decorate and furnish the house. She insisted that it must be carried out consistently and rather in the grand manner. 

No French rustique, nothing later in period than Louis XV. 

When Martin complained that it was not homelike she scornfully agreed that there was nothing "American rocking-chair" about it, if that was what he had expected. 

Regarding it from a different point of view, De Versac saw at once that she had contrived the perfect setting for her type of beauty. The almost unshaded lights, the paneled or tapestried walls, the graceful shapes of the furniture, all served to present her in picture after picture, which mirrors carefully placed duplicated and reduplicated. Had she been less beautiful it would have been unfortunate. As it was, the effect was astonishing. The Frenchman acknowledged to himself that there was not another woman of his acquaintance who could have conceived so perfect a shrine for her own loveliness.

For he understood that Mirabel had worked for the effect she had attained and accorded her the laurels due to genius. "Although," he said to himself with a sudden flash of intuition, “after all, it is not quite genius. She could not do for another what she has done there for love of self. She would not be interested."

The house changed his attitude toward her still further, which was proved by the fact that instead of taking her to call at the atelier of the great designer whom he considered temperamentally fitted to originate her costumes, he persuaded the man to see her in her own chosen surroundings; and that meeting had come up to his liveliest expectations. Monsieur Paul, something of a decorator himself and an artist to his finger tips, had recognized the importance of the material he was to work with. This woman, dressed as he would dress her, could not fail to create a furore.

But monsieur Paul was a man of the world. "Parfaitement," said monsieur Paul, buttoning the top button of his fur-lined overcoat as if he felt a chill. "I say to you, mon cher, parfaitement."

CHAPTER XIX 

BUSINESS at the new offices was strikingly unlike the dull routine of Schoonmaker and Company, Incorporated. 

Men came there, great men, celebrated men, for conferences with Martin Newbold. Sometimes they bore openly their own distinguished names, in which case they were entertained at his home and met his beautiful wife. More frequently they came incognito, and Hope identified them from pictures she had chanced to see in the pictorial supplements of home papers or illustrated magazine articles. Under all circumstances she was discreet. She never mentioned these visitors even to Martin until he introduced the subject, and outside the office walls conversations about business were with her taboo. 

Frequently her services as interpreter were called for at these conferences. Then it was her effort to translate as if she were an automaton without understanding of anything deeper than the dictionary definition of the words she elucidated. She knew that Martin and she would go over the progress made afterward, to plan the steps still to be taken; but, before these foreigners, she had no desire to appear as anything more than a negligible figure. It was a great game and she loved her part in it.

Martin too was enjoying the change from his position in New York. So far, his work had been done under cover, but it had come off exactly as he had planned, without a hitch. 

The astute financiers of Germany and France had seen the manifold advantages offered by his plan and were ready to appear in the open aligned beside him the moment he gave the word. Their working agreement had been put into operation at once, and binding documents had been sent to Mr. 

Shober in Philadelphia; where that gentleman had quietly organized an office to take entire charge of all accounts and other 139

FOOL'S GOLD 

details. This served a double purpose in that it kept the business under American control and relieved Martin of irritating minutiæ, thus leaving his mind free to grapple with more vital complications that were certain to crop up. 

The whole affair was to him a most exhilarating contest of wits and, if he could win Italy to his side, he would not hesitate to tackle John Bull.

Hope had been a little surprised that England had not been his first point of attack. She was quick to seize on his reasons when he explained that when he could go to them with a solid phalanx at his back of France, Germany, America and Italythese possibly reinforced by Spain, Argentina and Chili-the answer must be what he wished. "I understand!" she exclaimed, “you think they will be ready enough to share in reaping the harvest, while they might hesitate to take the risks of pioneers.' 

"Something like that," Martin conceded. "It's terribly hard for the British to forget that we were once their youngest and naughtiest offspring. There'd be a lot of humming and hawing before they'd be able to make up their minds to follow our lead, and they'd have a greatly magnified belief in their importance to us, I mean. When we're that far along, they'll see that they must join us or be left sitting out in the cold, and our terms will be accepted without haggling." "Then it's Italy next?" Hope asked, leaning toward him across the end of the desk, eager as a child for the continuance of an exciting tale.

Martin took a deep breath and braced his shoulders as if for a coming struggle. "It's Italy next-and not going to be so easy. To begin with there's Mantellini-" "And to go on with?” "To go on with there's probably Mantellini again,” Martin smiled. "Aside from that forceful gentleman, their laws are 

However, Uncle Jim's no not any too favorable to our plans. slouch at international law, and I think he's found a chink in their armor. At all events I'm going after them at once, and

FOOL'S GOLD 

141 when I get them, I'll say we're safe." "As it is, we're making money-" "That, Miss Willing, is what alarms me. 

We're making too darn much money. I'm afraid of our simple little scheme being brought into the light of day before I'm ready, by reason of its success- success beyond my wildest dreams!" "I see that possibility." Hope wrinkled her brow thoughtfully. "Well, there's no way to reduce that risk, and it's so much to everyone's advantage to keep under cover that I don't believe it's great. After all, in Paris no one knows anything except you and me-and of course, Mrs. Newbold."

With an expression she characterized as enigmatic, Martin stared at her a moment before he spoke. 

"Mrs. Newbold is not a business woman.' His tone was dry. "Since she takes no interest in financial affairs, I never bother her with details. She knows nothing of what I am doing save that a good income is assured to us." 

Hope found herself without a suitable response to this declaration. So much of Martin's energy was concentrated on the development of his present enterprise that the fact that Mirabel was shut out from any share in it seemed to her to mark a separation that must cut him to the quick. She looked around the offices, light, airy and substantially furnished, much as she had planned them in the beginning, and recalled her surprise that Mirabel had taken no part in their fitting out. Everything now was in perfect order and there was nothing in sight to excuse an abrupt change of subject or a flight into her own room, which something within her prompted. And she must make some reply. Martin was still facing her with that strange expression on his face. 

"Probably that's just as well," she said lamely, "then you and I are the only ones- "And neither of us is likely to talk," Martin completed her sentence for her. "That I grant you; so perhaps all's for the best in this best of all possible worlds. And now let's study your figures on Italy. I'm specially interested in Genoa, Naples and Messina—”

FOOL'S GOLD 

Discussion with any touch of the personal was over for the time and thereafter, as the months rolled by in rapid succession, Hope was happy, although she could not conceal from herself that association with Martin had not worked a cure for her. She loved him devotedly and unselfishly, so that she even resented Mirabel's indifference to him; and, so far as she could see, his attitude toward her never changed. He was impersonally friendly; he liked her; he appreciated her coöperation for what it was worth, which was a great deal. He was grateful for the interest she took in little Tony, whose devotion to her was touching. She gave the boy many happy hours. Together they enjoyed Punch and Judy shows, gauffres, trips on the Seine boats, picnic luncheons here and there in the woods and country. Mirabel did not care enough for the child to be jealous of his affection for another woman and saw in this association a means of keeping something of a hold on Hope. 

The Pages were Mirabel's primary reason for her continued interest in the girl, a fact Hope fully comprehended. For her own part, she was glad when they were in Paris to be able to keep up an appearance of friendly intercourse with the Newbolds, since her steady refusal of Mirabel's invitations might otherwise have given an impression of strained relations. In addition to Hope's cousins Mrs. Newbold never quite lost sight of Yaretsky. She still hoped through the girl to lure the musician to her house. Once there, she trusted to her own charms to bring him again.

Time passed without any appreciable change in the situation as regards these transplanted Americans, save that Mirabel, tactfully backed by De Versac, began to acquire a position of some authority in French society. Usually Americans who met with success there were liked because of their unvarnished Americanism. Mirabel was the exception. If not quite 

French, she at least was not at all American. And she was a marvelous decoration to add to any salon. Her passage through a room was sure to be followed by well-bred murmurs of admiration. She was invited many places inaccessible to her compatriots, and she was nearly content. Not quite. It

FOOL'S GOLD 

143 annoyed her that Martin had not time to add himself to the train that danced attendance on her. A French husband, De 

Versac for instance, would have known how to combine pleasure with business in a way her countrymen were never able to emulate. Not that there was any lack of men to pay her homage, it was simply that she grew tired of explaining that her husband was tied to his office. To her mind such conscientious fulfillment of a duty carried a distasteful taint of the middle class.

At last Martin had told her that he was making much money for his backers; was, in fact, in a fair way to make much more; consequently she now began to resent the participation of these others in the profits. Martin did the work, Martin's, therefore, should be all the gain. Painstakingly he went into some detail concerning the obligations devolving on one who was using the capital of others. "You see, don't you, that I could never have started this thing at all without Uncle Jim and Mr. Shober behind me?” "I see that you needed them to begin with. You acknowledge that you have made a big success and have returned their original investment. Well then, you don't owe them anything now and surely the time has come to throw them overboard?

One reason I agreed to your leaving the bank was that I wanted you to profit by your own business foresight. It strikes me that you are no more your own man here than you were in New York. Other people take the major part of your earnings." "Haven't I made it clear to you that without these other people there could have been no earnings?” "You've tried to," Mirabel returned sulkily. "You've quite failed to convince me." "Then I shall abandon the effort," Martin said, his weariness apparent. "I've gone over and over the subject for hours and, if I can't make you understand that I'm bound in honor to my backers, I can't-and that's all there is to it!" "In honor bound,'" Mirabel sniffed. "Those words are only an excuse for inertia, and if you won't take the way that's

FOOL'S GOLD 

as plain as the nose on your face you'll have to bestir yourself. 

I warned you before we came here how it would be. I need more money. I have a position to keep up. I ought to have a château somewhere. A place where I can invite my friends to stay. A place where I can entertain- "I thought you entertained now- 

"I thought you wished me to-I thought that was the way a woman helped the man she married. Consolidated his position socially, made it important- 

"Of course, of course," Martin interposed, "don't misunderstand me.

The solidity of your position is important. Only set your heart on something a little smaller than the palace of Versailles. I've made a pot of money but I still need it to put into investments that will make more." 

For the time, that ended the matter. His consent had been obtained, or at least so Mirabel assumed, and she began at once her search for a country house. She was not to be suited easily and Martin saw less and less of her. He hardly noticed this, so alien had their interests already become. 

This fact did nothing to increase his intimacy with Hope. 

On the surface their relations remained impersonal in the extreme, beginning and ending in the office. She was by his side during business hours, after which she disappeared within the sheltering doors of madame Masson's quiet pension to stay there, or so he fancied, until the hour approached when he would need her again.

Once or twice his conscience had pricked him at the thought of the dull life she must be leading, when her youth and beauty were taken into consideration, and he had suggested that 

Mirabel invite her to dine.

His wife had accepted the suggestion pleasantly enough, but Hope almost invariably eluded such invitations. She acknowledged to herself that she did not enjoy seeing Mirabel at the foot of Martin's table. No use to turn the knife in the wound. 

Moreover the girl found plenty of entertainment. Her steamer acquaintances had not lost sight of her and, to her surprise, she met more of her former friends in Paris than she

FOOL'S GOLD 

145 had in New York; while they, being on holiday, were quite ready to amuse and be amused. 

The Pages had made more than one flying visit to France. 

Larry Toland had accompanied them and talked vaguely of establishing a stable in England and coming over each year for the hunting, a suggestion that Hope did not take very seriously, knowing that he and Charlie had just acquired large tracts of land in Pennsylvania far enough from the city to escape the suburbia that was engulfing all the old haunts of the hunting set. On their last trip the two men flew to England where they bought a pack of English foxhounds and thereafter, in their desire to see these established and acclimated at home, cut short Caroline's visit to Paris, to that little lady's great disgust. "If I could have Hope with me-if she wasn't such a slave to that old Martin Newbold, I wouldn't go back with you," she scolded. "Stay, if you want to," said Charlie, who knew his Caroline and adored her. "And let you kill the pups by overfeeding? And miss Saratoga? And worry every second for fear you'll knock out what you use for brains in some foolish stunt or other? No, thank you!"

So they all left in a mad scramble to catch their boat, while 

Hope, breathless and laughing if a little homesick, resumed her normal life.

In this Yaretsky and Bob Warren both played parts, Bob's entirely unsentimental, Yaretsky's best described as puzzled. 

He admired Hope intensely. He rejoiced in the fact that she made no attempt to prove herself musical; that she frankly put her fingers in her ears to cut out some of the great clashes of sound from the orchestras he took her to hear, which she claimed "jarred" her head unpleasantly; that if she liked something he played, she said so as frankly as she expressed her dislikes and with the most naïve delight, which was measurably increased if she learned that she had been listening to the work of some great composer.

FOOL'S GOLD 

"I'm getting to be a wise one, Yarry," she would say. "Some day I'll know more than just to stand up when they play The Star Spangled Banner." 

Other women had been so quick to toady to him, to pretend an interest and comprehension of what pleased him, no matter how far it was beyond their understanding; above all so ready to seize on the first opportunity to inject a suggestion of lovemaking into their relations with him, that he more and more turned to Hope with a sense of relief. She was as companionable as a nice boy; yet, manlike, he could not help wondering if she could possibly be as indifferent as she seemed-and if so, why?

While Yaretsky had never had so impersonal a friend among women, Hope was not the only girl in Bob Warren's life with whom he had maintained purely platonic relations. He had known other Americans of her stamp. If you had asked him he would have told you that they were K.O. until they fell in love. 

Then they went "into a nose dive and more'n likely crashed." 

Not that he expected Hope to come to grief now. He had formed an opinion about her early in their acquaintance and had never seen any reason to change it. He believed that she had already lived her romance and left it behind her. If she married it would be when she was past thirty and was looking for companionship for her later life. He himself was involved in a hectic and disturbing love affair where, against his will and better judgment, he was held in chains. He welcomed Hope's calm friendliness as a contrast and relief.

Hope accepted him as another Willie Weismuller, a casual playmate and no more. She listened to his explanation of the engine the United States government refused to be interested in, which his enthusiasm led him to believe was bound to revolutionize aviation if he only had the money to build it; she encouraged him in his efforts to secure attention for it; she was a companion and a friend. And that was exactly as each desired it should be.

So passed the first year and part of the second of Hope's life in France.

FOOL'S GOLD 

147 She was negatively happy. If she had given up the hopes that, unconfessed, perhaps unrecognized, lie nearest to a girl's heart, at least she shared more of Martin Newbold's life than anyone else.

She was with him for eight or more hours on all their working days. He did not lunch at home and when finally he left the office he went to the tennis club to play with other men or to ride for the sake of the exercise, while most nights, until the small hours had begun to grow, Mirabel was at entertainments to which he rarely accompanied her. Hope knew that for days together he never saw his wife at all. 

The arrangement of the offices remained the same until one day Martin made a suggestion: "Miss Willing, what kind of a light would you have if your desk were moved to this side of your room? I'm going to be skating on thin ice for a few weeks and it might be as well for you to hear all that is on foot-" "Italy?" Hope inquired. "No, Austria and Roumania, although I'm in doubt about bothering with the latter under present conditions—' 

Hope nodded her comprehension. "Of course it will all depend on the terms. Now how about your desk? 

Would the change be too uncomfortable for you?"

There was no denying the fact that the light would fall from the wrong side; she would be working in her own shadow; yet, if Martin wanted her there, somehow it must be managed. "I'll get Gervaise to help me move it, then I'll try it," she suggested. "I'm still fairly husky," Martin averred. "Suppose we find out if I could qualify as a piano mover?" 149 he had, there would have been sympathy between these men who filled her heart. "No," Martin answered, regret in his voice. "I wish I had.

I am judging by the portrait of him I saw in your living room in New York. You remember I was there once with Uncle Jim?" 

Almost, at this, she laughed aloud. Men were so naïvesome men-nice men. As if she could forget one second of the momentous evening that had altered the whole course of her life. But it was comforting to find that he thought she might have forgotten. It showed she had played her part well and gave her courage to go on. "I wish you had known him," she said frankly. Of course the family blamed him because he wasn't a clever business man. I don't. I'm glad he was just what he was, sensitive, trustful, gullible if you will. 

I'd rather look back on the happy years we spent together than be the heiress of the fortune he might have left me had he been different." "I can understand that," Martin told her. Martin grinned. The phrase had a familiar ring. "However acquired, I'm thankful for your practicality.

Hope knit her brows, simulating intense concentration.

"It is borne in upon me that statuary is not the solution," she announced. "No?" Martin affected astonishment. "You surprise me.

Hope chuckled. "You seem to imagine I spend every day moving furniture. 

I don't need a reclining chair." "Well then, I tell you what I really would be glad to have here. Lots of times, after you've gone home, if I could take forty winks I'd be a new man, able to work for hours. Couldn't we get a leather couch that would fill the space satisfactorily?" 

For a moment Hope hesitated. "If you want a couch you ought to have it," she agreed, "but couldn't we move the filing cabinets in here and put the couch against your side wall?" "No," Martin objected, "that wouldn't do at all. I might need something from the files when I had a shifty beggar to deal with whom I wouldn't want to leave alone in my office. 

If there's to be a couch, this is the place for it." 

Hope told herself that her aversion to the suggestion was silly, the result of a recent course of French farces to which she had taken madame Masson, enjoying to the full that worthy woman's superhuman efforts to suppress her amusement at jokes which she considered unfit for the jeune fille under her charge. "Oh, all right," she gave in, her manner offhand. "I'll go where we bought the rest of the furniture and see what they have. You'll need a soft pillow such as would be most inappropriate for an office; but there's plenty of room for it in my closet."

So that matter was settled and the couch installed and forgotten, save on the rare occasions when Martin made use of it.

The two now faced each other at their work, and neither

FOOL'S GOLD 

151 head was ever raised without a glance to see what the other was doing.

Hope found this delightful but difficult. She was forced to guard her expression lest Martin surprise a tenderness she never meant to betray. At times she caught a look on his face that set her heart beating wildly and at once she took herself to task for misinterpreting it. She assured herself that she was his competent secretary to Martin and nothing more. That was the one thing that excused her remaining in his employ.

He had recognized her competence and their mutual success by several substantial raises in her salary, and they now had a Cerberus at the outer door of the offices, a boy in buttons who brought in the visitors' cards and sorted the mail. When 

Hope reached her desk in the morning she always found the letters neatly piled awaiting her. 

On this particular day the air was crisp, the sun was shining, the pavements, washed clean, still reflected the light. Paris had never seemed more full of life and color. Her step was swift and springy as she crossed her private room to hang up her hat and coat and find two vases for some flowers she had bought because she could not resist their tender beauty. 

Together, they were doing great things, she and Martin Newbold. Already the contracts he most coveted were within his reach. Success was surely to be theirs. Care-free, full of a sense of well-being, she turned toward her desk and let the flowers fall from her hands.

There, separated from the letters, lay the unmistakable envelope of a cablegram. 

She regarded it with interest quite free from alarm. What had turned up in America to necessitate a cable from Mr. Bohlen?

She recrossed the little room hurriedly, but even before she picked it up she saw that it was addressed not to Martin Newbold but to Hope Willing.

Always businesslike, she did not stop to question its contents but slit it open at once.

It was quite unlike any telegram she had ever read and when

FOOL'S GOLD 

she had mastered it, she stood as if turned to stone. 

If this were true, as it must be, what excuse had she for remaining longer in Paris at Martin Newbold's beck and call?

How, under these circumstances, account for a determination to continue to work for her living? For the message read:

HOPIE DARLING COME HOME STOP GOING TO HAVE A BABY STOP AM TERRIBLY FRIGHTENED STOP COUSIN HARRIET NORRIS DEAD STOP LEFT YOU SIX MILLION DOLLARS CAROLINE 

Was ever a bequest of six million dollars so unwelcome?

CHAPTER XX 

MECHANICALLY Hope glanced at her wrist watch as she sank into the chair by her desk. Her face was set and strained and she looked like one who had had news of a disaster rather than the recipient of a fortune. Luckily it was still early. Martin would not arrive for nearly an hour; she had time to reason out the situation that faced her and to recover her poise.

She never questioned the authenticity of the news in the cablegram. If Caroline said so, it was true; and of course she was glad to have the money. She had as much liking as any girl for what money would buy. It was the lack of personal excuse for holding on to her job that was agitating her. 

She told herself that come what might she did not mean to give up her work. She could not bear to; it was her joy in life.

Rather she would renounce this fortune if she could do so without detection. It was too late now to make a career of babbling and backgammon.

Back in America at the beginning of things, she had reasoned that when the day was reached that saw Martin's plans worked out to their logical conclusion and his arrangements all perfected so that no traitor could bring disaster, she would leave his employ. Now she knew better. She would cling to the solace of his companionship as long as she was able. He had no suspicion of her feeling for him and she meant that he never should have. After concealing it from everyone for two years she now had faith in her ability to keep it hidden forever.

How was she going to explain to him her choice of work with all the playgrounds of the world thrown suddenly open to her? 

It was in answer to this thought that the inspiration came to her. Why should he learn of this legacy? There was at least a chance that if she acted quickly this item of news could be suppressed. 153

FOOL'S GOLD 

At once she drew a pad of cable blanks toward her and proceeded to write two messages. There was no need now to count the pennies. The first was to Caroline. She had made up her mind about Caroline at once. Even when her cousin was little, she had funked any new experience until it came, when, of a sudden, she was brave and ready to meet it. If there was a tooth to come out or a throat to be painted she clung to Hope's hand until the dreaded moment was reached. It was Hope she wanted near her in any crisis, and Hope did not mean to fail her now. The cablegram to her read:

WHEN IS IT COMING YOU POOR FISH HOPIE 

Caroline would read into that all the affection it did not express. There was no need to slop over. 

The second telegram was to Charlie:

ASSUMING CAROLINES INFORMATION CORRECT ADVISE COUSIN HARRIETS LAWYERS TO MAINTAIN ABSOLUTE SILENCE ABOUT PERSONAL BEQUEST STOP don't want TO BE TARGET FOR ADVENTURERS STOP WILL RETURN TO AMERICA SHORTLY STOP UNTIL THEN BUSINESS CAN WAIT STOP DEPEND ON YOU TO ARRANGE THIS STOP VITALLY IMPORTANT 

HOPE

For all his lighter side, Hope knew Charlie Page to be an efficient executive. He would do his utmost to avoid publicity for this item, and his utmost was sure to be good. She would feel more secure when he knew of her desire, and at once jammed on her hat. She would file the messages herself, then she could plan her further movements at leisure. Not waiting to put on her coat, she flashed out of the building and hurried along the street her slender figure, poised and pliant, looking a model for speed.

155 When she returned, Mrs. Martin Newbold was before her.

Had he ever questioned the fact, he would still have assured himself that he loved his wife devotedly. Simply he regretted certain foibles, of small moment and outbalanced by her many admirable qualities and her great beauty. He imagined that after ten years of married life there were few men who could say they possessed a flawless jewel. He was luckier than most. 

Hope's generosity threw into higher relief Mirabel's pettiness, to give it a charitable name. He did not consciously compare the two, nor did he consciously avoid doing so; only, since 

Mirabel was not faultless, he was aware that he wished her special flaw were other than it was. 

Without further discussion he and his confidential secretary plunged into the business of the day. They were interrupted once by the telephone. "Mrs. Newbold wishes to speak to you." Hope handed him the instrument. "That you, Mirabel? You have? Perfect, you say? And for how much?" There was no enthusiasm in his voice and after listening for a moment more, he emitted a low whistle. "Sorry, my dear. I'm not a Rothschild yet. Don't carry your negotiations any further. It is quite out of the question. 

Absolutely impossible. Talk it over later? No, darling. Do be reasonable."

Embarrassed, Hope rose and started toward her own room. 

Martin stopped her with an emphatic movement of his head. "It just can't be done at present. I'm sorrier than I can say if you are disappointed, but I warned you not to set your heart on Versailles. The Government'd probably sell you that for less than the agent is asking you for this château." He listened a moment longer and hung up with a short ""Bye." Then turned to Hope. "Mrs. Newbold's found a place if you haven't," he said a trifle grimly. "I'm afraid young Tony's out of luck. She'd only let him go to you now if I opposed it."

FOOL'S GOLD 

Hope stood for a moment regarding him, her head on one side. Not for the first time it occurred to her that nice men were unfitted by nature to cope with women of the Mirabel type. She had never meant to set herself in opposition to 

Mirabel, but, telling herself that Tony must be protected, and trying to speak lightly, she now offered a suggestion: "It's not advice I approve of giving to the young and confiding, but why not oppose it then? For once, let's be Jesuitical and do wrong that good may come of it. If necessary, I'm prepared to 'lie like a gentleman' to save Tony from that school in Switzerland."

Martin seized her hand and shook it warmly. "You certainly are a regular fellow," he said appreciatively. "And you remind me forcibly of Larry Toland when you talk like that," Hope returned, which shortly was listed among those things better left unsaid; for both of them, aware of 

Larry's loudly proclaimed devotion, colored deeply and turned away. It was the first moment of painful embarrassment between them since Hope had been in Martin's employ.

COME AT ONCE ANGEL CHILD left her in doubt as to whether if she went at once she might not find herself in for a long vigil. Hope had feared this misunderstanding and knew it would not help her cause, yet was forced to tell the truth. "No, Mrs. Newbold. I've come about quite another matter-" and she broached the proposal she had in mind.

to American standards but appetites were large and bills mounted astonishingly fast. Her own expenses, once they had sailed, would be negligible. Altogether the arrangements were satisfactory to everybody when Hope sailed for home.

She had not notified anyone in America of the date of her departure, fearing some business necessity might arise to detain her; and, once aboard, forbore to wireless Caroline, childishly hugging the thought of her cousin's surprise. Consequently she was not met on her arrival in New York and rather enjoyed the novelty of seeing her own trunks through the customs and checking them to Chestnut Hill.

She found that there was a thrill in returning to New York. 

It struck her that the traffic had enormously augmented during her absence; that there were more pedestrians on the streets and that, despite talk of hard times, the people, looked prosperous and, above all, energetic. No doubt this was her country, these her people, and the thought came to her that when Tony was sent to school it should be at home. It was his birthright. No one wanted an American boy turned into an imitation Frenchman.

CHAPTER XXIII 

HOPE's greeting at the Pages' was not the boisterous welcome she had expected. True, the dogs sprang upon her, excited by the return of an old friend, even Mandarin Orange and Burnt Sienna, the chows, flattered her by losing the aloof detachment that is a chow's prerogative and charm, a moment later retiring from the leaping group to regard each other suspiciously, each evidently accusing the other of instigating their fall from their superior eminence. 

Charlie was away and Caroline was in her own room lying down. "Mrs. Page ain't so awful well, Miss Hope," Dobbs, Thompson's more lenient successor, remarked. "She don't eat next to nothing, and she always was what I'd call a hearty eater for a lady." "I'll go right up to her," Hope said, conscious of a constriction about her heart. (Little Caroline sick? Why hadn't they told her? But, of course, they had.) She ran lightly up the stairs and, pausing only a second outside the door to put her countenance in order, she opened it, presenting a smiling face.

Caroline, who was lying on a couch drawn close to the double windows looking down on an extended view of the Whitemarsh Valley, now flat and melancholy in the misty twilight, sat up and, wordless, put her hand to her throat. 

Hope ran to her, taking her in her arms, and Caroline, bowing her head on the shoulder that had never failed her, sobbed as might the child she was at heart. "Oh, Hopie, I thought you'd never come," she whispered. "Why didn't Charlie cable that I was really needed?" Hope demanded indignantly. "Why did he say everything was all right?" 179 missed being too late and that if she had been, life could never again have held any happiness for her. "I wouldn't let him," Caroline explained, wiping her eyes. And all the time I've been in such an awful funk for fear I'd go over the line and leave Charlie and the baby with nobody to take care of them." "You're not going over the line," Hope assured her with a confidence she was far from feeling. This was a new Caroline, this girl with a pale, strained face surmounting a heavy body; different indeed from the merry, athletic little cousin who, however her fingers might cling, always had a smile and a saucy word to offer to the world. "And the baby ought to be a perfect hero if he's going to take after his mother.' "Oh, Hopie, do you really mean it?" A faint stain of color for a moment lent a semblance of roundness to the sunken cheeks. "Charlie would be so ashamed if his son funked his fences." "He won't, never fear. He'll inherit nerve from both his parents. Stop maligning my godson. You haven't asked me yet, but I've elected myself to the honor of being godmother. 

And do you know what I've decided to give him instead of the usual silver porringer? A hunter, the best Charlie can buy." "Wou-wouldn't it be better to give him a donkey?" The expectant mother, her tears quite dry now, stammered delightedly. "You know he'll ride rather light at first." "A donkey!" Hope scoffed. "For this boy? Do you think 

I want to insult your son? No, madame, he gets a hunter, nothing less. And by the way, I find I take naturally to being an heiress. I've brought you the most perfectly lovely things from Paris. I wonder if my trunks are at the station?" "You angel!" Caroline screamed with pleasure. "Do telephone and find out."

When some time later Charlie came in, it was to a scene of cheer such as he had not enjoyed for weeks. Caroline

FOOL'S GOLD 

was looking almost herself, tricked out in a teagown straight from a Paris atelier, and the room resembled a bazar, so full was it of baby clothes and feminine frivolities. "I wanted like hell to send for you to come at once, but 

Carol wouldn't let me. I've no idea why-" "She was character-building for the offspring," Hope explained briefly. "Tell me what the doctor says, Charlie." It's what I'm here for. She's better already. Says she's hungry as a starved pup and is coming down to dinner." "Gosh, I'm glad!" said Charlie, which, coming from him, was a pæan of thankfulness. "My mother and father are still on that tour of the world, and Mother Pepper is worse than useless with Carol. She fusses her to bits." "She always did," Hope agreed. "Cousin Maria can find more things to agonize over than any other human I ever met." 

And they parted to dress.

Ten days later it was a very different Caroline who greeted Hope. "Wasn't I clever to have two at once?" she boasted. "Does that let you in for two hunters, Hopie? Now if you'd be a good guy and marry Larry Toland we could soon get together enough offspring to have polo matches between the two families." "Hate to be disobliging," Hope said briefly. 181 once sternly maternal. "Call the nurse," she commanded. "I can't allow that. It will ruin the shape of his thumb. And please telephone to

They might as well have been born in a slum. Tell them to send it today without fail. I want it for this child, not for the next one. 

That store exists under the delusion that it is still the easy-going nineties. That's why Mother and all the other old dowagers cling to it. It makes them feel well preserved to see how doddering the salespeople there have grown."

Hope followed orders, smiling happily to herself. This was her old Caroline, imperious, cheerful, full of life. It was going to be a wrench to leave her as she must in a few weeks. 

With the babies to cuddle and pet as much as modern methods permitted, it would be easier than ever to settle back into the well remembered round of Philadelphia living. A pleasant, lazy existence where the people she knew expended untold energy in amusing themselves. That most northern of Southern cities made Paris and the far-reaching schemes of Martin Newbold seem very distant. Yet when she thought of Martin, she knew she could not stay. However little she was to him, he was her all. Nor was that the only consideration. was not deluding herself when she said he needed her. It was too late to introduce another to Martin's intricate affairs.

The christening was the next event and was attended with pomp and circumstance. Despite Charlie's insistence that they be called Pepper (Caroline's maiden name) and Salt, because it went so well with pepper, the twins received the names 

Thorne Shippen and Brock Norris, the latter, as Caroline explained, because it would please Cousin Harriet, and she felt sorry that that rather trying lady had had no real mourners, since she had done so exactly the right thing in making Hope an heiress. 

On this felicitous occasion the twins distinguished themselves by unusual stoicism. Neither in church nor exposed to the adulation of their relations did they wince, thus earning the approval of the more sporting of the observers and causing their fond mother to wonder if they were entirely well.

Be that as it might, they were returned to their nursery without a stain on their characters and amid a chorus of praise that satisfied even Caroline's maternal pride. 

Egged on by Caroline who could not give up the hope of establishing her favorite cousin near her, this festive occasion was also marked by a return to the attack of Larry Toland, who was always at Hope's side. "I love you, I love you, I love you," was his refrain at all

Which, with a sigh, Larry accepted as the usual answer to his plaint.

Shortly thereafter, her business as well as her pleasure satisfactorily concluded, Hope sailed from New York intent on reaching Paris by the date she had promised.

CHAPTER XXIV 

BEING a fairy princess in disguise had presented quite a new range of sensations. Always generous, Hope had been forced by the narrow limit of her income to restrain her desire to give. Her first gratification of that impulse had been in buying the presents she took to Caroline. It had been fun to say, "I'll have this, and this and that," undeterred by the prices. 

She had enjoyed something of the same thrill when establishing the Weismullers. Mrs. Weismuller, entirely unused to a bottomless purse, had remarked cautiously that perhaps they might move when their lease was up. Hope's answer to that was to go out and rent an apartment such as she considered suitable. She was determined to see Herman comfortably settled, and she won her way by pointing out that, if either place must be left empty, the Central Park West apartment was the more expensive and therefore represented the greater loss.

Fairly trembling with excitement, and enthroned in his new wheel chair, so light that he propelled it easily with his hands, Herman had received her in his office the day before she sailed. "It's life for me, my dear," he said shakily. "I hope I may prove worthy of your trust.' 

"I'm not worrying about that," Hope assured him. "Does

As a lover Willie was not so eloquent as he was in the class room, but he was man enough to know the proper response to this, and Hope and her affairs ceased for the time to be the subject of a conversation which had reverted to the strictly personal. 

The villa Hope had rented at St. Cloud was a lucky find.

Not on a main road, it had the finished aspect of an earlier era. Its small front garden was laid out formally with a pebbled drive and a graceful little fountain. graceful little fountain. It was not till
