one had passed through or around the house that its character as a remodeled farm was revealed. 

Here Graham Craik had done nothing to alter the exterior. 

The sunny walls still bore their espaliered fruit trees. Pleasant homely herbs mingled with the hardy flowers in the borders. 

At a distance from the farm buildings a table had been built around a great tree. Reed chairs gaily cushioned, the children's toys, a rabbit hutch and a row of distant beehives had combined when first she saw the place to draw from Hope the exclamation: "The very thing for Tony!"

Back in France after her American trip, there was nothing for her to do but take up her residence there, ousting Martin, who went reluctantly to his club. The Paris house had never had in it one spot that he found homelike. It was ridiculous that he could not stay on at St. Cloud; but madame Voisin's pointed nose, bent on sniffing out situations that did not exist, had warned him that she was expecting developments to follow Hope's return. In this his instinct was correct. To madame 

Voisin's mind there could be only one explanation for the fact that a girl as handsome as "mademoiselle Villing" was willing to spend her days in a man's office. She thought the establishment of Tony at the villa was merely a notification of madame Newbold's complaisance, and she shrugged her narrow shoulders. It mattered nothing to her what these reckless Americans did. For her part, she had never been better paid or so comfortable in her life. If the wife did not object, why should she? She determined that she could be blind if need be, and she had no doubt of the need. It was disconcerting therefore, to have Martin pack up and leave the day before Miss Willing was expected. However, as she was incapable of accepting the fact that a lovely girl might live without a lover, she successively cast

Hope and Bob saw each other frequently, especially as he grew more down on his luck. The Italian and the French governments successively had rejected his plans for an improved aeroplane. "And I can't blame them altogether," he owned. "I'm afraid to show my whole hand. There are parts that I have no patent on. What's to prevent them from snapping up the ideas and then giving me the merry ha-ha?" "Why don't you build a plane and show them what it can do?" Hope asked, suspecting what the answer would be. 

Bob laughed sardonically. "Why doesn't one pigeon's egg make a big omelette? I can't any more than meet my current expenses," he confessed. 

Without a thought of how it would strike him, Hope said carelessly: "I'll lend you anything you need to build it." "Bless your heart.” He was touched, even while he was amused at this lack of practicality in one who boasted of being a business woman. "You're an angel-but aeroplanes run into real money."

193 itself. "You see, it would be impossible for me to take a woman's money to build a plane for myself. It's entirely different for me to build one for you. A millionairess has a right to buy a plane or a yacht or any darn foolishness she pleases.

There's only one point I must insist on," he added anxiously, "you've got to promise me you won't try any solo flying until I say you're ready. No promise, no plane, you know." "I promise," Hope said with uncontrollable relief. “I promise most faithfully. And I always keep my word."

Thus it happened that Miss Willing, in addition to six unwanted millions, found herself well on the road to becoming possessed of an unwanted aeroplane.

CHAPTER XXV 

IN a household constituted as was that at the villa at St. Cloud it was inevitable that a clash must come, and over Tony. 

Madame Voisin was a good teacher who took pride in the rapid advancement of her pupils along the prescribed paths of knowledge. Intensely national in her prejudices, she disliked 

Americans, as foreigners, if for no other reason, and worked for them only because of the high pay they offered. Also, she plumed herself on her extreme femininity, as she interpreted the word. Hope's quiet independence she set down as insolent license. Had Tony been a big and blustering boy he might have overcome her objection to him as a foreigner and conquered, even enslaved, his governess by the force of his masculine intensity. Delicate and sensitive as he was, she had accepted his sister's estimate of him as justified. He was one of those spoiled American children who are proverbial in Europe and virtually unknown on their own shores. Thus she had neither sympathy nor affection for him and her authority was absolute; while Hope's love for the child was tender, her understanding of him born of a realization of his impressionable and responsive nature, yet she was without authority even to shield him from injustice. 

More than once she had been present when madame Voisin had deprived Tony of simple pleasures as punishment for faults that, to her mind, were no more than misunderstandings of what was required of him, which a little willingness to enter into the boy's point of view would have cleared away. She had concealed her partisanship, she thought successfully, had refrained from intervening in any way, and only when alone with the boy had tried to explain to him why madame Voisin had taken the stand she did. 

And Tony, quite unaware that he was making mischief, 194

FOOL'S GOLD 

195 had later recounted their conversation to the governess who, instead of being placated by Miss Willing's good intentions, saw in all she had said an effort to make herself all-important and to belittle madame Voisin in the eyes of her pupil. To the mind of the jealous woman there could be only one reason for this. A desire on the part of the girl to ingratiate herself with his doting brother.

Again the Frenchwoman found herself wavering in favor of her first suspicions regarding Hope. It might even be that so far there was nothing between her and her employer, but the governess was prepared to discover that the young lady was scheming to capture him. He was a striking-looking man, and young. Madame Newbold was a fool to permit her husband to have anyone so-she hesitated in selecting terms of praise, for she did not admire Hope-so unusual in appearance as mademoiselle, at his beck and call every day, unless- The woman smiled a demure smile. Enfin, madame was no fool.

That had been her first thought—and first thoughts were often inspirations straight from-some place! From that time, without consciousness of spying, madame Voisin was on the alert to confirm her assumption. And again, for some time, there was nothing that even one with a preconceived theory to prove could find suspicious. Then came the affair of the pony. 

For the sake of his health, Hope had been anxious that Tony should learn to ride, and, after careful calculations based on her bank deposits before she knew herself for an heiress, she had decided that she could afford to keep a pony for him and an inexpensive hack for herself without giving rise to accusations of hidden wealth.

Lacking advice from Charlie or Larry, she bought for herself a young horse which she had frequently hired and which was guaranteed sound. The pony proved more difficult to find, those brought to her notice being Welsh and too fiery for an inexperienced rider. Finally, however, Carter Ridgeway turned up in Paris with the daylight hours hanging heavy on his hands, and she commissioned him to find a Shetland for her. "Not too small, not white, gentle and one that doesn't

FOOL'S GOLD 

stumble," had been her anxious specifications. And at last, on the very eve of his departure for Burmah, Ridgeway had bought for her exactly what she wanted. 

Wishing to be a witness of Tony's joy in such a present, she' had ordered that the little coal-black beast should not be brought to the villa until five o'clock in the afternoon, and when she reached home, her first thought was for the boy. 

She did not enter by the front door but went around the house into the pleasaunce at the back where at that hour she might expect to find him feeding his rabbits. He was not there. "Tony," she called. "Oh, Tony!" There was no response.

At the scullery window the head of Mélanie the cook, bobbed up, then as quickly ducked out of sight. It struck her the woman looked a little frightened.

CHAPTER XXVI 

FROM the beginning there had been difficulty about the Italian affair.

Martin had voiced his belief that they were plotting to double-cross him, were repeatedly postponing with a purpose, perhaps at the very moment were trying to discover what countries he had not approached-had not contracted with- Hope had listened, brow puckered, deep in puzzled thought. 

Finally she looked up with the quick lift of her eyes he had come to know so well. you "I don't think so. My idea of the Italians is that they are- 

I don't mean timid exactly, I mean doubters. They're never quite sure they've made the best bargain they can, have got out of the ultimate cent of profit. Now they are afraid Mantellini may drop down on them and interfere-possibly confiscate your last payments. You see, they're avaricious. That's the reason, if you are buying anything in Italy, it is wise to place your gold on the shop counter in full sight. It's rarely they permit you to pick it up and go away with it.' And so it proved. An agreement had quickly been signed when the offer had been what Martin called "Spot cash-on the nail." "They think they can slip out of the country, rich men, if Mantellini objects once the deal is consummated," Hope said. "What effect would that have on us?" "None!" Martin's smile was confident. "Even a dictator must bow to such a coalition as we will have at our backs. 

And, as Uncle Jim has fixed it, their own laws support us. 

All I've got to do is get together more real money than I ever 203

FOOL'S GOLD 

expected to see in my life. Fortunately they don't ask for gold.

American or Bank of England notes will be entirely satisfactory, I'm given to understand."

With a view to the approaching consummation of this agreement, which would give him command of the most important link in the chain he was forging, Martin had accumulated a large sum in his safety deposit box. He always made his cash payments in currency drawn from banks all over the country against letters of credit issued in America in many cities, some in his name, some in Hope's, thus avoiding the curiosity in banking circles that might be aroused by withdrawals of the magnitude he required. Mr. Shober and Mr. Bohlen attended to the business arrangements in the United States and there took many precautions, since it was important even yet that none of the international bankers should get wind of their enterprise, although all concerned were amazed that it could have grown to such proportions undiscovered. 

Meanwhile, with unlimited money at his command, Bob had completed the plane and Hope had gone up with him once. 

This was an excursion that she had dreaded, and she was delighted to find that it was less alarming than she had feared. 

Lack of experience led her to question her own judgment, yet it struck her that there was no sensation of unsteadiness such as she had heard complained of by others who had ventured on flight in the smaller planes. She had no nausea. But then the fact that she was a seasoned sailor might account for that. 

At all events, Bob was delighted with the results both of the flight and her reaction to it. "There are still some things I want to try out," he confided to her, patting the plane affectionately. "The Shooting 

Star's a beauty and no mistake. It won't be long till she's fit to fly the Atlantic."

As this woman talked Hope was thinking rapidly. There was her own money-she could lend him enough to take on this new steel combine; yet how could he bring himself to make use of information "wheedled" from one of her admirers by his own wife? The answer was that he could not, any more than he could desert partners who had given him his start. She need not worry herself. Martin would know how to defend his own probity. Mirabel, even though she was his wife, could never bring him to consent to such faithlessness. 

Mrs. Newbold was already on her feet and Hope too now rose. "I beg of you not to count too much on obtaining Mr. Newbold's consent to this enterprise you are proposing-" "You doubt its success?" Mirabel was insolently confident of her power. Plainly she meant to use every arrow in her quiver; and looking on her really amazing beauty, Hope was shaken in her faith. the mingled amusement and contempt which Mirabel was at no pains to conceal, that she had betrayed her secret to one who would have no scruples about using her knowledge should she see a way to make it serviceable to her. Indeed now she echoed and emphasized that unconscious "Martin." "Martin shall do it," Mirabel declared. "Don't underestimate the hold a wife has on her husband. When she cares to make use of her power, in the end he always does anything she urges upon him."

CHAPTER XXVII 

HOPE finished her work at the office mechanically, at least part of her mind still going over and over her late interview with Mirabel.

In the afternoon a messenger brought her a note from Yaretsky, asking her to accompany him to a concert where one of his compositions (Opus XXVII, Skyscrapers) was to be played for the first time in public. She hesitated, prepared in advance to find his music unmusical to her untrained ears, only to accept in the end because an evening alone with herself appeared to stretch on and on to intolerable lengths. The thought of Tony's disappointment was discounted on this occasion, since she had already warned the child she was unlikely to be home, expecting the double work thrown on her shoulders to detain her.

Being no longer limited in her expenditures, she had retained one room at the pension to house evening clothes and to give her a place to sleep when it was inconvenient to return to the villa. She had made infrequent use of it, but it was always ready, and madame Masson held her in genuine regard, which stopped short of affection only because Hope belonged to the ungenerous race who had paid not only their debt to Lafayette but most of the French war expenditures as well.

She dined at the pension, Yaretsky always being too nervous to entertain any guests before a concert in which he had a personal interest, even though he was neither playing nor conducting. 

Madame Masson was gently disapproving of Hope's going unchaperoned, while unwillingly conceding a certain unreasonableness in demanding a chaperone for a girl who worked daily in a masculine office. 211

FOOL'S GOLD 

"But your employer is, you tell me, a married man-” "And would it be more to your taste if I were going to this concert with a married man?" Hope laughed. "It might be arranged." "The good God forbid!" Madame ejaculated piously.

To give her conception of a tone of respectability to what to her mind barely escaped being escapades, she always made a point of appearing in her best bourgeoise black silk to interview 

Hope's friends for a moment or two before the girl left the shelter of her roof, bestowing a motherly twitch to a fold or a ruffle just to emphasize the fact that the young lady was not without feminine guardianship. The American youths who were most frequently her escorts had no idea of what it was all about, and would doubtless have considered madame, mustache and all, nothing more than a persistent bore had their encounters with her been of longer duration. They sensed the fact that she disapproved of them only less than she did of their cigarette-smoking, independent sisters and cousins, but forgot her once her doors had closed upon them. 

Yaretsky was of different clay. He rejoiced in what he called "the perfection of madame's performance," and spent considerable time thinking up crytic remarks to please her or displease her as his mood dictated. 

On this occasion he was concealing great nervousness under a mad gayety. Indeed when they had at last departed madame could not be sure he had not been drinking more than was seemly for a man who was to act as the sole escort of a "wellborn, well-instructed demoiselle." "Do not expect her back at all, madame," he had said at parting. "The only way I can induce mademoiselle to listen to my prayers is to compromise her hopelessly." "Monsieur comprehends that is no way to talk before innocence, but you would marry her, would you not, monsieur 

Yaretsky?" Madame made this inquiry anxiously, marriage being to her mind an end in itself, and Yaretsky undeniably eligible as well as distinguished. 

Yaretsky twisted his mouth as if in doubt.

FOOL'S GOLD 

213 "I cannot be sure." He shot a sidelong glance at Hope to see if she was enjoying the play. "Should she beg it of me very prettily, I might be tempted. I am soft of the heart-" "And of the head!" Hope interpolated with scorn. "But remember I pray you, madame, even in that so Puritan land of America this woman is above the age of consent- 

"That may be," Hope interrupted. "I promise you she won't look it in the court room when she sues you for breach of promise and injury to her young affections." "But mademoiselle would never do anything so indelicate!" 

Madame exclaimed, scandalized. "To parade a broken heart in the courts and ask for the salve of money—" "Ne vous fachez pas, madame," Yaretsky interposed soothingly. "Fear nothing for me. My lawyers will first demand proof that she ever had a heart." "If you spend another hour talking nonsense to madame, we will miss the concert altogether," Hope suggested, tiring of the rather thin jokes whose only merit was the shock they administered to madame Masson. "In fact I may as well take off my wrap." "Come on then," said Yaretsky in the tone of a martyr. "You will only be bored, I warn you-and especially with

Skyscrapers," he added on the pavement as he lifted his cane to signal to a passing taxi. "I don't believe I shall be bored," Hope told him. "I'm always too curious to find out what it's all about. All the clangor and noise that to you is so enthralling—” "Noise? Noise?" Yaretsky spoke irritably. "Music, my child, should not be spoken of with such disrespect. It is tone, sound, volume if you please; while noise is something crude and brutal, an insult to the hearing. You look so poetic, so intense, burning, sympathetic, that it is hard for me to accept the fact that you are really a barbarian, incapable of appreciating the highest in art." "But am I?" Hope asked in pretended seriousness, feeling the tenseness of her companion's nerves and bent on making him relax. "Has it never occurred to you that you are setting

FOOL'S GOLD 

your own sense of hearing up as the only standard? May it not be that I have a finer, more acute sense than you, and that what your coarser perceptions accept and even enjoy (just as a public less advanced than you cries out for jazz and the cheap popular songs that are beneath your notice), so I, endowed by nature with a more sensitive apparatus than yours, may find only pain in the crudities that appeal to you?"

Stiff and straight as if transfixed by the shock of the idea presented to him, Yaretsky sat in the taxi, saying nothing. As they drew up under the lights of the Salle Gaveau he turned to her a face alive with vivid interest, Skyscrapers quite forgotten. "But what you say may be possible. Think of dogs and how some one note too intense makes them suffer. Why does nobody put it to me like that before? For of course it is true. 

Each individual ear is different. Perhaps it may be that the ears of a nation bear a resemblance to each other. Take the Chinese, for instance. We know that they have subtleties of tone which we cannot apprehend-"

They were blocking the way. The brass-buttoned commissionnaire who had opened the door of the decrepit taxi, recognizing the great Yaretsky, hesitated to attempt to hurry him. 

Hope, interested to find her flimsy hypothesis given such respectful attention, yet sensed the attendant's dilemma and put a hand on Yaretsky's arm. "Get out, Yarry," she urged softly. "We'll talk of this when we reach our seats. There's a whole line of motors waiting behind us.' 

Under her urging he descended from the cab and paid the driver. Having done that much, he would have stood on the pavement elaborating the fascinating theme she had suggested, had she permitted it. "We'll be mobbed if we stand here," she warned him. "I see the Mortons in the next taxi, and Kreisler is in the limousine just ahead of them with Zimbalist. Nod to them, they have their eyes on you. Oh, and there's Mary Garden, dripping with pearls, and-"

FOOL'S GOLD 

215 "Get me out of here," said Yaretsky imploringly. "I don't want to talk to anybody. I want to think."

Thus urged, Hope waved a hand to the Mortons and, taking Yarry's arm, hurried him onward. To reach their seats was something of an ordeal, since friends and people who were merely acquaintances all wanted to exchange a word or two with the musician. Showing less than his usual rather florid courtesy he cut them short and finally sank into his seat with a grunt of disgust. "Never again shall I come to a concert," he vowed. "Never, never, never! One might as well risk one's life in the rabble at your American football games. It leaves one all hot and bothered and not at all in the mood for music." "I agree with you," Hope declared gravely, her eyes dancing. "There should be a private runway from the curb to each seat in the house. And then, when all are seated, what would you think of opening the proceedings with prayer? 

That we might be given ears to hear, brains to appreciate' 

"I do not know that it is the brain that appreciates," Yaretsky interrupted. "With me, I think it is the backbone-what you call the marrow of the spine, where all the nerves go prickling up and down." "Here come the musicians. This highly scientific conversation will have to wait." Hope looked at her program, glad to find that there were no very stirring numbers before Skyscrapers by Jascha Yaretsky. She knew him well enough to feel sure that, once it had been played, be its reception good or ill, he would be relieved and happy that the ordeal was over. 

When it was reached, Skyscrapers proved to be one of those modern pieces of music in which, striving for color and contrasts, the composer has opposed soft and subtle harmonies to tremendous weight of sound. Yaretsky listened to it, slid down in his seat until he seemed to rest on the very middle of that appreciative backbone.

When the music ended it was greeted by a furore of applause, cries of "Bravo!" calls for "Yaretsky! Yaretsky!"

Hope, who had managed to keep her fingers out of her ears

FOOL'S GOLD 

through the loud passages, glanced at him sidewise. He was sliding ever farther down in his seat, but his face was at peace.

The turmoil continued unabated. "I will not make a speech. I will not!" he said childishly. "If they do not stop I shall crawl under my loge. I should weep if I tried to talk in public. Do not let them make me, Hope, I beg it of you. I should be disgraced for all eternity."

A messenger hastening to the end of the row, Yaretsky refused to see him, and it fell to Hope to open the note he bore. 

For God's sake, if you won't come on the stage at least stand up and bow!

She read it to him, but he remained obdurate; so she found a gold pencil in her evening-bag and wrote on the reverse of the sheet of paper:

Monsieur Yaretsky, overcome with the success of his Skyscrapers, begs you to thank the audience for their generous response and also to congratulate the orchestra. 

She the folded note to the messenger. gave

The applause showed no sign of abating, instead it increased in volume until finally a little fat man with shiny lacquered hair and a waxed mustache, whose broad expanse of shirtfront and black evening clothes gave him a fascinating resemblance to a penguin, waddled out and read Hope's message aloud.

He was immediately popular with the audience, and the musicians were at length allowed to continue the program. 

No sooner had they begun the next number than Yaretsky whispered to Hope: "We are now leaving!" and leaping to his feet, slipped quietly up the aisle and out of the house, Hope, her wrap hurriedly dragged around her, close in his wake. 

It was not until they were seated in a taxi that either of them spoke. Then it was he who burst forth: "Dolts, idiots! And that double-distilled essence of fool!"

FOOL'S GOLD 

217 "Who?" Hope asked it timidly. She had never seen him so violent. "That dumb Jew. That polished parrot, Isemberg-Rothstein." "Who is Isemberg-Rothstein?" "The man who spouted his few choice words after the massacre of my masterpiece. Are there no musicians left in the world? Is there no one to divine when a thing is wrong?" "The massacre of your masterpiece?" Hope was amazed. "The audience went crazy over it. I even thought you were pleased. Wasn't it all right?" "In a way, I was pleased," Yaretsky grunted grudgingly. "Parts of it were magnificent. The mass of the tall buildings, stiff and stark against the clear blue sky with its rolling clouds, the winds soft and gentle that wooed it, the winds fierce and jealous that resented its challenge. It was, for the moment, real to Yaretsky; and that fact alone kept Hope from hysterical laughter. In that great blare of sound, where was the piccolo? 

At the very exclusive little night club Yaretsky flattered with his patronage, when exquisite food and drink had brought him to a more reasonable frame of mind, she again broached the subject of the missing piccolo notes. He must have been asleep-half a beat at least he was slow.

If so, it was obvious that they were waiting in the hope that the Frenchman would be able to gather together the capital he needed. 

Her natural curiosity urged her to find out, and Mr. Rush, with no idea that he might meet serious competition, had no hesitancy in giving her the information she sought.

badly.

She wished she felt at liberty to tell Mr. Rush what she knew. As Mrs. Newbold's secret information was not hers to disseminate, she held her tongue.

CHAPTER XXVIII 

MARTIN waited with what patience he could for Mirabel's arrival, although reassured by a message Hope had sent the moment she was alone in the office, telling him that Mrs. Newbold's errand was merely a business one. 

His expectation, therefore, was that another and more desirable château had been found, and he wondered how he might best resist the pressure that would be brought to bear on him. 

At the moment, he could as well divert all the money he had in hand as any part of it. He needed every penny he could scrape together. Mirabel must, perforce, be disappointed again.

And, although he tried to resist the thought when it shaped itself in his mind, Mirabel took disappointments so damn' badly!

Consequently it was with a feeling of relief that he heard her say, after greeting him with more warmth than usual: "Mart, I'm going into partnership with you at last. I'm going to help you make money instead of just spending it for you. "Good!" he said heartily, although he had no idea what she meant. "At last I'm going to be a real help to you," she announced it as one proud of her purpose. Over and over again on her way from Paris, she had carefully rehearsed the part she meant to play. Her histrionic ability was to be called on to help her cause. She knew what she was going to say, and how she was going to say it, as well. She was aware that Mart had prejudices which she would be forced to override, and painstakingly in her mind she had marshaled these and sought out arguments to meet them. But before all else, she was counting on her allure, her feminine charm. With it she had always won 221

Immediately on Martin's return from Marseilles, Hope came to him with the notes she had made at Mirabel's dictation. The proposition had seemed to her from the beginning a monstrous one. She felt as if in presenting it to his notice she were conniving at an insult to Martin, yet she had no right to suppress it. She had thought of typing Mrs. Newbold's whole plan in

227 detail, as that lady had elaborated it, and leaving the script on his desk. She decided against this. It would be infinitely easier for her; but, since that was not her custom, it would at once mark the communication as something out of the ordinary and, to save Martin humiliation, she wanted to make the matter part of the daily routine and as casual as possible. 

He looked up at her approach, smiling at her, and she thought how much he had improved physically since they came to France. He had lost the drawn look that had told its tale of confinement and overstrain. They had worked hard together, but, mothering him as a woman always mothers the man she loves, she had usually managed so to arrange matters that he found himself free for his tennis, even if he returned later to go over tabulations or letters which she had completed during his absence.

She had not yet made up her mind what difference Mirabel's discovery of her feeling toward him was going to make. CHAPTER XXIX 

On the evening of the day after he returned from Marseilles,

Martin was surprised at his club by a visit from monsieur de Versac. There had never been any intimacy between the two men, but he was inclined to like the Frenchman, and he greeted him cordially enough. His recent interview with 

Mirabel led him to imagine that he knew the visitor's errand. 

Doubtless De Versac had failed to raise all the money required for his project, and an American to the Parisian was simply another name for an unlimited flow of gold.

He would be obliged to refuse to lend or to join in the undertaking and, believing it sound, he was sorry. "Is there any place where we can talk in privacy?" It was De Versac's first remark after they had exchanged greetings. "If you mean have I a sitting room, I haven't," Martin re- "There's a lounge that isn't very popular at this turned. hour.' 

"We should run no risk of listeners?" asked De Versac.

There was no intention to offend by this innocent remark, yet Martin detected in the duc's eyes a spark of resentment quickly quenched or concealed. "French elevators are slow," he conceded. You are an American and I can proceed openly, in what I understand to be the best and most enlightened American taste." Again he paused and cleared his throat. "I have taken your hand in friendship, I have accepted your hospitality and now that I wish to marry your wife I am come to tell you so straightforwardly, in the friendliest manner, man to man, as honor demands." 

Martin stood, the match he had struck to light his own cigarette burning down to his fingers. He was divided between

FOOL'S GOLD 

233 a desire to laugh and an inclination to kick the man down the whole length of the stair they had so lately ascended amicably side by side. "That's flattering to my taste, of course," he said at last, ironically. "What is it your idea that I can do about it?"

The duc took this au pied de la lettre. "You will understand without lengthy explanations that a man of my rank cannot marry a penniless bride. Madame

Newbold had built up bit by bit a very reliable, substantial, social structure. Here in Paris she is much admired. Were it possible, I would gladly marry her without a sou, but there are estates to be reëstablished, a position to keep up. We must have a suitable entourage. There must be an heir to the name- "And how will Mirabel fancy that idea?" Martin inquired dryly. His interest in these plans was growing, his amusement mastering all other emotion.

De Versac shrugged. He was becoming less cosmopolitan and more French momentarily. "There, I tell you, mon cher, was where you made your little mistake. You Americans let your wives trample on youand you like it. With us it is far otherwise. We tell our wives what to like and they adore us for doing so. Mirabel will be no exception to the rule. As long as she is reasonable she will be a happy woman. And I shall be happy too. Thus, you perceive, the whole matter of our mutual happiness rests in your hands." Having brought his argument to so fortunate a conclusion, he bowed, looking to Martin for encouragement. "And how do you figure that out?" The American asked him curiously. "As I explained," the duc said reproachfully. “My dear 

Newbold, I fear you cannot have been attending. It is the dot, naturellement, which is of the first importance. The question is how much are you prepared to settle on Mirabel in order to see her efforts crowned with a ducal coronet?"

The whole interview was to Martin's mind so ridiculous, so much a bit out of a foreign farce satirizing American manners

FOOL'S GOLD 

and morals, that he could hardly keep his tongue out of his cheek. Then a less amusing thought struck him. "Did Mirabel authorize you to come to me?" he demanded.

De Versac looked at him with melting eyes. He was really a very sentimental man and now he was indulging himself with a mood of sympathy for Martin, the cast-off lover and husband. "You do not mean it, but there you strike at my honor," he said reproachfully. "Do you not stand in the place of a father to her, since Mirabel is an orphan? Assuredly, I must have your consent before I address her. This, you perceive, is not an intrigue. Is it not the mother of my children whose hand 

I seek? With your consent and approval a marriage can be arranged always providing the dot is sufficient." 

A sudden suspicion entered Martin's mind. This man was in need of money, one million dollars, to be exact, to further his ambitious plans in extending his business. What then was the sum he was about to demand to make Mirabel a smiling duchess? "I presume you have some amount in mind which you would consider adequate?" He had assumed his poker face, and the duc thought his cause was won. "For a lady of madame Newbold's luxurious taste, a million dollars would be suitable pocket money," he said suavely. “It could be amply secured to her and her heirs, you understand. 

I shall be liberal there. I shall only require that I be made one of the trustees, with power to direct its investment, subject to the approval of another trustee of your appointing. I am no vulgar fortune hunter, nor spendthrift either, mon ami." "Clever!" thought Martin. "Damn' clever, and so planned as to look magnanimous.' 

"I assume that you would not expect any of this money paid in before the divorce and signing of marriage settlements?" 

This was the most important point in De Versac's proposition, to his mind the most unreasonable and the one where he most feared failure. "I would not wish to seem a Shylock," he rejoined with a

FOOL'S GOLD 

235 strained smile; "but yes, I would require one half the agreed amount now, as evidence of good faith, and also because, as a business man, I have before me an opportunity for investment such as comes but once in a lifetime. It is only by reason of this chance to make a great deal of money that I am able to listen to my heart and make my demands so moderate. You will, assurément, wish to be convinced of the truth of this statement, and I am prepared at the proper time to offer you every satisfaction in that direction. My honor demands that you should be entirely confident of my probity." 

His honor? It flashed into Martin's mind that there were as many varieties of that precious and imponderable form of grace as of a certain celebrated brand of pickles. "My dear duc," he said with a straight face, "how could I fail to appreciate that? Frankly I can think of no other man who would have approached me as you have, openly and without reserve. It only remains then to present your proposition to (he was tempted to say "my honorary daughter," but substituted her name) Mirabel. I think that I am the proper person to do this. 

When she makes her decision I shall at once notify you.'

He never suffered a doubt of what that decision would be, while noting that the duc was equally confident. "I should prefer to do my courting à l'Americaine, yet I bow to your superior judgment-only let us waste no time. 

I shall return to the yacht at once. Suppose I ask Mirabel to run up to Paris to see you?" "Do that." And now Martin did suppress a smile. “I am always delighted to see so charming a lady."

With many protestations of mutual esteem they parted. 

When the man was gone Martin found his hat and went out to walk along the quais. It was too late to go to St. Cloud' to see Hope, the one person he could count on to appreciate the magnitude of the joke, had he been able to tell it to her, which, on consideration, he decided was impossible.

No, rich as it was, he must keep it to himself. He had long before reluctantly recognized that Mirabel was endowed

FOOL'S GOLD 

with no more than a fitful sense of humor and could not be counted on to laugh with him. Anyone else to whom he told the story of his late interview, even Hope, might question his wife's share in bringing De Versac to a state of mind where he felt no uncertainty of her favor.

As he walked alone through the night Martin's amusement lessened, while gradually his anger rose. In spite of his ducal rank, the man was a rotten bounder to have broached such a project to him. Expecting him to be flattered, by Jove, that he wanted his wife at any price! If it had been an American who had come to him with such a proposal he would promptly have knocked him down. And yet would he? Could he have had recourse to the natural outlet for his feelings with a fellow so greatly his inferior in size? If he had struck the Frenchman, De Versac would have sent his seconds and then the farcical situation would have become more farcical still, because De Versac was a noted fencer while Martin had never handled a foil in his life. To avenge his honor, he would then have had to submit to standing up before a skilled swordsman until he was scratched somewhere. Or, if the choice of weapons were his, as on second thought he believed it would be, and he chose pistols, he was a fair shot and might succeed in winging his bird; but what would be the satisfaction in that? 

And what havoc a fatality would play with his life. If he killed De Versac, what would become of Tony? Of his business? Of everything? Of-of Hope? He did not notice that he had not asked himself what would become of Mirabel, but the fact was he had no anxiety about his wife. She was eminently capable of taking care of herself. 

And, by Jove, the papers! What a feast for the tabloids.

No, a duel was not the answer. French duels were silly, futile things, and no American could feel any satisfaction however favorable the outcome.

Martin had walked himself tired and now turned his footsteps toward the club. Before he reached there he had arrived at the answer to his problem. When she heard the whole absurd story, Mirabel would naturally leave the yacht and

FOOL'S GOLD 

237 come home to stay. Once there, she must drop De Versac.

Not violently, in a way to cause remark, but decisively none the less. Women knew how to engineer such affairs so as to rouse no comment. And, so far as he himself was concerned, he might forget it. So long as Mirabel was not involved sentimentally the man had simply made himself ridiculous. 

With a wide yawn Martin mounted the steps of the club and went straightway to bed.

CHAPTER XXXI 

MIRABEL'S sudden appearance at his office was a complete surprise to Martin, who believed her to have rejoined Lord 

Reddin's yacht. She made a dramatic entrance, sweeping across the room to Hope's door, which she closed with emphasis before she turned to her husband. "Maurice said you wished to see me.' 

She was dressed with even more than her usual care and looked very handsome as she stood with the polished wood of the door behind her. There was no other spot in the room that would have set her off to as great advantage. Her husband acknowledged that she had a talent for backgrounds. A calculated defiance in her pose brought a smile to his lips. He had a thorough appreciation of her histrionic leanings. "I always want to see you, my dear, and this is an unexpected pleasure since, after your refusal to return to the city with me, I naturally assumed that you had gone back to the yacht, where I was given to understand that you were indispensable." Although ten minutes earlier he would have ridiculed such a suggestion, and even yet might have denied its possibility, he now sensed her errand and was on his guard. 

Evidently that Frenchman was flattering her into making a fool of herself. "I came back to consult Maurice," she told him sullenly. "I needed advice from someone I could trust." “As he trusts you, I suppose?" In spite of his better judgment, he had allowed her remark to prick him, but he hastened to cover it up. "I hope I am to be admitted to the conference, but, even if it is strictly confidential, you need not have shut that door. Miss Willing has gone for the afternoon." He was amazed to find himself so cool in the face of his wife's announced alliance with De Versac. He decided that he must 246

FOOL'S GOLD 

247 be numbed by the blow, for how could it fail to be a blow that she could propose such a rupture of their relations, even though he did not yet believe her determination irrevocable?

The fact was that he felt himself more than usually alert, and not inclined to say anything that would make her explanation, if she proposed to offer one, any easier for Mirabel. "That is just as well," she rejoined with the suggestion of a sneer. "For all our sakes, this affair must be managed with a minimum of vulgar publicity." "What affair?" Martin asked. "Do sit down, Mirabel. 

The new styles suit you admirably, and, while they don't look it, I presume they permit the relaxation of sitting. This chair is quite comfortable." He placed it for her in a strong light, and she twisted it sidewise before seating herself. "What's the use of fencing?" she demanded harshly, "Maurice told me he had talked things over with you. You know why I'm here." "De Versac was very frank about his own state of mind. 

He also explained to me in detail that every Frenchman expects the American husband to do his duty in the way of settlements, and to like his medicine. I didn't think it worth while to attempt to enlighten him as to the American man's point of view, because, frankly, it had never occurred to me that you were preparing to demand my resignation." He looked at her inquiringly. "I am," came crisply from Mirabel. 

Martin raised his eyebrows and regarded her with a smile. "Really? Have you considered all the pros and cons? De

Versac's going bald before long, and I have it on the best authority that I have very nice hair."

For the life of him he could not take her seriously, and 

Mirabel made an impatient gesture. "I consider that remark in very questionable taste. I didn't come here to joke with you. I came to settle any debatable points." 

Again Martin regarded her quizzically. "My dear, we have never had a serious disagreement in our

FOOL'S GOLD 

married lives. How can I believe that you are really contemplating divorce? I supply you with a luxurious establishment, with clothes the experts pronounce the most distinguished in 

France, and you go your own way without hindrance from me. 

It may interest you to hear that De Versac considers me meek to a fault. In the course of a few years I have assured you that your most ambitious dreams will be possible of fulfillment." "A few years!" Mirabel caught the words from his mouth, endowing them with her own scornful intonation. "How often have I heard that? I am not immortal. How can you expect me to wait and wait when I've put it in your power to gratify my wishes at once? If you will take up this steel combine, we can begin over again. Otherwise not." "So that's the meat in the cocoanut? you think you've found a way to force my hand: to oblige me to go into this deal? 

Well, all I can say is you've mistaken your man. My own method may be slow but it's sure; and it's one more thing. 

It's honorable. Why, if your scheme would net me a hundred millions in twenty-four hours I'd think myself a scoundrel if 

I made use of information my wife 'wheedled' out of a man who is infatuated with her." Martin was genuinely roused at last. "You won't use it?" Mirabel spoke as if she could hardly believe her ears. "You won't use it, when you know all the trouble I took to get it for you?" "I will not," Martin looked her squarely in the eyes. "If you had any sense of honor, you'd know it to be impossible." "Then you'll supply the money for Maurice."

This was not a question, it was a threat; and, as such, it angered Martin, who so far had kept a naturally quick temper under excellent control. "You needn't pretend to make De Versac's mistake,” he told her. "You know as well as I do that alimony in the

United States is not exacted on so extravagant a scale, and also that it is in monthly or yearly payments at the conclusion of the suit and terminating on re-marriage, not in immediate lump sums, except under some special arrangement such as I

FOOL'S GOLD 

249 am in no mood to grant you.' 

Mirabel now took out her vanity case and looked at herself long in the mirror, touching her face here and there with powder and rouging her lips with her lip stick before making any reply. She had seen the adventuress use this piece of business in a crisis in a recent play and had thought it extremely effective as a heightener of suspense. When she shut her bag with a snap it was as if to prepare in a leisurely way for her exit from the stage. "This will have to be a very special arrangement, my dear 

Martin. After all, there is Tony to consider." "T-Tony?" Martin actually stammered. The fact was that in this unreal, fantastic comedy which he felt himself to be playing he had forgotten the boy; and even yet, he did not believe Mirabel to be in earnest. He mentally characterized their whole interview as "one more dodge to get her own way.' 

"Tony? You know darn well you'll be glad to get rid of him."

In the best tradition of outraged feelings Mirabel raised reproachful eyes to his. "My dear little brother," she murmured in tones to melt a heart of stone. "How can you say anything so cruel? I fear, Martin, I gravely fear, you are scheming to obtain control of Tony's fortune. The poor child is unfortunately so unattractive that none save a blood relation could really take a disinterested interest in him." 

Previously Martin's anger had been hot and heedless, the sort of emotion that culminates in the unwelcome statement of disagreeable truths or clears the air by quick and overemphasized utterance. Of a sudden it changed to a cold hate and distrust of Mirabel. A woman who was ready to use her little brother as an article of barter might stoop to anything.

He made no attempt to analyze her feeling for De Versac; it no longer seemed to concern him greatly. All at once he saw that what really mattered was her attitude toward him,

FOOL'S GOLD 

which he recognized as that of calculation unmixed with any gentler emotion. How long was it since she had cared for anything beyond what he could give her in a material way? 

Had she ever been different? Now, at all events, she had abandoned any attempt to conceal her motives from him, proposing without disguise or shame to throw in her lot with whichever man secured these steel companies. 

That was bad enough, but she went even further in her cold calculation. If Martin would not do her bidding and snatch control of the companies on her list, she meant to make him supply the money necessary to clinch the deal for his rival! And to do this she did not scruple to use Tony as make weight to shift the balance her way. 

God! What a fool he had been ever to think he loved her. Why he had never known the woman. He had set up an ideal with her face and worshiped that with a boy's passion soon burnt out, nothing more. Rid of her, he would be a new man.

It was indeed a new Martin who looked at her, a Martin as cold and hard as she was cold and hard herself. "You probably have informed yourself and know much better than I the law and the formalities attending such dissolutions of partnership, yet it occurs to me, as I should think it would to you, that we are wasting our energies over what in this enlightened age I understand to be a very simple affair. 

Our lawyers are the people to speak for us. I shall order my man to arrange for one elegant and refined divorce after the best Parisian fashion. (I understand that Paris has now distinctly the advantage over Reno in the minds of connoisseurs, and fortunately our residences are genuine beyond the possibility of cavil.) As to the money to make De Versac's fortune, I regret that I cannot be accommodating. I like the fellow in a measure. For a man of the world he is so amazingly naïve. Yet I can hardly see my way to crippling my own enterprise to further his. So, sans rancune, I must regretfully decline to meet your views." "And I suppose you have counted the cost of your refusal,

FOOL'S GOLD 

251 

Martin darling?"

Martin winced. Her artificialities, in the circumstances, were disgusting. "Would it not be in better taste if you reserved your endearments for De Versac at this juncture?, He is in the position, I take it, of your unofficial fiancé. I should hate to have him call me out for trifling with the affections of his inamorata." "I think, in your interest in the decoration of my speech, you lost sight of its gist. I repeat: Have you counted the cost of refusal?" "And I think I may safely say I have." Martin stuck his hands in his trousers pockets and tilted back his chair. In the end he expected to pay and to pay high for the possession of Tony. He was, however, quite sure that the price, when all was said and done, would be nothing like the present demand upon him, which was beyond all reason. He was convinced that Mirabel would be very disagreeably disappointed in the amount of alimony she would be able to collect and that fact, combined with resulting shortness of funds, would induce a more conciliatory frame of mind when the custody of the boy was all that she had to sell.

Watching intently, as if bent on divining his thoughts, Mirabel shook her head. "I don't believe you have." She spoke with an assumption of kindly indulgence, the leniency of one who knows she still holds the highest trump. "I honestly don't believe you have.

Has it occurred to you to ask yourself whom I shall name as co-respondent?" And she cast a meaning glance toward the shut door of Hope's room.

Martin was as nearly stunned as a man can be and retain his consciousness. There was no chance of misunderstanding her threat. He said not a word and she went on: "I can make out a most interesting case there, Mr. Newbold. In spite of my opposition, you brought this young woman over on the same boat with me. all normal office hours are over. Indeed your whole business is very mysterious. You have a carefully furnished inner room with couch and pillows complete. Martin squirm. "To be sure I know it is," she admitted brazenly, "but, since there's no one here to hear me say it, that admission will be of no use to you. There'll be plenty ready to believe it. I'll even a lot of old scores in one way or another, you can count on that, and De Versac will 'lie like a gentleman' to back me up." She paused. 

Martin, who was staring at the blank wall with unseeing eyes, never moved a muscle; and, feeling vaguely uncomfortable for all she considered this the hour of her triumph, she hurried on: "You alone can spare us all such unpleasantness, dear Mr. 

Newbold. (I suppose I can say 'dear Mr. Newbold,' since it really means nothing.) You can spare all our feelings by ceasing in good time to be stubborn. You will note I say "In good time." She laughed. She had played the trump she had held in reserve for so long and had played it well. 

Martin leaped to his feet, sending his chair crashing over backward. He was white to the lips but he crossed the room without undue haste and threw open the outer door of the offices. "Go!" he said. Mr. Newbold.”

However she might try to flatter herself, she knew that her exit was not up to her entrance, and even that would have been more effective had Martin only risen to the occasion and accepted the fact that his part was tragic. In many ways, he had always been disappointing.

CHAPTER XXXII 

WHEN Hope reached the office the next morning it was to find Martin there before her. Although she had no suspicion of it, he had been there all night, his mind occupied with the complications of the situation that would be set up if Mirabel carried out her threat and involved the girl in her divorce suit. 

That he must prevent at all cost to himself. But how? If

Mirabel had been actuated by jealousy, had had any belief in the truth of her accusation, he would have sought to prove her mistake to her. Since she acknowledged that she was ruthlessly preparing to use a trumped-up charge to bring him to terms, he was robbed of hope in that direction. Moreover it was obvious that she would take a malicious pleasure in the consternation of his and Hope's relatives, against whom she nourished a grudge for real or fancied slights, a pleasure she would not forgo except for some substantial gain. 

She had, in fact, set her price at a million dollars. Doubtless she would have made it higher had it not been for the immediacy of De Versac's need for that sum to set his project in motion.

She must be aware that Martin had no such amount of liquid capital belonging exclusively to him. There was the money for the Italian combination ready to his hand. If he used that, if he broke faith with the Italians, which, as Hope had pointed out, would mean the ultimate collapse of his whole plan, necessarily founded on trust in his personal probity, he might he almost certainly would-be able to satisfy Mr. 

Shober with the new investment through large returns. His uncle would never be reconciled to what he would consider a failure in honor. 

Yet what other course was open to him?

Feverishly Martin had set down on paper the sources of 254

FOOL'S GOLD 

255 capital on which he might be able to draw and the sums he could count on from each. The result was pitiably inadequate. Almost all he had made above living expenses, which included Mirabel's extravagances, had been turned back into his own project.

Somehow Hope must be saved. She must never learn of this shocking suggestion. All that he had worked to accumulate was not too great a sacrifice if only he could find a way to ensure her safety from slander.

More- He had spent the night going down one blind alley after another, and always coming back to face three facts. First: 

No mercy was to be expected from Mirabel. Second: The effect of such a charge on a girl of Hope Willing's idealism and sense of personal honor would be devastating. over, she would find herself marked for life. Caroline and 

Charlie Page could be counted on to rush to her rescue; but, as Mirabel had been at pains to point out to him, there were all too many in Philadelphia who would be quick to recall that she had lived alone in New York for years, in itself a very questionable proceeding to the Philadelphian mind. "When there could have been no necessity for it, my dear. With all the money in her family, it is absurd to say she had to work for her living. Of course the entanglement began then." In imagination, Martin could hear the clack of the gossips' tongues and the picturesque additions that would be related as evidence when the story, passing from one to another, grew by leaps and bounds into a tale of clandestine love and its gratification. 

The third fact was one to which he had wilfully blinded himself for long.

He loved Hope. In the fire of this devotion, his boyhood passion for Mirabel paled to a sickly flame. Here he had found the woman who should have been his wife. No use telling him the unlike should marry. He knew better now. 

Hope was his mate. They had sprung from the same stock. 

The beliefs that had made him what he was had also nurtured her. Noblesse oblige. Because of those who had gone before, those generations who had held their heads high and

FOOL'S GOLD 

kept their honor spotless, they two had the strength to do, to bear, if necessary to renounce.

Taking another turn, his thoughts returned to Mr. Bohlen, for whom he had a deep affection. Uncle Jim would never forgive him, that was a sure thing. Yet, having entrusted 

Hope to him (Martin did not know how far Mr. Bohlen had been from counting any possible cost to Hope), he would expect him to defend her from any smirch.

Martin's own feelings in the matter did not occupy a large place in his thoughts. He told himself there was no time. for personal grief. He would have years in which to realize how this affected his own life. For the moment the pressing point was the predicament Hope was about to be placed in by the unscrupulous woman he had married.

In the end he reached the conclusion that he had no alternative. Each horn of the dilemma meant trouble for him. 

Far the easier way would have been to go calmly on with his own project, to keep faith with his associates, to let Mirabel carry her threat into execution. Should he pursue that course she would gain nothing by it save the gratification of a vindictive spite and such alimony as the courts would decide equitable. And he would gain his liberty. A boon unspeakable. 

He had never given that possibility a thought until his wife had revealed herself to him in new colors. Now, at each recurrence of the suggestion, something within him flared into life only to sink again. It was impossible. It meant the sacrifice of Hope. She alone would bear the burden. Martin turned from the temptation with disgust. No man could be that despicable.

The only way open to him to save her was to cancel the Italian transaction, the final arrangement of which he had hailed as such a triumph; to fight down his natural instincts that were calling aloud for freedom to woo the woman he loved, for fidelity to his partners, for release from that other woman who a few hours earlier had torn from him the last shreds of his illusion about her. To do Mirabel's bidding would rob her of any excuse for an attack on Hope.

FOOL'S GOLD 

257 The thought of condoning his wife's proposed treachery was abhorrent to him. That she should sit at his table, should pose as a loving mate was monstrous. Yet he knew well that if she forced him to surrender, she would overwhelm him with assurances of her disinterested devotion, would insist, until she convinced even herself of the truth of her statements, that she had embarked on her tortuous course solely because of her devotion to him. Loving him as she did, how could she bear to see him doomed to drag out a miserable existence in a subordinate position? He could fancy how she would throw herself into the part she had invented for herself. He could foretell every argument she would bring forward and, for Hope's protection he would be forced to pretend to believe her. That is, at least until he had found a new secretary. 

Not that he wanted to rid himself of Hope. When she had been in America the loneliness of the offices had been unbearable. But to keep her now would never be safe for her. No telling when Mirabel would again find it convenient to use her as a means to a desired end. For her own sake, he would be forced to let Hope go. Only he must find some way to do it that would ensure her future. 

He looked about him dazedly, picturing the place without her, and a deep flush rose to his brow as he was stung by a new conjecture. No doubt Hope would not wish to stay with him when he had proved false to his pledges. He would have earned a contempt that would render it impossible for her to remain in his employ. And even that he would have to bear without protest, for her sake!

His attitude toward De Versac was his last consideration, and in a way his least concern. He had a repugnance amounting almost to physical nausea from making use of information acquired so questionably by Mirabel; but the man had set out to steal his wife from him and he could not place him in the category of those to whom he owed loyalty. And what a revenge this would be! Martin smiled sourly as he realized how little he cared for this aspect of the case.

Thereafter she did her work in a conflict of emotions. He couldn't do this thing, this despicable thing, for mere money. 

How could he? He was a gentleman, a man of honor. It was unthinkable that he should so violate his code, yet the determination in his voice left her no room for doubt. His mind was made up now and he would have the rest of his life to repent in!

That he would repent she had no doubt. He was of too fine a fiber to view complacently his own fall from grace, no matter how much it benefited him financially. And this was a double treason. He was betraying his partners in America as well as the Italians who had been brought to trust him, and, more shameful still, he was preparing to profit from secrets obtained by unsparing use of his wife's charms. 

He would so quickly learn how little joy money brings! She was thinking of her own fortune, safe in Herman Weismuller's keeping, and doubtless destined to act as a magnet to attract more and more money as the years went by. What use was it to her? She would gladly have given it all to save Martin from this hateful decision. She had no idea what had brought him to it, but later in the day she was to guess who had persuaded him.

A telegram for Martin was sent from his club to the office. 

Hope was authorized to open all office mail and telegrams but, coming by way of the club, this might be different. She hesitated for some time before she slit the envelope. At once it was plain that the message was not meant for her eyes, yet she felt no regret for her mistake since it resolved all her doubts and crystallized her speculations into actions.

At the end of that time she got to her feet and began preparations deliberate and considered. Only once was she interrupted and that was by a telephone call, to which she answered eagerly: "Good, good! I'll be there," and hung up.

First among these preparations was a long letter of instructions to Herman Weismuller, in which she proposed to enclose a copy of her will, all ready in the safe with the exception of a desirable codicil. She then made an appointment over the telephone with her lawyer, and had it and other necessary documents executed at his office within the hour.

She had her dinner there, and then, contrary to her custom, returned to the office in the city, carrying with her a small bag. Two hours more of work there and everything was ready.

It was not yet time to go. She settled herself in the easiest chair and napped a little, nervous lest she should oversleep. 

To amuse herself, she rummaged in her desk drawer and brought out the Spanish books she had been studying in her effort to learn the language.

It seemed to her it was written for Martin, a warning of what must surely happen if she failed in her intervention.

If she loved him purely, as she believed she did, the sacrifice of all she had clung to throughout her life could not be in vain, nor would she count it too great. After all, a woman's honor was as nothing as compared to a man's.

Stripped of his honor, Martin could never, never in the world be happy with her. Even if she did not taunt him with his weakness (and Hope had no faith in Mirabel's generosity), every look at her face, every glance from her eyes would be a reminder of what she had cost him.

The time to go was near. Hurriedly Hope drew toward her a sheet of paper and scrawled: 

I have taken all the money from the safety deposit box. 

Never before could I afford to steal. Now I can't afford not to.

HOPE WILLING. 

This was for Martin's eyes only. She went to the safe, expertly manipulated the combination and threw open the doors.

Looking around for something to prop her note she seized the Spanish books, braced the paper conspicuously within the safe, again locked it, turned off the lights and with a firm step left the office.

As she reached the street the first chill hint of dawn was beginning to lighten the sky arched above the glare of the electric lights.

CHAPTER XXXIII 

ON Martin's return he was mildly surprised to find Hope's room vacant. He expected her back at any moment and it was not until some hours later that he asked Gervaise if he knew where mademoiselle had gone?

Gervaise, mildly surprised in his turn by this question, replied that mademoiselle had not gone anywhere that day. It was simply that she had not come.

Martin's wonder grew. It was unusual, in fact unprecedented, for Hope to absent herself from the office while he was away. He began to believe that she must be ill, and reached for the telephone to call the villa; but before he took up the instrument the thought of madame Voisin occurred to him. It was she who would answer any call, and Mirabel had said she could be counted on to help build the slander that would ruin Hope. He dared not question her, perceiving all too plainly what interpretation the woman would place on such a call from him. 

After a moment's thought he decided that without risk he might let Gervaise arrange for him to take Tony riding in the afternoon. There was nothing unusual in that, and he wanted to see the child.

He rang for Gervaise and gave him instructions to call the governess and make the engagement with the little boy. At least he would learn from Tony just what was the matter, if no news reached him before.

Then he busied himself with his work, drafting a plan of approach to the various steel companies and trying not to feel as if he were composing his own death warrant.

Serious as the situation was, Martin could hardly suppress a grin at this descent from the sublime. De Versac would ever be a puzzle to him, yet on this occasion he could sympathize with the Frenchman's desire to clear his skirts of complicity in such a dirty business.

The article that followed said all the things that could de said about such foolhardy attempts to cross the Atlantic, and 

We are forced to the conclusion that Warren was an unpractical dreamer. His inventions had been rejected by the experts of almost every first-class nation. He still persisted in his faith that he had achieved something destined if not to revolutionize at least to advance aviation by a quarter of a century. In this invention, seemingly, he succeeded in interesting Miss Willing, a novice in the air, for there appears to be no doubt that it was her money that financed the building of the ill-fated aeroplane, Shooting Star.

The name might almost be taken as prophetic, for the men at the hangar report that the start was most auspicious and that the plane vanished in the sky in such short order that its speed must have been tremendous. 

Like a shooting star it flashed across the zenith, and like a shooting star it fell to extinction. "But it can't be Hope!" cried Martin, forgetting his audience in his horror. CHAPTER XXXIV 

It was not on the impulse of a moment that Hope had decided to accompany Bob Warren. Her resolve to save Martin from himself had been instantaneous, while the method to be pursued was slower to dawn on her. She had two facts to work on. The Italian transfers would not be ready for three weeks; while the opportunity for the great steel merger would cease to exist in two, when one of the key companies would be driven by the necessity for cash to accept the offer of assistance from the American Steel Corporation, urged upon them daily by Mr. Rush, thus ending for the time the European menace.

It lay in her power to supply Martin with money to carry out his wife's scheme, but there precisely was Hope's difficulty. 

It was the use of information so obtained that constituted to her mind the gravest breach of a man's code. 

From this, as a logical step forward, came the thought that if Martin were deprived of his accumulated funds for the coming two weeks it would be possible to return the money in time to complete the Italian combination on which she knew his heart to be set; and, even in spite of himself, his honor would be saved.

That in order to do this she must sacrifice herself appeared to her to be inevitable. Not that she feared public disgrace.

She was sure Martin would spare her publicity. It was 

Mirabel who would be less merciful. Her husband would do his best to keep her silent by pointing out that they could not afford to antagonize Uncle Jim, but she was revengeful and, in the end, something would seep out. Philadelphia would be full of little crawling insinuations, and that Hope did not mean ever to face.

There was one course open to her and one only. She must 272

FOOL'S GOLD 

273 disappear, not for the few weeks needful for the success of her plan but for life. 

And the way was clear before her. Did she not own an aeroplane?

It never occurred to her that Bob would object to her flight. To her surprise, when she announced her intention, he put up a very brisk opposition. "It won't do," he said; "it just won't do. Although it's only what I predicted. I always knew you'd go dippy over flying once you got the feel of it. If you insist on going with me the flight's off. It's all very well to risk my worthless neck. I'm not going to be responsible for yours." "You've taken me up already," Hope murmured, stupefied by this unexpected obstacle. "An altogether different proposition," Bob averred. "Such a flight is practically devoid of danger in this plane. No transatlantic flight can be said to be that as yet. There are too many factors to be figured on, of which the addition of your weight is not negligible.' "9 "Suppose I was not thinking of a transatlantic flight? Suppose I had in mind only such a voyage as Mimi suggested.

I have to go somewhere where no one can find me.' Once again a bag was opened before him and Bob Warren peered into its depths to see neat packages of bank notes. FOOL'S GOLD 

had been to credit her shadowy tale of sudden riches, "delusions of grandeur" he supposed that would be called; what a double-distilled ass to lead her on to spend all she had and more on that damn' aeroplane. He very nearly shed tears in his self-blame. In the end, controlling himself, he managed to speak soothingly: "See here, Hope. Don't you worry over this any more. 

I'll give up this flight and we'll sell the plane. I blame myself deeply for letting you see how badly I wanted the money.

I'll take it to the owners and explain. Don't you let it bother you one minute. All you've got to do is to go home and go right straight to bed. What you need's rest, a good long rest. You've been working too hard-too hard for a man, let alone a mere girl—”

In spite of herself, Hope began to giggle, a somewhat hysterical giggle to be sure, excited by Bob's concern and misunderstanding. Yet he was funny too, sending her to bed to restore her lost honor! "Bob!" she spoke imperiously. "Can you be persuaded to be quiet and listen to me for a minute? I'm not cracked, and although what I've done is outrageous from one point of view, in the end no one will suffer except myself. All I'm asking you is to make that suffering as light as possible. I've arranged to have this money, or rather an equivalent amount, returned when it is too late to harm the man I love. Is that clear?”

Then the plane mounted swiftly and, with an effect of startled flight, hurtled through space. "I hope poor Bob at least is leaving his troubles behind him,” was the girl's last conscious thought before she settled back to feel herself definitely separated from the life she had lived.

CHAPTER XXXV 

MARTIN wondered why they did not go. It seemed to him that everything had been said, yet still they lingered, Mirabel sullenly, because she felt herself cheated of complete success at the moment when she believed all she aspired to to be within her grasp, and her nimble brain had not given up searching for some means to bend her husband to her will; De Versac now that his honor had been vindicated and business affairs tentatively arranged, with a desire to show Martin the delicacy of the sympathy he felt for him. The Frenchman was in no doubt of the state of the American's mind. To him it was entirely natural that a man who had indulged his wife until she had assumed and asserted an arrogance that must be intolerable to his masculinity, should then turn from her to a girl who was gentle, feminine and of a physically opposite type. This in no way decreased his admiration for Mirabel.

He felt himself entirely capable of coping with her, and he had no thought of renunciation. As Martin was an American, he greatly overestimated the amount that, in the present circumstance, he would be able to scrape together for his ex-wife, and, although not as a rule inclined to sympathize with the discarded husband, in this case he was influenced by a real liking for the man. He would have considered the arrangement ideal had Hope lived to console him. As it was, it was sad. But too sad!

However the thought of abandoning his plans for Mirabel never darkened his mind. With the training he would give, she would make him an admirable duchess and he had no belief that her company now would assuage Martin's grief. "Perhaps we should go," he murmured to her, and docilely she proceeded to gather together her furs. At that moment

CHAPTER XXXVI 

WEEKS and months went by without further news of the Shooting Star, and the public interest in the fatality flickered and went out.

Martin was not disappointed because from the beginning he had been hopeless, and everything that transpired strengthened his belief that Hope had not intended to return from her amazing adventure.

Before he had canceled his business agreements with the various financial potentates of Italy, he had received a cable from Herman Weismuller asking him to defer action until the arrival of documents then on the way to him. These proved to contain an assignment to him by Hope of bank deposits to cover the full amount she had taken, the same to be paid over to him at a date that permitted the completion of the Italian affair, but too late to allow him to forestall De Versac, had he still been so inclined.

Plainly Hope did not count on a change of heart on his part. · Or, at any rate, she was firm in her determination not to run the risk of his availing himself of Mirabel's stolen information. 

For it was stolen. His wife had learned of the plan in confidence from De Versac, who would have bitten out his tongue before he would have placed the details of his cherished project in the hands of a rival investor.

It was hard now for Martin to realize that he had actually contemplated forestalling De Versac even to protect Hope; yet he had, and he knew he would have gone through with it. 

He had written to his uncle of Mirabel's desire for a divorce and the reply was a prompt cable from that gentleman. Decoded it read:

CONGRATULATIONS STOP CALL ON ME FOR WHAT IS 

286

FOOL'S GOLD 

287 

NECESSARY STOP CHEAP AT ANY PRICE BOHLEN 

Mirabel had received, if not the million first demanded, at least a substantial sum of money in lieu of alimony. So that matter had arranged itself. He understood that De Versac had not consummated his cherished steel merger, although rumors of changes in the steel line-up were in the air. Not knowing of Mr. Rush's intervention, he still hoped the Frenchman would make a success of it. He was grateful to him for the way he had come forward to help protect Hope's name even from Mirabel.

And somehow time passed, although day followed day, lifelessly, joylessly. Business absorbed him. He had not installed anyone else in Hope's place and was doing the work of two. It helped to have constant occupation. For Tony's sake, he had finally consented to live at the villa, after he had supplanted madame Voisin, whose treachery he could not forgive. Her successor was a roly-poly, round-faced, merry, maiden lady of at least fifty. Tony had taken to mademoiselle Carette at once, and under her kindly rule everything ran smoothly. 

The boy was the only one who still persisted in the faith that Hope was alive. Indeed he never questioned it, since the word “dead” had not been mentioned in his hearing. She had "gone away." If he wondered why or how he doubtless thought of a ship or a train. 

"She never said good-by to me!" He was grieved by that, yet it was on it that he chiefly based his certainty of her return. "I know Mirabel sent me to Arcachon, but Hope would understand I couldn't help that. She couldn't go away for good without telling me why she had to go. You wait and see. She'll be back.'

Martin encouraged the boy to talk of her. Now that the girl whom he loved was lost to him he welcomed anything that brought her nearer; and he was to learn many things from Tony, little lovely things such as only a child would cherish. Poetic tales told him during their rambles together,

FOOL'S GOLD 

bits of folk-lore twisted to fit situations they encountered, stories of dead heroes or of King Arthur's Court and the Round Table. Nothing was hackneyed, nothing unimportant, if the boy prefaced it with: "Hope said-"

In some ways, since there was no shade of Mirabel between them, she seemed nearer to him than she had when her place was in the office across from his and, on a shallow pretext, he had induced her to move her desk so that every time he lifted his eyes from his work they could rest on her head-her dear, beautifully shaped head, carried so proudly.

And yet he had not known that he loved her, or was it that he had wilfully shut his eyes to that possibility, content so long as she was near? "And I said, "But, Hopie darling?"" Tony rambled on. (Martin suppressed a start-no one save he should call her "Hopie darling.") "I don't b'lieve I can try to be like King 

Arthur or Sir Galahad, even. You see, I don't feel quite intimate enough with them.' And she laughed-you know the funny way she laughs, Mart, as if she knew an awf❜lly good joke?" (God, did he know it!) "And then she said, 'I'll tell you, Tony, it will do just as well if you try to be like Martin-just exactly like him in every way.' 9 99 Things such as this left Martin without a reply, sadly conscious that save for her intervention the bright shield of his honor would have been stained; although, thank God, it was to save her, not himself. He still might cling to that much consolation. Could she but have known that something less ignoble than she had feared had urged him to that course he would have borne his load of grief without complaint. But

Tony was demanding attention and he must not be allowed to suspect the agony of regret his words had roused. "Do you know where I think she is, Mart? I think she's with that Caroline. Prob❜ly she got some more twins somewhere and Hope had to go to her. Hope always did go to her whenever Carol wanted her. She told me so herself and 

Hope's always reliable, isn't she?" 

For the time he could bear no more.
