289 "Very, old man," he said briefly. "Let's take a couple of lumps of sugar down to the stable, I've an idea they might be appreciated." "And after that it'll be time to feed the rabbits, won't it? 

There's one thing I'll say for them, they don't need any cod liver oil. They always have good appetites!"

It was not hard to divert a child, but after a while Martin would find himself hungry for the sound of Hope's name. 

Again he would lead Tony to speak of her. Again it would be more than he could bear, and he would choke him off somehow, careful always not to let the boy suspect anything amiss, for was he not the only person who could still speak of her cheerfully and naturally?

Tony was faithful in his likes and dislikes, yet it was a surprise to Martin on coming home one afternoon to find him entertaining the duc de Versac with particulars of the family life of m❜sieur et madame Petit Pois, his white rabbits. "I'm a little disappointed in them," he was saying as Martin came into the garden, "just a little wee bit disappointed in them. I don't think they appreciate education-oh, here's 

Martin now!"

The divorce had been accomplished some time before, with a minimum of unpleasantness. The culpable lady in the case (provided by Martin's lawyers and guaranteed to be one who would not later attempt blackmail), while they sat primly waiting for the dawn on the elegant red velvet and golden chairs of a hotel suite, had entertained Martin with feeling recitals of her troubles with her second child's tonsils and adenoids. He had reciprocated with tales of Tony's minor difficulties, and they had parted cordially, in mutual esteem and respect.

Since then he had expected to read any day of the nuptials of 

Mrs. Mercur Newbold and monsieur le duc de Versac. "You are surprised to see me, mon ami?" De Versac came toward him, his hand outstretched, a quizzical expression on his face; "but now at least, there is nothing to bar our friendship. We are, how do you say it? at sea in the same boat,

FOOL'S GOLD 

thrown overboard together." 

Martin had no faintest suspicion of the man's meaning.

Noting his significant look toward Tony, he took the hint and sent the boy to have his pony saddled and go for a ride with the groom. "Now what are you driving at?” he asked his visitor. "Mirabel-it is of our Mirabel, naturally, that I have come to speak to you. Mirabel went to England in the early spring, the time of primroses and hawthorns. I do not claim she had the flowers in mind; she suggested that nothing established a woman's social status more positively than presentation at the court of Great Britain, which, after her divorce, would become impossible for her. I acquiesced. I cared little for that bourgeoise court, but I wanted no gossip concerning our relations here in France. "Enfin, she went. After the divorce the same prudence suggested a further stay in that land of smuts and fog. However, all things end and the time at last came for a discreet return. When I suggested it, this is what I got in reply." 

He flourished a letter addressed in Mirabel's unmistakable and characteristic writing. "Shall I read it aloud?" "Do," said Martin, "if that is your wish. I confess to some curiosity." 

With a speaking glance, De Versac plunged into the effusion: 

"MY EVER-DEAR MAURICE:- 

It has occurred to me more than once, as perhaps it has to you, that it might not be convenable for me to marry any man who was known as an intimate of our house in 

Paris during my unhappy married life. 

Deprived even of my little brother, whom I gave up unwillingly because Martin Newbold had won his love. away from me, I find myself so lonesome (being naturally as you know of a warm and affectionate disposition), that I have decided once more to marry and, when this

FOOL'S GOLD 

291 reaches you, I will already have taken the fatal step with one whom I never met before my divorce; one, my dear

Maurice, who does not share your enlightened views on the subject of wife culture. Moreover, a widower, who, having already supplied the nation with five brawny and brainless sons, now demands nothing more than a friend, a companion and a sharer of his honors and his fortune; this last as well as the family jewels, being all the most exacting could hope for. 

In the name of our long and sincere friendship, dearest Maurice, which I trust will always endure, I would counsel you when you look for a helpmate, as in the past you have once and again considered doing, not to select an American. Something tells me that, unfortunately, docility is not one of our outstanding national traits. 

I have spoken of you so often to my darling Toddles that he is looking forward with the sincerest anticipation of pleasure to making your acquaintance. Needless to say, any time you wish to visit us after the expiration of our honeymoon, you will be le bien venu. 

Always Faithfully, Your Friend,

MIRABEL.

Speechless, the duc looked at Martin. Speechless, Martin looked at the duc. Then simultaneously they burst into a roar of laughter and shook hands vehemently. "I was sorry for you," Martin declared. "I felt you didn't know what you were letting yourself in for." "And, in the past, I for you." De Versac wiped the tears from his eyes. "I suppose, mon ami, every man fancies he would make a better lion-tamer than his neighbor." "Naturally," Martin agreed. the warm but to the cold-blooded animals." "At all events," said De Versac, "my pride is not injured to an extent that will keep me from visiting the lovely lady in England. I can hardly wait until I see that ménage. I prophesy that she will play her rôle excellently well.” 

They sat and talked until Tony returned from his ride, flushed and almost robust looking. "The pony shied. He shied like everything, and I didn't fall off," he announced proudly. "You'd think Bob would have more sense than to wave his hat like that--but then Hope always said he never thought of anything but aeroplanes." "Bob?" said Martin idly. "What Bob?" "Bob Warren." "You can't mean Bob Warren!” "Why not? That's just who it was." "Think again, old man. It certainly wasn't Warren." 

Martin was absolutely incredulous, so incredulous that the suggestion did not ruffle his calm. "Sure thing," Tony insisted. "He's been flying all over 

Africa he told me. He was glad to see me. He waved his hat at me like everything." 

Over the child's head the two men exchanged glances. "The boy's mistaken, of course. De Versac fitted a cigarette in his long holder with care. "Of course," Martin agreed briefly, directing his gaze at the ground at his feet, his face setting in lines of tragedy. "I'm not mistaken, too-I mean either." Tony forgot ceremony in his indignation. "Do you s'pose I don't know my own friends? Why shouldn't I? He doesn't look a mite different."

Martin started up, a wild hope coming to life within him, then he shook his head despondently and leaned back in his chair, staring down at the dust he was stirring with the toe of his shoe.

Bob's ramblings were not so trying to Martin as might be supposed. The aviator's presence was proof that Hope was alive. For the moment he asked no more than that to fill him with glorious happiness. Sooner or later he would find out where she was and then nothing should keep him from her side. She was alive, she was alive, and he needed no one to assure him that she was faithful. He knew instinctively that their love for each other was no light thing. "So here I am," Bob went on, "and my first question is: 

Are you fond of her?" "Fond of her!" Martin exploded. "I adore her."

Bob wrinkled his brows trying to do the heavy father and only succeeding in making himself look like the clown who thinks he has lost the feather he is balancing on his nose. "And your intentions are honorable?" "Don't be more of an ass than you can help," thus Martin with base ingratitude, coming suddenly to life. "If she'll only have me, I want to marry her.
